# Small but Sabi: How Far Can Reinforcement Learning Take a 270M Translator into 77 African Languages?

**Jeffrey Otoibhi**  
Aletheia AI  
*Preprint draft of 2026-10-26 (revised after the contamination audit of 2026-10-26: Section 6.9a is new and the statements about the exposure of the evaluation sets, the cross-paper `clean` comparisons and the final run's independence were corrected; the statements about the extra reward hardening were revised on 2026-10-25, Section 7.9). "Sabi" is Nigerian Pidgin for "to know (how to)". The controlled ablation of Section 8.4 has finished and is reported (one run per arm, four languages). Open questions to the authors are marked* TODO(owner: ...) *and collected in `paper/TODO.md`.*

<div class="abstract" markdown="1">

**Abstract.** A 270M-parameter model can write 77 African languages, but fine-tuning alone leaves it with sentences it never finishes, source text it copies and languages it barely writes. We ask how far reinforcement learning (GRPO) with quality-estimation rewards can close that gap without any new parallel data. On FLORES+, RL lifts macro chrF++ from 28.7 to 34.1 (+5.4, 95% CI [5.2, 5.6]; all 45 languages improve), most where the fine-tuned model was weakest (Kabuverdianu +14.2, Kabiye +14.1, Kamba +12.1); part of that gain is repaired unfinished or looping output. **The test sets were not unseen by our pipeline** (Section 6.9a): the fine-tuning text held the English side of 95% of the FLORES+ devtest sentences and, for seven languages, the reference translation itself; the eight benchmarks rest on five English texts; and the reward's calibration was fitted on test splits (the Efik thresholds on IbomMT; the final run's anchors on six test sets), so our levels are inflated (on FLORES+ by 3.8 chrF++ for SFT and 2.6 for RL) and some results are not independent tests. The gain does not depend on this: on the rows whose English was never in our training text RL beats SFT on all eight benchmarks (FLORES+ +6.6 [5.8, 7.2], NTREX-128 +4.1, MAFAND-MT +3.7, SmolSent +7.1, SALT +2.3). A fresh evaluation is planned and has not been run. A pre-registered ablation (four languages, one run per arm) shows that the gain survives a much weaker fine-tuning start (+7.4 against +5.7 chrF++) and that RL beats continued fine-tuning of equal compute (+7.0); it does not show that RL replaces fine-tuning. On identical sentences (also on the never-seen rows: 3.16 over 44 languages) the model stays 3.32 chrF++ behind NLLB-200 3.3B on the 42 languages without the two Ge'ez-script languages (median 2.78; 3.85 and 3.17 on all 44, because a few languages carry the gap: Kabuverdianu, Amharic and Tigrinya alone 30%); Amharic and Tigrinya, left out of that headline because they are an outlier with an untested cause, are far behind (19.0 and 13.8 against 38.0 and 24.7), and Kabuverdianu, our largest gain over SFT, is also the largest remaining gap (21.0); we do not beat NLLB. Quantised to 8 bits it runs on four CPU cores with no measurable quality loss (about two sentences per second on a server CPU). We evaluate against human references, metrics that correlate with human judgement (AfriCOMET, SSA-COMET) and an independent LLM judge (Spearman about 0.5 with human ratings); there is no human evaluation, and we say where our reward and our metrics overlap.

</div>

## 1 Introduction

Most African languages are served poorly by machine translation. Large systems cover a few dozen of them, and the community benchmarks that measure the rest are small. At the same time there is demand for models that run on a phone or a laptop without a network. We ask how far a *very small* model, Gemma 3 with 270 million parameters, can be pushed on English-to-African translation, and where the limits lie, when the training signal after supervised fine-tuning (SFT) comes from reinforcement learning (RL) with learned quality metrics instead of more parallel data.

The setting is demanding in three ways. First, the languages: 77 targets, from Swahili and Zulu (tens of millions of speakers, decent data) to Esan, Edo, Ghomala and Okpe, for which the only parallel text we could find is largely religious. For 23 of them no learned translation-quality metric exists at all. Second, the model: 270M parameters, of which 168M are the 262k-entry embedding table, so the transformer proper is tiny. Third, the length: we wanted the model to translate whole documents of up to 2,048 source tokens, which in a language with fragmented tokenisation means an output of 4,000 tokens or more.

RL with a quality-estimation (QE) reward is an obvious tool, but a reward that is a learned model is a target to be gamed, and the literature and our own corruption tests list the cheap exploits: copying the English source, appending text, repeating, answering in the wrong language, stopping early, or writing the same stock sentence for every prompt. Most of this paper is therefore about what surrounds the reward and about the measurements that motivated each safeguard. Whether the policy would have found these exploits without the safeguards our runs cannot say, because every run had the hard failure classes, and for the extra terms of the final run (anchors, ensemble, long-document reward) a stored-output check finds no demonstrated protective effect, only a design and some lower loop rates without a control run (Section 7.9). The paper tells the story in order, including the parts that did not work: a training phase that diverged, a detector with a length bug, a teacher model that could not teach, a science fine-tuning stage that erased what RL had gained until it was interpolated away, and a long-document goal that was met for RL-final only by changing the decoding strategy, and natively, with omissions, only after the science stage.

**Contributions.** To our knowledge, this is the first open 270M-parameter model fine-tuned with RL against an African-language quality estimator (a QE-ensemble reward) on prompts of up to 2,048 source tokens; the single-pass document result is narrower than that training range (Sections 6.4 and 6.10), and the statement concerns the training prompts, not the quality of document translation relative to other systems. Our literature search (about 28 queries on 2026-10-22 and 2026-10-23, Section 2) found no earlier work with this combination; Section 2 lists the closest prior work and what we do not claim. On breadth we claim only that, to our knowledge, it is the open translation model with the widest African target-language coverage below 1B parameters (77 languages, against 55 for NLLB-200-600M), and that it covers 33 African languages absent from FLORES-200 (17 of which we could not find in NLLB-200, MADLAD-400 or TranslateGemma); we do not claim to be the first or the only system with this breadth (Section 6.8).

1. *A reward design and its evidence* (Sections 4.3, 4.4, 7). A pessimistic QE ensemble (mean of two judges, minimum when they disagree by more than one reward unit), per-language shaping on human anchors, language-aware sentence alignment in which omissions are failed chunks, a consensus signal for languages without a metric, hard failure classes, constraint detectors calibrated on human text with measured false-positive rates, and a second reward for long documents. For each component we report the measurement that motivated it, including negative ones (a SSA-COMET copy exploit on corrupted references, a detector flagging 100% of long human documents, a consensus attractor in simulation), and in Section 7.9 what the extra terms of the final run did and did not do on stored outputs: no demonstrated protection against copying, entity loss or stop-word profile (RL-final did not show these failures), padding and repetition 2-4 pp lower than RL-final in the weak and consensus-only languages, and no control run that would let us attribute either result to the terms.
2. *Gains on 77 languages with a 270M model* (Sections 6.1-6.3). On FLORES+ the macro chrF++ rises by 5.4 (28.7 to 34.1, 95% interval [5.2, 5.6]; 45 of 45 languages improve), and by 6.6 [5.8, 7.2] on the 48 sentences per language whose English side was never in our training text; on the never-seen rows RL also gains over SFT on NTREX-128 (+4.1), MAFAND-MT (+3.7), SmolSent (+7.1), GlotStoryBook (+8.3) and SALT (+2.3), each with an interval above zero, while the levels of both models are inflated by the exposure of the test sets to our training and reward calibration (Section 6.9a). Gains are largest where the SFT model was weakest, including languages whose reward contained no learned metric (Kabuverdianu +14.2, Kabiye +14.1, Kamba +12.1, Luo +11.9, Nuer +11.4, Kanuri +11.0 chrF++ on FLORES+; mean +5.35, median +4.38 over 44 languages; part of the largest gains is repaired unfinished output and the controlled ablation of Section 8.4 shows a smaller gain on finished sentences). A sentence-by-sentence comparison with the SFT model on 161k sentences (Section 6.9) finds RL preferred in 63.7% (chrF++) and about 70% (reference-based AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET) of the sentences on which they differ, in 65 of 66 languages and every benchmark and group, with a check against reward hacking; it is evidence about agreement with human references under automatic metrics, not a human evaluation. An independent LLM judge, validated at Spearman about 0.5 against human ratings of other systems' output, also prefers RL-final on 1,570 FLORES+ sentences in eight languages (+4.0 of 100, 95% interval [3.2, 4.9]; higher in seven languages, level in Xhosa; Section 6.12). On identical sentences the model remains 3.32 chrF++ and 0.043 AfriCOMET behind NLLB-200 3.3B on the 42 languages without the two Ge'ez-script languages (our headline comparison; 3.85 and 0.050 on all languages) and 1.13 and 0.028 behind NLLB-200-distilled-600M (1.69 and 0.035 on all), while Amharic and Tigrinya, left out of the headline because they are an outlier with an untested cause, are 19.0 and 13.8 chrF++ against 38.0 and 24.7 for the 3.3B model (Section 6.8). The mean gap is skewed by a few languages (median 2.78 over the 42, 3.17 over all 44; Kabuverdianu, Amharic and Tigrinya carry 30% of the all-language gap), and Kabuverdianu, which stays in every set, is both the largest gain over SFT (+14.2) and the largest remaining gap to NLLB-200 3.3B (21.0). We make no claim of superiority or inferiority over the published 4-14B Afrique LLMs: their numbers are for all 1,012 sentences and their per-sentence outputs are not available (Section 6.2).
3. *An honest account of length* (Sections 4.2, 6.4, 6.10, 7.4). A curriculum of five length bins up to 2,048-token inputs with a chunked reward extended the single-pass translation range from about 256 to about 512 source tokens, but greedy whole-document translation of 1,024- and 2,048-token inputs still loops or truncates (finished 65% and 42%); translating sentence by sentence with the same model (a wrapper that we use to compare with systems that cannot take long input; our models generate the whole document natively), and repairing the 1% of unfinished sentences, gives 55.6-60.1 chrF++ against an NLLB reference for 1,024-4,096-token documents and about 52 chrF++ against human references at 2,048 tokens. We diagnose why the reward could not teach single-pass long translation and give a second reward that fixes its detectors. A benchmark against other systems (Section 6.10) tests the hypothesis that our model translates 2,048-token documents far better than others and finds it only partly true: after the supervised science stage (not the RL checkpoint) the model finishes and writes the target language on 1,000-2,000-token documents in a single pass (chrF++ 39.4-39.6 and 28.2-28.8 against human references, against 5.9-14.9 for NLLB-200 and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM in whole-document mode), but it omits 13-30% of the sentences, and a sentence-by-sentence wrapper gives every system higher scores, with NLLB and TranslatePsy 1-4 chrF++ ahead of ours: better on native long input is not better document translation. An independent LLM judge on aligned units agrees and is sharper (Section 6.12): the final model's whole-document units score 25.8 of 100 against 67.0 for its sentence-by-sentence wrapper and 85.6 for NLLB-200 3.3B (five languages; 16.4% of the sampled sentences get no output).
4. *Where the gain comes from, why it stops, and a research question* (Sections 6.3, 8, 9, 10). The sentence-level gain was complete after roughly a million sampled completions; the next 0.9 million changed nothing at sentence level. We present four hypotheses for the plateau (capacity, reward alignment, data, measurement) with the evidence for each. We also pose the research question whether RL is a *data-efficient complement to SFT*, which our runs inform but do not answer: our runs show gains from RL (+5.4 chrF++ macro; about +4 for the Ge'ez-script languages Amharic and Tigrinya) against at most +0.6 from the parallel data we could add, with per-unit-of-data numbers, eight listed confounds and and a small controlled ablation (RL from a re-created weak SFT with 12.7% of the SFT tokens, four languages, 49 steps, one seed per arm, Section 8.4). In the ablation, RL from the weak start gained +7.36 chrF++ against +5.72 from SFT-final and beat two continued-SFT controls (+0.32 and -1.01), but it did not reach SFT-final (-6.25), and on sentences that every system finished the chrF++ gain from the weak start is the smaller one (+1.13 against +2.23), so we do not say that RL replaces SFT.
5. *Case studies* (Section 9). The Ge'ez-script languages, where the tokenizer, the SFT data and RL interact; the 23 consensus-only languages, where no learned metric exists and a 31B teacher did not help; and Urhobo, where an LLM-generated silver set serves as a detector check but not as evidence of quality.
6. *A like-for-like comparison with other systems* (Section 6.8). AfriCOMET is published for almost none of the baselines, so we re-scored the systems we could run (NLLB-200 600M and 3.3B, TranslatePsy-AfriSLM, our checkpoints) on identical sentences with one metric stack; we do not beat NLLB (headline: 3.32 chrF++ behind the 3.3B model over the 42 languages without Amharic and Tigrinya, 3.85 over all 44, the two Ge'ez-script languages reported next to it in the same table; medians 2.78 and 3.17, because a few languages carry the gap), and we state precisely which coverage claims we can and cannot make. A reward widening for a next round is calibrated but not used (Section 7.8).
7. *Tokenisation cost and CPU deployment* (Sections 3.3, 6.11, 10). The Gemma tokenizer needs 1.5-1.7 times as many output tokens for Yoruba and Tigrinya as for Swahili, which makes a sentence take about 1.6 times as long on a CPU, and the languages that cost most are those with the lowest chrF++; quantised to GGUF Q8\_0 the model loses nothing measurable against the GPU output (-0.02 chrF++, [-0.22, +0.16]) and translates about two sentences per second on four cores of a shared server (laptop speed: an estimate). NLLB-200 is shown as a reference only.
8. *Negative results and practical lessons* (Sections 4.5, 6.6, 7). A divergence of GRPO without standardised advantages, with the guards that fixed it; a padding detector whose false-positive rate depended on text length; a plain science fine-tuning stage that lowered the languages RL had improved, repaired by weight interpolation and a guardrail rule; Gemma-4-31B as a data teacher failing for 21 of 23 weak languages; the coverage limits of NLLB; and the limits of every benchmark we used.
9. *An audit of how the evaluation sets entered our pipeline* (Section 6.9a). A contamination audit (2026-10-26) traced the test sets through the SFT text, the RL prompts, the reward calibration and the selection of checkpoints. It found that the eight benchmarks rest on five English texts; that the SFT text held the English side of 95.3% of the FLORES+ devtest sentences and the reference translation itself for seven languages; that the Efik reward thresholds of RL-final were fitted on 997 of the 1,012 IbomMT test sentences and the final run's anchors on six test splits; that selection used development sets only; and that a cross-paper comparison on `clean` rows in earlier drafts was not like for like (removed). The conclusions that survive are stated in the section; the stage-by-stage account and the tables of exposure are generated from the audit's files.

**Outline.** Section 3 describes languages, data and the supervised model; Section 4 the RL method and reward; Section 5 the setup; Section 6 the results (including the final run, a comparison with other systems on identical sentences, a pairwise comparison of SFT and RL and the audit of the exposure of the evaluation sets, Section 6.9a); Section 7 the reward analysis; Section 8 the research question on RL and SFT data, with the controlled ablation; Section 9 three case studies; Section 10 the discussion of the plateau and practical deployment; Sections 11-13 limitations, ethics and reproducibility; Section 14 concludes.

## 2 Related work

**Machine translation for African languages.** The field's data and evaluation infrastructure comes from community efforts: the participatory Masakhane work [@nekoto2020participatory], MAFAND-MT, a news-domain corpus for 16 languages showing that a few thousand high-quality pairs go a long way when fine-tuning large pretrained models [@adelani2022mafand], AfroMT [@reid2021afromt], FLORES-200 and the NLLB-200 system covering 200 languages [@goyal2022flores101; @nllb2022nllb; @nllb2024nature], its community-maintained successor FLORES+ [@oldi2025floresplus; @dale2025oldi], MADLAD-400 [@kudugunta2023madlad], SALT for Ugandan languages [@akera2022salt], SMOL and GATITOS for very low-resource languages [@caswell2025smol], and the Bible and Jehovah's Witnesses corpora [@agic2019jw300; @akerman2023ebible] on which much low-resource training data still rests (an issue we measure in Section 3.4). Quality estimation for African languages is led by AfriCOMET [@wang2024africomet] and SSA-COMET, with human annotations for 14 pairs [@li2025ssacomet], the latter reported by the recent African-MT papers while AfriCOMET is published for almost none of the baseline systems (Section 6.8); AfroBench evaluates LLMs across 64 languages and finds a large gap to English [@ojo2025afrobench]. Recent LLM-based work trains much larger models than ours: AfriqueLLM adapts 4-14B Llama, Gemma and Qwen models with 16K context [@yu2026afriquellm], Sunflower post-trains Qwen3 14B/32B for 31 Ugandan languages [@akera2025sunflower], and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM is the nearest small-model competitor, 0.8B-4B models for 19 African languages trained by supervised fine-tuning with QE filtering and synthetic data [@gritta2026translatepsy]; smaller African-oriented models include InkubaLM (0.4B) [@tonja2024inkubalm] and a pruned NLLB-600M [@moslem2026afrinllb]. NLLB-200's own model card states that it was trained on inputs of at most 512 tokens and that it is not intended for document translation [@nllb200_card].

**Document-level and long-output translation.** LLMs can translate documents [@wang2023document]; open and specialised models are prone to off-target output [@wu2024adapting], translation quality of sequence models degrades on lengths unseen in training [@varis2021sequence], and long-context models use information in the middle of the input less reliably [@liu2024lost]; under-generation and repetition of long outputs are what we observe ourselves (Section 6.4). For African languages the dedicated resource is AfriDoc-MT, five languages and about 650 English words per document, where sentence-level models generalise poorly to long inputs and sentence-by-sentence translation beats pseudo-document translation for most models [@alabi2025afridocmt]; AfriScience-MT reports a large sentence-to-document quality drop on six languages [@abdulmumin2026afriscience]; AfriqueLLM reports document translation on AfriDoc-MT, with a smallest model of 4B [@yu2026afriquellm]. Sentence-level alignment of long outputs goes back to Gale and Church [@gale1993program] and Vecalign [@thompson2019vecalign]; repetition and degeneration in long generation are classic phenomena [@holtzman2020curious; @welleck2020unlikelihood] that reappear as the dominant failure of our whole-document decoding. FormatRL applies GRPO to document-level translation of structured XML documents [@song2025formatrl] and LongWriter-Zero uses RL for ultra-long writing [@wu2025longwriterzero]; we found no RL study of end-to-end translation of long documents with a small model.

**Reinforcement learning for translation.** GRPO [@shao2024deepseekmath] and its analyses [@guo2025deepseekr1; @liu2025understanding; @yu2025dapo] (group-relative advantages, length and standard-deviation normalisation, KL estimators [@schulman2020approximating]) underlie R1-style translation work: MT-R1-Zero (3B and 7B models) [@feng2025mtr1zero], R1-T1 [@he2025r1t1], DeepTrans and ExTrans (7B backbones) [@wang2025deeptrans; @wang2025extrans], Seed-X (7B) [@cheng2025seedx], Hunyuan-MT and HY-MT1.5 [@zheng2025hunyuanmt; @zheng2025hymt15], and TranslateGemma, which applies RL with a reward-model ensemble (MetricX-QE, an AutoMQM-style QE model, chrF and others) to Gemma 3 at 4B, 12B and 27B [@finkelstein2026translategemma]. Closest to our setting are RL fine-tuning of NLLB-600M/1.3B with a round-trip chrF++ reward, covering Wolof and Dyula among other languages [@attia2026roundtrip], reference-free GRPO on NLLB with LaBSE and COMET-Kiwi over 13 languages [@garciaestrada2026reffree], and RL with a chrF reward for unseen-language translation [@hu2026rlunseen]. Earlier work optimised metrics directly [@shen2016mrt] or used them for preference optimisation [@xu2024cpo] and decoding [@freitag2022mbr; @fernandes2022qualityaware; @eikema2020map]. Label-free RL signals such as agreement among samples [@zuo2025ttrl; @wang2023selfconsistency] are the analogue of our consensus reward. Length curricula for RL exist for reasoning [@song2025fastcurl; @hammoud2025trainlong]; we found none for translation.

**Metrics, reward hacking and quality estimation.** COMET-family and MetricX metrics [@rei2020comet; @rei2022cometkiwi; @guerreiro2024xcomet; @juraska2024metricx24] are strong on high-resource languages and less reliable for low-resource ones; the size of a metric difference that matters is metric-specific and thresholds may not transfer across languages [@kocmi2024metricsmaze], chrF/chrF++ and spBLEU remain the surface reference [@popovic2017chrfpp; @post2018call], and bootstrap resampling is the usual significance test [@koehn2004significance]. Optimising a learned reward overoptimises it [@gao2023scaling; @karwowski2023goodhart; @krakovna2020specification], ensembles mitigate this [@coste2024ensembles], and length is a classic exploit [@singhal2024long]. For translation specifically, WALAR documents self-generated references, non-translations, over- and under-translation and wrong-language output as hacks of a QE reward and adds word- and language-alignment rewards [@liu2026walar]; MiLMMT-style work gates a two-QE reward by language identification [@han2026milmmtrl]; language identification for low-resource languages is the job of GlotLID [@kargaran2023glotlid]. We extend this line to African languages, a 270M model and long documents, and add measurements of detector false-positive rates against human text.

**Small models, multilinguality, forgetting.** Gemma 3 and its 270M variant [@gemmateam2025gemma3; @google2025gemma270m] are positioned for task-specific fine-tuning; small multilingual models face the curse of multilinguality [@conneau2020unsupervised; @chang2024multilinguality], unfair tokenisation of low-resource scripts [@petrov2023tokenizer] and language-sampling choices [@chung2023unimax]. Weight-space interpolation between a fine-tuned and a base model recovers robustness [@wortsman2022robust], and averaging or combining fine-tuned weights is a general tool [@wortsman2022soups; @ilharco2023editing]; replay mitigates forgetting [@rolnick2019experience], LoRA learns and forgets less than full fine-tuning [@biderman2024lora], and RL forgets less than SFT and changes small subnetworks [@shenfeld2025razor; @mukherjee2025subnetworks]. Whether RL generalises where SFT memorises [@chu2025sft] or only sharpens what the base model already contains [@yue2025doesrl] is open for reasoning, and we know of no controlled comparison for translation; reference-free RL has been proposed as a route where parallel data are scarce [@liu2026walar], and Section 8 contributes one observational data point (RL against added parallel data, for two African languages) and a small controlled ablation (four languages, one seed per arm).

**Positioning and the novelty claim.** To our knowledge no earlier work applies RL with a quality-estimation reward ensemble to a decoder-only model of about 270M parameters for English-to-African translation over 77 targets, or trains such a model with an output-length curriculum up to 2,048-token inputs; in the wording we are prepared to defend, this is the first *open* 270M-parameter model fine-tuned with RL against an African-language quality estimator on prompts of up to 2,048 tokens. Neighbouring work limits what we can claim: RL for MT with small models exists (NLLB-600M/1.3B), RL for MT with a QE-ensemble reward exists (TranslateGemma, 4B and up), reward-hacking defences for MT-RL have been studied (WALAR), and small African MT models (TranslatePsy-AfriSLM) and African document-level benchmarks (AfriDoc-MT) exist. We therefore claim neither first RL for low-resource MT nor first small model nor first reward-hacking analysis. *On language coverage we make no claim of priority.* NLLB-200 supports 55 African languages [@nllb2022nllb]; MADLAD-400 lists about 85 African entries [@kudugunta2023madlad] and TranslateGemma about 60 [@finkelstein2026translategemma] (both our manual tallies of the papers' tables, unverified); Omnilingual MT (Meta, 2026) reports a far wider language list that we did not verify. What we claim is narrower and conditional (Section 6.8): to our knowledge the open model with the widest African target-language coverage *below 1B parameters* (77 languages against 55 for NLLB-200-600M), which says nothing about quality, where our model is below NLLB-200-600M; and 33 languages absent from FLORES-200, 17 of which we could not find in NLLB-200, MADLAD-400 or the TranslateGemma language lists. In addition, our own long-document result (Section 6.4) is narrower than the goal that motivated it: the RL-trained model translates 2,048- and 4,096-token documents only with sentence-level chunked decoding and its single-pass translation works to about 512 tokens; the science-stage model finishes whole documents of up to about 2,048 tokens but omits 13-30% of the sentences, and NLLB-200 and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM with a sentence-by-sentence wrapper are 1-4 chrF++ ahead of it (Section 6.10). The literature search behind these statements (about 28 searches, 2026-10-22 and 2026-10-23, plus a coverage survey of 35 systems, Appendix G) is recorded in `paper/lit/notes_*.md` and `reports/data/language_coverage_claim_check.md`; it should be repeated on Google Scholar, the ACL Anthology (WMT and EMNLP 2026) and Hugging Face papers before submission.

## 3 Languages, data and the supervised model

### 3.1 Task, model and output format

The task is English-to-African-language translation with one fixed prompt, `Translate the text to {language}:  {english}` (two spaces after the colon), and one fixed answer format, `{iso} {translation}<end_of_turn>`: the model first writes the three-letter tag of the target language and then the translation. The tag is part of the loss in SFT and is *required* by the RL reward (a missing or wrong tag is a hard failure), so that the model cannot silently answer in another language. Only English sources are used. The model is `google/gemma-3-270m-it` (18 layers, hidden size 640, 4 attention heads with one key-value head, sliding window 512, 262,144-token vocabulary; the embedding table alone holds about 168 M of the roughly 270 M parameters by our count, about 170 M according to the model announcement) [@gemmateam2025gemma3; @google2025gemma270m]. We train the full model; no adapters.

### 3.2 Languages and groups

The language table has 85 rows, of which 77 are the *target languages of SFT and of the RL phases* and 8 are table entries that were not RL-trained (`nde`, `fat`, `knc`, `gaa`, `men`, `luy`, `tzm`, `ful`); according to `gemma_mt/languages.py` and D-38 none of these 8 has SFT training data, so they are zero-shot (one report lists four of them as having some data; TODO(owner: confirm)), and only Kanuri and North Ndebele are scored on a benchmark. The 77 RL languages are split by the *content signal* that is available to the reward ({{tref:t_groups_def}}): 42 languages whose AfriCOMET-QE score was validated against human references (AUC for human versus mismatched references of at least 0.70), 12 languages named on the AfriCOMET card or varieties of a validated language but without reference-based validation (QE is blended 50/50 with a consensus signal), and 23 languages that AfriCOMET does not cover, for which the reward has no learned metric at all (consensus signal plus constraint penalties). Of the 85 rows, 66 have at least one human-reference benchmark in our evaluation (45 on FLORES+); 19 have none, 13 of them RL languages ({{tref:t_nobench}}, {{fref:fig8_coverage}}). For these 13 languages quality was judged only by the reward models.

{{table:t_groups_def|Language groups. The group determines which content signal enters the reward (Section 4.3). Counts from `reports/final_model_per_language_2026-10-21.md`.}}

{{figure:fig8_coverage|(a) The 85 language-table rows by family and by reward group. (b) Human-reference benchmarks and the number of languages each covers. 19 languages have no human-reference benchmark ({{tref:t_nobench}}).|95}}

### 3.3 Tokenizer

We keep the Gemma 3 tokenizer unchanged. The relevant measured facts are modest: the number of Gemma tokens of the human reference per Gemma token of the English source ranges from 1.12 to 3.19 (median 1.88) over the 54 targets with human documents (`reports/longdoc_benchmark_plan_2026-10-20_parts/data/fertility.json`, `DECISIONS.md` D-44), so a 2,048-token English document translates into an output of roughly 4,000 tokens or more in the more fragmented languages; the tokenizer has no normaliser, and decomposed (NFD) text of a diacritic-rich language costs up to 1.29 times as many tokens as NFC text, so all training text, rewards and metrics are Unicode-NFC (`DECISIONS.md` D-29; memory notes on the NFC audit). Gemma token counts are 0.88-0.90 times the AfroXLM-R token counts for the English sources and 0.97-1.43 times for the human references (54 languages; same file), and the AfroXLM-R counts define our length bins.

**Tokenisation cost by language (a measured finding).** The same sentence costs a very different number of tokens in different languages, and a decoder pays for every output token. On the 1,012 FLORES+ devtest sentences the Gemma tokenizer gives 3.11 characters per token for Swahili, 2.99 for Hausa, 2.74 for Kinyarwanda, 1.94 for Yoruba, 1.66 for Amharic and 1.35 for Tigrinya (English: 4.87; `reports/data/weak_language_data_2026-10-22/tokenizer_flores_devtest.csv`, {{tref:t_tokcost_lang}}, {{fref:fig17_token_cost}}). On the 170-200 sentences per language of the CPU speed set (Section 6.11) the order is the same (2.92, 2.88, 2.65, 1.87, 1.61 and 1.31 characters per reference token, derived from `tokens_per_sentence.csv`). The English source is equally long in all six runs (26.6-28.5 Gemma tokens), so the difference is entirely on the output side: the reference translation into Yoruba or Tigrinya has 1.50-1.51 times as many tokens as the Swahili one, and our model writes 76.3 and 74.3 tokens per sentence against 44.5 for Swahili (1.71 and 1.67 times). Characters per token is not the whole story: Amharic has the second-lowest characters per token but Amharic sentences are short (88 reference characters against 136 for Swahili), so it costs only 1.24 times the Swahili output tokens. On four CPU cores with the Q8\_0 build the consequence is direct: Yoruba and Tigrinya run at 1.58 and 1.55 sentences/s against 2.46 for Swahili at batch 32, that is 1.56 and 1.59 times as long per sentence (about 176-179 hours per million sentences against 113 for Swahili and 111 for Hausa; best of two repetitions; one Kinyarwanda repetition ran at 0.42 sentences/s against 2.11 for the other, probably because of another job on the shared machine, which is why the best repetition is used). The "about 1.6 times" in a casual statement of this finding is therefore the time per sentence; the output-token ratio is 1.7 for the generated text and 1.5 for the references. Time and memory of a deployment thus depend on the target language, and a throughput figure without the language (such as the 139 hours per million sentences of Section 6.11, a mixture of six languages) hides a factor of about 1.6.

{{table:t_tokcost_lang|Tokenisation cost by language, six languages of the CPU speed set. Characters per token and tokens vs English: Gemma tokenizer of the final checkpoint on 1,012 FLORES+ devtest sentences (`tokenizer_flores_devtest.csv`). Reference and generated tokens per sentence and the Q8\_0 speed: `reports/data/cpu_speed_2026-10-23/tokens_per_sentence.csv` and `per_language_speed.csv` (170-200 sentences per language, 4 threads, batch 32, best of two repetitions). chrF++ of the final-run checkpoint on exactly these sentences: `quality.csv` (GPU, greedy); RL-final on the full FLORES+ devtest (all rows): `reports/data/final_model_per_language.csv`. Source: `paper/scripts/make_cpu.py`.}}

{{figure:fig17_token_cost|(a) Characters per Gemma token (FLORES+ devtest) against chrF++ of RL-final on FLORES+ for the 16 languages for which both exist (six speed-set languages in blue). (b) Tokens generated per sentence (left) and Q8\_0 sentences per second (right) for the six languages of the speed set. Source: `paper/scripts/make_cpu.py`.|100}}

The languages that cost most per sentence are also those the model translates worst: in the six languages the order by characters per token and by chrF++ of the final-run checkpoint is identical (58.0, 45.7, 43.5, 29.4, 19.3, 13.8 from Swahili to Tigrinya), and over the 16 languages with both numbers the Spearman correlation of characters per token with the chrF++ of RL-final is 0.91 ({{fref:fig17_token_cost}}a). We read this as an association, not a mechanism: fragmentation of a language by the vocabulary goes with little pretraining text, little SFT text (Section 3.4; in the weak-language report one million Amharic tokens are about 330 thousand words against about 540 thousand for Hausa), script and morphology, and Oromo (2.71 characters per token, 31.3 chrF++) and Bambara (2.24, 24.9) lie off the line. Two implications follow. First, the cost of a deployment is not uniform: a service that translates into Tigrinya or Yoruba needs about 1.6 times the compute per sentence of one that translates into Swahili. Second, the output budget must be set per language, because the same English text needs 1.5 to 2.6 times (Section 9.1) as many output tokens in the fragmenting languages, which also lengthens the context and the cache of long documents (not measured). We measured the cost on CPU only; on a GPU the number of decoding steps scales with output tokens in the same way, but we did not measure it.

*A possible mitigation, untested.* TODO(vocab: idea only, no experiment has been run and no number of this paper depends on it; a follow-up will add the result here). Since a decoder pays for a softmax over the whole vocabulary at every step, and 168M of the 270M parameters are that 262,145-entry table (which is also the output head), one could restrict decoding to a per-language subset of the vocabulary: the tokens that occur in the target-language training text (for example those covering 99.9% of the target-language token occurrences of the SFT corpus) plus the language tag, digits, punctuation, a Latin core for names and loanwords, and the special tokens. This would cut the cost of each output token (and, for a single-language deployment, the size of the table held in memory), but not the number of tokens per sentence, which is a property of the tokenizer: Yoruba and Tigrinya would stay about 1.5 to 1.7 times Swahili in tokens. Open questions: how much chrF++ is lost on names, numerals and code-switched text; whether a model trained with the full softmax tolerates the renormalisation without fine-tuning; how large the speed-up is when the rest of the step (attention, feed-forward, memory traffic) is unchanged; and whether the per-token cost is dominated by the output projection at all (Section 6.11 states this as a hypothesis).

### 3.4 Supervised fine-tuning data

All parallel data are *existing* public datasets (we did not create translations); they were regrouped, not newly translated. The sources include benchmark-derived data (found by the audit of Section 6.9a): the SSA-MTE train split (English source with the human reference), SMOL translations (Bambara, Wolof, Ewe), MAFAND-MT test pairs inside aggregated collections, and FLORES+ rows inside aggregated Igbo, Yoruba, Hausa, Luganda and Zulu collections (`African-Languages-Lab/multi-open`, repackaged in `<HF_ORG_LEGACY>/sft-new`, holds 23 FLORES+ devtest and 36 dev sentences with their Igbo translation; the other carriers could not be traced because the SFT windows were not backed up). 82% of the original pairs are single sentences, so consecutive pairs of the same source and language pair were concatenated into *windows* (pseudo-paragraphs, no newline) in four length bins by the English side in AfroXLM-R tokens (b64, b256, b512, b1024; token-balanced, 20-29% of the tokens per bin), so that the model sees long inputs, order and sentence count during SFT; discourse in the true sense comes only from the RL documents. Windows are packed into 4,096-token packs, one language per pack, with block-diagonal attention so that packed samples never see each other and positions restart per sample. The final build has 11,442,172 windows, 538,480 training packs and 2.204 B tokens (1.318 B target tokens), 77 target languages, forward direction (English to X) only. Pairs were vetted with AfriCOMET-QE where the language is covered (bottom 15% per language and QE below 0.20 removed) and with rule-based checks otherwise; training text is NFC (`DECISIONS.md` D-14 and the memory notes; `reports/gemma_sft_training_plan_2026-10-14.md`).

Two properties of this corpus matter for the interpretation of every later result. First, it is dominated by religious text for the low-resource languages: in the replay pool sampled from the SFT windows (87,498 windows, 77 languages) Jehovah's Witnesses / Bible-style text makes up on average about 58% of the tokens and 75-93% for most of the weakest languages (`reports/weak_language_data_2026-10-22.md` section 0, item 4). Second, a large part of it comes from repositories whose licence is unstated: of 292 scanned repositories 213 had no stated licence and were used with a flag, about 58% of the replay tokens are Jehovah's Witnesses text whose publisher copyright is not cleared, and the audit records two owner-approved non-commercial exceptions (`reports/weak_language_data_2026-10-22.md` section 5). The project lead reports a separate verification that found no non-commercial data in the SFT mixture; that verification is not in the repository (TODO(owner: attach the licence verification)) (Section 11).

### 3.5 SFT optimisation

SFT ran in two stages with one set of weights (`reports/gemma_sft_training_plan_2026-10-14.md`; `gemma_mt/configs/sft_unimax.yaml`). Stage A: AdamW, learning rate 5e-5 with 3% warm-up and cosine decay, betas (0.9, 0.95), weight decay 0.01, gradient clip 1.0, fp32 master weights with bf16 autocast, 131,072 tokens per step (32 packs), languages drawn with probability proportional to the square root of their number of packs (floor 0.2%, a language is retired after four passes over its data), stopped at about step 4,750. Stage B (restart with a fresh optimiser and schedule): Muon with learning rate 4e-5 matched to the AdamW update scale, a UniMax-style language budget of 3 B tokens in which every pack of every language is used between one and two times and 20 priority languages get four times the weight. The final checkpoint is step 27,638 of the combined run, with a macro validation loss (mean of the 77 per-language losses) of 1.2654. All languages use the same weights; the choice among checkpoints was made by macro validation loss, which was still falling slowly (1.2706 at step 25,750 to 1.2654 at step 27,638). A checkpoint of this run at step 20,500 (macro validation loss 1.3025) was benchmarked against the untouched base model: on the FLORES+ rows whose English sentence is not in the SFT text the SFT model reaches 27.0 chrF++ against 13.0 for `gemma-3-270m-it` (`reports/gemma_sft_benchmarks_2026-10-18.md` section 2.1), so the base model contributes little African-language ability by itself.

### 3.6 Evaluation data, metrics and the contamination split

We evaluate English-to-X on eight human-reference benchmarks, always on *test* splits: FLORES+ devtest (1,012 sentences, 45 of our languages) [@oldi2025floresplus; @goyal2022flores101; @nllb2022nllb], NTREX-128 (1,997 sentences, 24), MAFAND-MT test (15 languages) [@adelani2022mafand], SmolSent (863 sentences, 45) [@caswell2025smol], IbomMT (Ibibio, Efik), FLORES-MOZ (Sena), SALT (5) [@akera2022salt] and GlotStoryBook (8). **These eight test sets rest on five independent English texts:** FLORES+ devtest, IbomMT and FLORES-MOZ share the same 1,012 English sentences, and the others are NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT, SmolSent (with SmolDoc) and SALT with GlotStoryBook. Metrics are chrF++ (sacreBLEU, word order 2) [@popovic2017chrfpp] and spBLEU (flores200 tokenizer), corpus-level per language and subset, macro-averaged over languages, on NFC text. Greedy decoding with up to 768 new tokens unless stated; a completion that does not end inside the budget is scored as it stands, and the *finished rate* is reported next to it. No test split was used as a training target. Test splits were used as *calibration or selection* material in four places, listed in Section 6.9a: the Efik reward (IbomMT test), the anchors and long-document limits of the final run (NTREX-128, SmolSent, SALT, GlotStoryBook, IbomMT, FLORES-MOZ, SmolDoc, AfriLongDoc core), the choice of the five NLLB-signal languages of the final run (FLORES+ devtest) and the decoding study (FLORES+ devtest).

The SFT text contains the English side of 95.3% of the FLORES+ devtest sentences (and 98.7% of dev), 91.2% of SmolSent, 84.4% of SALT, 39.9% of NTREX-128, 21.9% of MAFAND-MT and 7.2% of GlotStoryBook, and for some languages the reference translation itself (FLORES+ devtest: Hausa 83%, Igbo 55%, Yoruba 35% of the sentences, 933 of 1,012 sentences in at least one language; MAFAND-MT test: Igbo 90%, Yoruba 48%; SALT: Luganda 84%; SmolSent: Wolof 73%, Bambara 65%, Ewe 61%). We could not trace most of the source repositories because the SFT windows were not kept; an 87,498-window sample of the windows shows that the FLORES+ English entered through single-sentence Luganda windows with an independent Luganda translation and through Igbo, Yoruba, Hausa and Zulu windows, some of which carry the FLORES+ translation (Section 6.9a). Every number is therefore reported on three subsets: `all` (every row), `no_pair` (rows whose reference translation was not in the SFT text; 96% of the FLORES+ rows; the headline) and `clean` (rows whose English sentence was never seen; only 48 FLORES+ sentences per language, so per-language `clean` figures are noisy). **`no_pair` removes the reference-pair leak but not the English-side exposure; only `clean` removes both.** Levels on `all` and `no_pair` rows are inflated: on FLORES+ the SFT-final macro is 28.9 on all rows and 25.1 on `clean` rows (RL-final 34.3 and 31.7); we therefore use differences between models that share the exposure, and `clean` rows, as evidence, not levels. Confidence intervals are 95% paired bootstrap intervals over sentences (1,000 resamples; 500 for the per-language table) of the SFT-to-RL difference; they measure sentence-sampling noise, not language-sampling noise, because every language is weighted equally and the language set is fixed. The RL prompt pools were audited against the English side of the test sets for this paper: exact overlap is at most 0.7% of the sentences of any benchmark (Section 6.9). The audit covers the prompts; the reward's thresholds were fitted on human references (FLORES+ dev and MAFAND dev; for Efik the IbomMT test set), see Section 6.9a.

## 4 Reinforcement learning method

{{figure:fig1_pipeline|Overview. (A) The training chronology: each arrow marks the model that starts the next phase; phase 2c restarts from the step-488 model of the diverged phase 2b. (B) The reward of one rollout, computed per sentence chunk and aggregated as a source-length-weighted mean (Sections 4.3 and 4.4).|100}}

### 4.1 GRPO as used here

We use group-relative policy optimisation [@shao2024deepseekmath] with a single on-policy update per sampled batch (so the importance ratio is exactly one and no clipping is needed; this is asserted in the code at every step). For a prompt $x$ with target language $\ell$ the policy samples a group of $G$ completions $y_1,\dots,y_G$ at temperature 1, top-$p$ 1, top-$k$ disabled, so that the sampling distribution is the scored distribution. Each completion receives a scalar reward $r_i \in [-2, 2.25]$ (Section 4.3). The advantage is the group-standardised reward multiplied by a per-language weight $w_\ell$ that reflects how reliable the content signal of that language is,
$$A_i = w_\ell \, \frac{r_i - \bar r}{\sigma_r + 10^{-4}},$$
with $A_i=0$ for every member of a group whose standard deviation is below $10^{-6}$. The loss is the token-level policy-gradient term plus $\beta\,\mathrm{KL}$ to a frozen reference, normalised by the number of completion tokens of the whole optimiser step. The KL term uses the $k_3$ estimator, clamped at a log-ratio of 5 ($k_{3c}$, Section 4.5); $\beta=0.05$ in phases 1-2c and 0.1 in the final run. Optimisation is AdamW (betas 0.9 / 0.95, no weight decay, gradient clip 1.0, fp32 master weights, bf16 autocast) at learning rates of 1e-6 to 3e-6 ({{tref:t_hparams}} in the appendix). Rollouts use vLLM; the trainer checks every 25 steps that engine and trainer log-probabilities agree.

**Prompts.** RL prompts are English windows of consecutive whole sentences from one document (English Wikipedia, FineWeb-Edu), filtered for readability, technical vocabulary, digits, markup and repetition (the scorer is weakest on dense scientific English), in length bins named by their upper bound in AfroXLM-R tokens: b64, b256, b512, b1024 and, from phase 2c, b2048 (windows of 1,025-2,048 tokens made of up to 183 sentences). There are 77 language files; the draw weights favour the weakest languages (1.5-4.0 against 1.0; 2.5-4.0 in phase 2c) and a floor guarantees every language at least two prompts in every block of 20 steps. The final run draws 70% of its prompts from science documents (AfriScience-MT training side, PubMed, arXiv, LibreTexts) and 30% from the general pools.

### 4.2 Length curriculum

The bins are trained in stages, b64 then b256, b512, b1024, then (phase 2c) b2048, and finally a random mixture of all bins. Phase 1 used 40, 60, 60 and 60 steps for the first four stages, then the mixture. Phase 2c, restarted from the b64-stage model of phase 2b, used 391 steps of b256, 446 of b512, 149 of b1024, 298 of b2048 and 149 of the mixture (1,433 steps, about 900,000 sampled completions). Per step the number of prompts shrinks with the length (64 / 48 / 42 / 42 / 21 / 42 prompts for b64 to mixed, each with 16 completions), and the token budget grows with it (384 new tokens for b64 up to 7,168 for b2048 and the mixture, 3.5 times the source length). The curriculum is trained with the *chunked* reward below, so a long output is scored sentence by sentence even though it is generated in one pass.

### 4.3 The reward

The reward is the central engineering contribution of this work. Its structure is in {{fref:fig1_pipeline}}B; this section gives the logic, Appendix C the formulas and calibration facts.

**Sanitisation and hard failures.** The completion is cut at the first stop token, decoded, scrubbed of special-token strings, NFC-normalised and stripped of the language tag; only this text reaches any scorer. A completion that does not end inside the budget, writes anything after the end token, contains a turn marker, has a missing or wrong tag, is empty, copies the source (word-set Jaccard of at least 0.9), is degenerate (one word repeated more than four times in a row, fewer than 30% distinct words), has a character length ratio outside [0.2, 5] (lower bound 0.4 for languages without a human length window), or is classified by GlotLID [@kargaran2023glotlid] as another language receives the floor reward $-2$ and is never scored. The GlotLID guard is enabled for 75 of the 77 languages (those whose measured recall on human text is at least 0.90; off for Pidgin and Kongo), with sibling labels accepted where the classifier confuses close languages and a minimum text length per language.

**Chunking.** Source and output are split into sentences and aligned: if the counts are equal the $i$-th pair is scored, otherwise a language-aware Gale-Church alignment [@gale1993program] with constant-cost omission and extra-sentence beads (the length model comes from the language's human references, not from the pair itself) pairs them. An omitted sentence is a *failed chunk* (reward $-2$, weighted by its source characters); an unmatched extra output sentence or text appended beyond the language's length window is a failed chunk weighted by its output characters. Without the language-aware cost the legacy aligner found an omission bead in only 2 of 20,000 synthetic trials (0% sentence recall); with it, 56% of dropped sentences, 77% of documents and 89% of dropped characters are found at 0.6% false alarms on seven of eight languages, and extra sentences go from 42% to 93% ({{tref:t_anchors}} and Appendix C).

**Content signal.** For the 42 validated languages and the 12 blend languages the content signal is reference-free quality estimation. We started with AfriCOMET-QE [@wang2024africomet] and, after a study of agreement with human judgments (Section 7.1), replaced it for the final run by an *ensemble* with SSA-COMET-MTL run without reference [@li2025ssacomet]. For each sentence pair the two scores are shaped separately into rewards $r_1,r_2$ (below) and combined as
$$r = \begin{cases} \tfrac12 (r_1 + r_2) & |r_1 - r_2| \le \delta \\ \min(r_1, r_2) & \text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad \delta = 1.0,$$
so that a chunk that only one judge likes (the signature of copying the English source, which the joint-encoder SSA-COMET-MTL rewards) earns the lower of the two. This is a design choice tested on corrupted references (Section 7.2); its effect on the trained policy was not measured (Section 7.9). For the 23 consensus-only languages, and with weight 0.5 for the 12 blend languages, the content signal is the *consensus*: the mean sentence chrF++ of a completion against the other valid completions of its own group, mapped linearly onto the reward range ($-2 + 4.25\cdot\mathrm{chrF}/100$). Groups with fewer than three valid members are skipped; near-loops are kept out of the pool by a self-repetition guard. The final run adds, for five covered weak languages (Kabuverdianu, Kabiye, Nuer, Bambara, Kabyle), a signal from pseudo-references produced by NLLB-200 3.3B (weight 0.23-0.35); see Section 7.5 for why this is restricted.

**Shaping.** A raw QE score is mapped per language onto a piecewise-linear, monotone curve with three anchors measured on human references of that language: the reward is 0 at the 90th percentile of the score of *mismatched* human references (the failure line), $+1$ at the median of the score of matched human references and $+2$ at their 90th percentile, with only a further +0.25 in total between that point and a score of 1 ("a score above what human translations get is where a learned metric is gamed"). The reward range is therefore $[-2, +2.25]$ and a translation at the level of a human median earns $+1$. Each language also receives an advantage weight in $[0, 1]$ derived from how reliably its QE score ranks the human reference above corrupted versions of the same source.

**Penalties.** Subtracted from the shaped chunk reward and floored at $-2$: a length-window and a word-ratio penalty against the human distribution of the language; a lexical penalty (numbers and names in the source that disappear, content-word precision); a diacritic penalty (density of tone marks and special letters below the human 10th percentile, or foreign, orphan, unseen or over-decorated marks); and, for the final run, the *anchors* of Section 7.3 (substring copy of the source, graded entity loss, function-word profile, padding by type/token ratio, repetition and a cross-prompt boilerplate buffer). Every threshold of every penalty is a quantile of *human* text (for RL-final the dev references of FLORES+ and MAFAND, and for Efik the IbomMT *test* set; for the anchors of the final run also the test splits of six benchmarks: Section 6.9a) and every penalty is a penalty only, never a bonus: a bonus for retaining numbers or names would push towards copying. The anchors are a design motivated by detection tests on corrupted human text (Section 7.3). What they did in the final run is reported in Section 7.9: no demonstrated protective effect for the copy, entity and function-word terms (RL-final did not show these failures), padding and repetition flags 2-4 pp lower than RL-final in the weak and consensus-only languages, and no control run from the same start with the plain reward.

**Aggregation.** The reward of a completion is the source-length-weighted mean of its chunk rewards, in which failed chunks count as $-2$.

### 4.4 Reward v2 for long documents

For sources of at least 150 words the final run uses a second reward (Section 7.4). The v1 rule that flags a document as degenerate when fewer than 30% of its words are distinct fires on length alone (it flags 47% of good sentence-by-sentence translations at b4096), so v2 replaces it by a continuous multi-level repetition *severity*: word 4-gram repeat share, share of words in repeated word 8-grams, duplicated sentences, repeated blocks and periodic word cycles, each measured as the excess over the same share of the English source and ramped between the 99.5th percentile of human documents of that length bucket and full degeneracy. A stuttering sentence is a failed chunk, not a failed document. Alignment uses numbers and names as anchors and removes redundant copies first (pair F1 0.89 against 0.66 for the legacy aligner over twelve corruptions). The sentence rewards are aggregated by the mean minus the tail deficit beyond what human documents show (`cvar_excess`), and a coverage term lowers the reward towards the failure value as the failed share of the source grows. Section 7.9 reports what the final run achieved with it: whole-document output far better than RL-final's, of which about three quarters were already in place after the science stage and before this reward, a rise of severe repetition from step 250 to the end of the run, and no control run with reward v1.

### 4.5 Stability: the divergence of phase 2b, and the guards

Phase 2b (lr 3e-6, group of 16 with one greedy member, *no* division by the group standard deviation, $k_3$ KL with coefficient 0.05) ran smoothly in its b64 stage, with a KL of 0.03-0.04 to the reference and a gradient norm of 0.5-0.7, and diverged when the b256 stage began at step 489: the mean KL rose from 0.04 to 0.27 at step 541, 1.8 at step 661 and 2.6 at step 781, the finished rate of the evaluation prompts fell from 0.95 to 0.56, and we stopped the run at step 839 ({{fref:fig3_phase_diagnostics}}). Sentence-level dev chrF++ stayed at 41.9-42.0 until step 700 and fell to 41.4 only at step 750: the metric that selects checkpoints noticed the divergence last. Phase 2c restarted from the step-488 model with three changes made together: standardised advantages (as in phase 2), a lower learning rate of 2e-6, and a *KL guard* that halves the learning rate (down to 1/8) when the mean KL of the last five steps exceeds 0.15. We did not run ablations that isolate which change fixed the instability; the greedy group member was also removed in 2c, and later measurements show that a looping greedy completion of several thousand tokens produces gradient norms of 8-19 (against 0.5 without it) because all its tokens push in the same direction (`reports/final_run_integration_2026-10-21.md` section 5), so it is a candidate cause as well.

Later in 2c two single steps showed KL values of 0.51 (step 607) and 1,651 (step 747) with gradient norms of 220 and 285,381: the unbounded $k_3$ estimator $e^{d}-d-1$ explodes for a sampled token whose probability the policy has pushed far below the reference. With the gradient clip at 1.0 such a step is effectively discarded, but the guard cut the learning rate after step 747 although nothing had diverged. We therefore clamp the log-ratio at 5 ($k_{3c}$, identical below the clamp, no gradient beyond it) and compute the guard from the median instead of the mean of its window; both were applied from the b2048 restart at step 900.

### 4.6 Science stage and weight interpolation

Between the general RL (step 1,433) and the final run we added a short supervised stage on the AfriScience-MT corpus [@abdulmumin2026afriscience] (six African languages, simplified science text, 5,792 training sentences per language) mixed with replay of the general human references, self-distilled "anchor" examples, the best translations saved by the RL archive and *long-document distillation*: documents translated sentence by sentence by the RL model with a best-of-four choice by the QE ensemble and joined into one target, so that the model learns to translate a whole document and to stop (13.5% of the chosen mixture). The stage has about 320 steps at 3e-6. Because plain fine-tuning lowered the weak-blend languages, the result is *interpolated* with the RL model, $\theta=(1-\alpha)\theta_{\text{RL}}+\alpha\theta_{\text{SFT-stage}}$ [@wortsman2022robust], and the mixture and $\alpha$ were chosen by guardrails (Section 6.6): best sentence-level chrF++ on the AfriScience dev set such that the general dev set, every weak-language group and the paper-level finished rate do not get worse.

### 4.7 The final run (final2)

The final run starts from the chosen interpolated model (mixture "A2W", $\alpha=0.7$) with the KL reference set to that model, 700 planned steps (the run was wound down after step 350 and ended at step 401, Section 6.7) at learning rate 1e-6 (20 warm-up steps), 16 completions per group of which one is greedy, with the greedy member's advantage computed from the statistics of the sampled members only, capped at 3 and scaled by 0.3, the KL guard, the QE ensemble, the v2/v3 anchors, the long-document reward v2, the NLLB signal for five languages, the language floor and a static per-language draw-weight list (Appendix B, {{tref:t_hparams}}). A chrF-informed adaptive language-weight controller exists but ran in dry-run mode only (it logged proposals and changed nothing). The run had a planned restart at step 150 and was OOM-killed twice on 2026-10-23 and resumed from its step-250 checkpoint, with the learning-rate scale raised from 0.5 to 0.75 and the KL guard reset (Section 6.7); it ended at step 401 after a 50-step linear learning-rate wind-down.

## 5 Experimental setup

**Systems.** *SFT-final*: the supervised model at step 27,638 (Section 3.5). *RL-final*: the general RL model at GRPO step 1,433 of phase 2c (our "final general model"). *A2W*: the science-stage model interpolated at $\alpha=0.7$, the start of the final run. *final2*: the final science and general RL run (401 steps, Section 6.7). Published baselines are taken from the literature, not re-run: the per-language English-to-X chrF++ tables of NLLB-200 1.3B and 3.3B (model release, FLORES-200 devtest, beam search) [@nllb2022nllb] and of AfriqueLlama-8B, AfriqueGemma-12B and AfriqueQwen-14B (Table 22 of that paper: FLORES-200 English-to-X chrF++ run through the AfroBench-Lite harness; split and number of shots for this table not stated, base models are evaluated few-shot) [@yu2026afriquellm]; they cover 44 and 21 of our 77 languages respectively. Differences in benchmark version (FLORES+ devtest here, FLORES-200 devtest there), decoding and metric implementation (not documented for the Afrique tables; the NLLB values are rounded to one decimal) are discussed in Section 6.2. We re-ran NLLB-200 3.3B and 600M ourselves as teacher and pseudo-reference generator (Section 7.5, beam 4, CTranslate2); those translations of the FLORES+ devtest, and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B (and, for 9 languages, 2B) run zero-shot with the prompt of its model card, were re-scored with the same metric stack as our own checkpoints for the comparison on identical sentences of Section 6.8 (chrF++, spBLEU, reference-based AfriCOMET-STL-1.1, SSA-COMET-MTL for 22 languages), because AfriCOMET is published for almost none of the baselines.

**Long documents.** AfriLongDoc is a private benchmark we built for this work: 450 English core documents in six length bins (b64 to b4096; 73 / 90 / 100 / 89 / 63 / 35 documents in the evaluated subset) from relaxed-licence sources, with NLLB-200 3.3B and MADLAD-400 3B sentence-by-sentence pseudo-references, plus SmolDoc human references (documents of up to about 680 tokens, and unrelated documents *stitched* to about 512, 1,024 and 2,048 tokens, which test length robustness but not discourse). The long-document tables use 16 languages (12 with a human and an NLLB reference, 4 with NLLB only) and, for every configuration comparison, a fixed subset of 8 languages (hau, yor, swh, amh, zul, wol, kea, sag). Document-level chrF++ is a corpus statistic summed over documents; bootstrap intervals resample documents.

**Compute.** One RTX PRO 6000 (96 GB) rented on vast.ai at about 1.19 USD per hour (the rate stated by the project lead; an earlier internal estimate used 1.03 USD per hour) for RL phases 1-2c and the final run, plus an RTX 5070 (12 GB) for the first part of SFT. Phase 2c took 40.5 hours of wall-clock time including restarts (median 107 s per step, 38.5 hours of step time), i.e. about 48 USD at the quoted rate; the final run took 26.2 hours of step time (median 218 s per step) for its 401 steps and 28.4 hours between its first and last logged step, i.e. about 34 USD at the quoted rate. The final run had a planned restart after step 150 and was OOM-killed twice on 2026-10-23 (at 04:22 and 04:49 UTC) because other jobs on the shared GPU had filled its memory, and was resumed from its step-250 checkpoint; the learning-rate scale was raised from 0.5 to 0.75 and the KL guard was reset by the restarts (Section 6.7). Steps 251-267 were run twice more and discarded, so the wall-clock cost of the run exceeds the step time of its final lineage. The full ledger of what was run is in Appendix F.

## 6 Results

### 6.1 From SFT to RL: sentence-level translation

We lead with the evidence least affected by the exposure of the test sets (Section 6.9a) and keep `all` and `no_pair` visible in {{tref:t_main}}. On the `clean` rows (the English sentence was never in the SFT text; 48 FLORES+ sentences per language) RL raises the FLORES+ macro chrF++ over 45 languages from 25.1 to 31.7 (+6.62 [5.83, 7.24]), and the gain is as large or larger on NTREX-128 (+4.09 [3.87, 4.32]), SmolSent (+7.06 [6.17, 7.70]) and GlotStoryBook (+8.26 [7.58, 8.87]), equal on MAFAND-MT (+3.66 [3.03, 4.16]) and smaller but positive on SALT (+2.31 [0.85, 3.57]). On FLORES+ devtest restricted to the rows whose reference pair the model never saw (`no_pair`, 96% of the rows; the headline of the earlier drafts) RL raises the macro chrF++ over 45 languages from 28.7 to 34.1, a gain of 5.42 [5.22, 5.61], and spBLEU from 9.2 to 12.4 (+3.19 [2.97, 3.38]). The exposure raises the *level* of both models: on FLORES+ the macro over all rows minus the macro over the `clean` rows is +3.8 chrF++ for SFT-final and +2.6 for RL-final (about 3.2 and 2.0 after the 0.6 that the harder clean sentences cost the untrained base model), +6.4 and +4.6 on SmolSent, and the `no_pair` headline levels (28.7 and 34.1) stay about 3.6 and 2.4 above the `clean` level; the headline *differences* are less affected because both models share the SFT exposure. All 45 FLORES+ languages improve and none drops; on `no_pair` rows the same pattern holds on NTREX-128 (+3.86 [3.72, 4.01]), MAFAND-MT (+3.66 [3.12, 4.11]), SmolSent (+5.36 [5.11, 5.57]), GlotStoryBook (+8.16) and IbomMT (+11.04), whose Ibibio and Efik reference sets are outside the usual benchmarks. **IbomMT is two languages and only one of them is clean of the reward calibration:** the Efik thresholds of the reward were fitted on the IbomMT test set (997 of its 1,012 sentences, Section 6.9a), so the Efik gain (SFT 18.5 to RL 31.2, +12.70 [11.67, 13.56]) is not an independent result, whereas the Ibibio gain (12.9 to 22.3, +9.37 [8.69, 9.99]) is unaffected; the macro of +11.04 mixes the two ({{tref:t_main}}, {{fref:fig2_per_language_gain}}). The *finished rate* (the completion ends inside the 768-token budget) rises from 90.4% to 100.0%, and the GlotLID right-language rate from 95.2% to 96.7%.

{{table:t_main|SFT-final versus RL-final (GRPO step 1,433), greedy decoding, English to X. Macro over the languages of each benchmark; $\Delta$ with 95% paired-bootstrap intervals over sentences (macro, equal language weights). `all`: every row; `no\_pair`: reference pair not in the SFT text; `clean`: English sentence not in the SFT text. $^\dagger$ The Efik reward thresholds were fitted on this test set (Section 6.9a); Ibibio alone: +9.37 [8.69, 9.99]. IbomMT and FLORES-MOZ share the English of FLORES+ devtest (five English texts in eight benchmarks). Source: `reports/final_model_per_language_2026-10-21.md` section 2.1 and `reports/data/final_model_runs/macro_boot.json`.}}

Two qualifications belong next to this headline. (i) *Budget.* A looping completion is scored as it stands, so the SFT model is penalised more at a generous budget: at the 320-token budget of our earlier reports the same comparison is 30.4 to 34.1 (+3.70); about 1.7 of the 5.4 points of the 768-token gain are the SFT model's runaway tail, and the remaining 3.7 still contain the 10% of SFT sentences that loop inside 320 tokens. Part of what RL buys is therefore *learning to stop*, and our data cannot separate it from translation quality. (ii) *Groups.* Gains are larger where the SFT model is weaker ({{tref:t_groups}}): +4.95 [4.75, 5.14] for the 40 QE-validated FLORES+ languages, +11.22 [10.68, 11.75] for the two consensus-only languages (Kabuverdianu, Makhuwa), +14.59 for the one Creole in the table, and only +1.26 for Afrikaans and Malagasy. On the *primary* benchmark of every language (a coverage summary, not a like-for-like score) the macro gain is +4.97 [4.78, 5.16] for the 42 QE-validated, +7.93 [7.55, 8.36] for the 9 blend and +7.69 [7.30, 8.07] for the 13 consensus-only languages that have a human-reference set (Appendix A, {{tref:t_master}}). The consensus-only languages, for which the reward contained *no learned quality metric*, thus improved at least as much as the validated ones. *The largest gains.* On the 44 languages that also have an NLLB row (the identical-sentence pipeline of Section 6.8, `no_pair`) the largest gains of RL-final over SFT-final are Kabuverdianu +14.2, Kabiye +14.1, Kamba +12.1, Luo +11.9, Nuer +11.4, Kanuri +11.0, Bambara +9.2 and Kimbundu +8.9 chrF++, against a mean gain of +5.35 and a median of +4.38 ({{tref:t_skew_langs}}; the generation-run table of this section gives +14.6, +14.2 and +11.5 for Kabuverdianu, Kabiye and Nuer, differing from this pipeline by at most 0.53 chrF++ for any of the 45 languages). Part of the largest gains is repair, not new knowledge: the SFT model finished only 34% of the Kabiye, 51% of the Nuer, 70% of the Kamba and 76% of the Kabuverdianu translations (RL-final 100%), Kanuri has no SFT data and its gain is a stopping effect, and in the controlled ablation of Section 8.4 the gain on the sentences that every system finished is much smaller than on all sentences (+1.13 against +7.36 from the weak start, +2.23 against +5.72 from SFT-final; four languages, one run per arm). The gains of Luo, Bambara and Kimbundu, where SFT finished 85-91%, cannot be explained by repair alone. Kabuverdianu is both the largest gain over SFT (+14.2) and the largest remaining gap to NLLB-200 3.3B (21.0), and we show both.

{{table:t_groups|SFT to RL by reward group and by family on FLORES+ devtest (`no\_pair`). Group sizes on this benchmark are small for blend (2), consensus-only (2) and SFT-only (1) languages.}}

{{figure:fig2_per_language_gain|Per-language chrF++ on FLORES+ devtest (`no_pair` rows), SFT-final to RL-final, sorted by the RL score; colours are the reward groups. Black ticks: NLLB-200 3.3B and pink diamonds: AfriqueGemma-12B, both as published for FLORES-200 devtest (a different benchmark version and decoding; see Section 6.2). Right: RL minus SFT with 95% paired-bootstrap intervals (500 resamples per language). Kanuri (`knc`, grey) has no SFT data; see the text.|100}}

Where RL is *not* better: the only chrF++ regression whose interval excludes zero on a `no_pair` subset is Luganda on SALT (-2.62 [-5.17, -0.33], 81 sentences); spBLEU falls slightly for Sesotho on FLORES+ (-0.90 [-1.36, -0.42]) and for Hausa on MAFAND (-0.48 [-0.77, -0.17]) although chrF++ rises there; and the smallest gains, for Sesotho, Zulu, Xhosa, Afrikaans, Somali and Swahili (+0.4 to +1.1), are the high-resource languages that start from the highest chrF++, where several intervals include zero (Zulu +0.5 [-0.0, +1.0], Sesotho +0.4 [-0.1, +1.0]).

A caution on *zero-shot* languages. Kanuri (`knc`) and North Ndebele (`nde`) have no SFT data. Kanuri improves from 3.9 to 14.8 chrF++ (+10.89), but GlotLID recognises none of its output as Kanuri (0.0% before and after), the model does not write the language tag for it, and the finished rate rises from 37.6% to 99.9%; we read this as a stopping and script effect on a language that the model cannot yet write, **not** as evidence that RL generalises to a language without data.

### 6.2 Comparison with published systems

{{tref:t_published}} compares the model with the per-language chrF++ tables published for NLLB-200 and for three Afrique LLMs. The caveats are material: the published numbers are for FLORES-200 devtest (the same sentences as FLORES+ devtest, with some corrected references), NLLB uses beam search, the Afrique models are evaluated few-shot through a different harness (split not stated), and our model saw the English side of 95% of the sentences (we did not check what the published systems saw: NLLB-200 used FLORES as its development set [@nllb2022nllb]; for the others we do not know). We therefore show the `all` and `no_pair` rows only, which are inflated by the exposure of Section 6.9a (by about 3.6 chrF++ against the `clean` level for SFT-final and 2.4 for RL-final). **We do not compare our `clean` rows with published numbers:** an earlier draft did (a gap of 6.6 to NLLB-200 3.3B on `clean` rows, and a sign change against the Afrique LLMs), but our `clean` rows are 48 harder sentences per language and the published numbers are for all 1,012 sentences, so the comparison was not like for like (the clean sentences are harder for every system: NLLB-200 3.3B scores 38.3 on all rows and 35.0 on `clean` rows of the identical sentences). On identical FLORES+ sentences scored by us (Section 6.8) the gap to NLLB-200 3.3B is 3.84 chrF++ on all rows, 3.85 on `no_pair` and 3.16 on `clean` rows (44 languages). On the 43 languages shared with NLLB-200 3.3B our model is 3.9 chrF++ below on all rows (4.1 on `no_pair`), better on 8 of 43 (Yoruba, Fon, Wolof and Dyula lead by 0.8-2.3), with the largest deficits for Kabuverdianu (-21.1), Amharic (-19.0), Tigrinya (-11.0) and Sesotho (-10.1, against AfriqueGemma-12B). A 270 M-parameter model is therefore within about three to four chrF++ points of a model more than 12 times larger on average, but not better. On the 21 languages covered by all five systems the macro of our `no_pair` rows is 0.6 to 2.5 chrF++ above the three Afrique LLMs (8-14 B parameters) and that of our `all` rows 1.0 to 2.9 above, but we read neither as a result: our rows contain the English of 95% of the sentences, the published numbers were measured on all 1,012 sentences, and a comparison on `clean` rows is not possible because the per-sentence outputs of those systems are not available. We make no claim about superiority or inferiority to the Afrique LLMs. As we read it, TranslatePsy-AfriSLM reports 19-language macros over both translation directions, which cannot be compared with an English-to-X number [@gritta2026translatepsy] (we did not confirm the aggregation in the paper's full text); our English-to-X macro on those 19 languages is 41.7 (`reports/comparison_with_published_2026-10-21.md` section 5). Section 6.8 repeats the comparison on identical sentences, with NLLB-200 and TranslatePsy re-scored by us, and supersedes the cross-paper reading of this subsection wherever the two differ.

{{table:t_published|Macro chrF++ of RL-final on the languages shared with each published system (English to X, FLORES+ devtest for us, FLORES-200 devtest for them) and the difference. "ours better on": number of shared languages where RL-final scores higher. Only `all` and `no_pair` rows are shown: `clean` rows (48 sentences per language, harder for every system) cannot be set against published numbers for all 1,012 sentences, and both shown subsets are inflated by the exposure of Section 6.9a. A comparison of this kind is indicative only (different harness, decoding and split).}}

{{table:t_published21|Macro chrF++ on the 21 languages covered by all five systems (afr amh ewe hau ibo kin lin lug orm sna som sot swh tir tsn twi wol xho yor zul and nya). The published values are fixed; only the subset of our rows changes. The `clean` row of earlier drafts is removed (not like for like, see Section 6.2); both rows shown are inflated by the exposure of Section 6.9a.}}

### 6.3 Where the gain was made

{{fref:fig3_phase_diagnostics}} (top row) and {{tref:t_phases}} show *when* the gain appeared. On the 760-sentence dev set (19 languages with human references, sentences whose English side is not in the SFT text; 40 rows per language of a 10,043-row file made of 1,190 FLORES+ *dev* rows, 70 for each of 17 languages, and 8,853 MAFAND *dev* rows, with no FLORES+ devtest sentence: 0 of its 30,418 parent rows have their English in devtest) macro chrF++ rises from 39.65 for SFT-final to 41.00 after the first 50 steps of phase 1, to 41.77 at step 100 of phase 2, and then stays between 41.8 and 42.0 through the stable b64 stage of phase 2b (to step 488) and between 41.57 and 41.98 over the whole of phase 2c: over its 1,433 steps and about 900,000 sampled completions the dev macro moves from 41.89 to 41.88 (31 evaluations, range 41.57 to 41.98). On the sets that matter for the weakest languages (13 consensus-only and 19 blend languages with benchmarks, evaluated at each stage branch, 320-token budget) most of the gain is in place at the end of the b64 stage of phase 2b: +4.8 [3.6, 5.9] chrF++ for the consensus-only group against +1.6 [1.1, 2.3] after phase 1, and +5.1 [3.8, 6.2] at the end of 2c, i.e. the b256-to-mixed stages of 2c add 0.3. For the 10 control languages (QE-validated) the gains are +1.8 after phase 1, +2.5 after 2b, +2.4 at the end. The headline checkpoint is the last step (1,433; dev chrF++ 41.883), not the dev-best step (986; 41.977): the dev curve is flat (41.567-41.977) and selection by dev played no role for RL-final. We interpret the plateau in Section 10.

{{table:t_phases|Group means of chrF++ (all rows, 320-token budget) for the checkpoints at the ends of the RL stages (`reports/rl_vs_sft_weak_signal_2026-10-21/results.json`). "2b (step 488)" is the model that phase 2c started from.}}

{{table:t_phases_delta|Difference to SFT-final with 95% bootstrap intervals over languages.}}

{{figure:fig3_phase_diagnostics|Training diagnostics per phase (columns: width proportional to the number of steps). Row 1: dev chrF++ (19 languages, 760 sentences). Row 2: KL to the reference (log scale). Row 3: pre-clip gradient norm (log). Row 4: mean training reward (the definition changed between phases). Phase 2b (orange) diverged from step ~490; phase 2c restarted from its step-488 model, and its curriculum stages are shaded; the two $k_3$ outliers at steps 607 and 747 motivated the clamped estimator. The final2 column is the final run (401 steps; the shaded band is its learning-rate wind-down, steps 351-401; the dev curve is the general dev set, Section 6.7). Sources: `paper/data/rl*_steps.csv` (from the trainer logs), `rl2c_metrics.jsonl`, `final2_metrics.jsonl`.|100}}

### 6.4 Long documents: what the length curriculum did and did not achieve

The goal of the curriculum was to make a 270 M model translate documents of up to 2,048 tokens. {{fref:fig4_length_bins}} and {{tref:t_longdoc_chrf}} show the result and it is mixed.

*Whole-document generation by RL-final does not work beyond about 512 source tokens, whatever the decoding setting* (this subsection is about the SFT-final and RL-final checkpoints; the science-stage checkpoints and other systems are evaluated in Section 6.10). With greedy decoding the final model finishes 98 / 97 / 92 / 65 / 42 / 31% of the documents of the bins b64, b256, b512, b1024, b2048, b4096, 0 / 1 / 13 / 53 / 73 / 73% of its outputs contain a loop that the reference does not, and chrF++ against the NLLB reference falls from 47.6 to 47.7, 47.7, 29.7, 18.2 and 9.8 ({{tref:t_longdoc_fin}}). This reproduces the reward measured on b2048 prompts (greedy total reward -1.95, 7% "ok"). The SFT model fails earlier: whole-document SFT output is already degraded at b256 (74% finished) and reaches 32.5, 17.0, 11.1 and 6.9 chrF++ at b512 to b4096. RL therefore *did* extend the range of single-pass translation, from about 256 to about 512 source tokens (b512: 32.5 to 47.7), and it improved every longer bin, but it did not make single-pass translation of 1,024- or 2,048-token inputs work. Repetition penalties, frequency penalty, temperature from 0.3 to 1.0, best-of-N and no-repeat n-grams trade loops for *truncation*: at T = 0.7 documents finish (99 / 95 / 78% at b1024 / b2048 / b4096) but the output is only 77%, 56% and 33% as long as the reference, so chrF++ stays at 26-37 for b1024-b2048 and 14.5 at b4096. We did not evaluate the intermediate checkpoints on AfriLongDoc, so the gain in single-pass range cannot be attributed to the b2048 stage rather than to the earlier stages.
