*Chunking with the same model works.* Translating sentence by sentence (greedy) and re-decoding the 1.0% of units that do not emit the end token by best-of-four at T = 0.7 ("sentfix") gives chrF++ of 47.6, 49.0, 53.5, 55.6, 58.4 and 60.1 for the six bins, 100% sentence coverage and 0-4% loops; against the *human* SmolDoc references it reaches 47.2 / 49.1 / 50.6 chrF++ on short documents and on stitched documents of about 512 and 1,024 tokens (six languages) and 52.4 on stitched documents of about 2,048 tokens (four languages), where whole-document greedy decoding reaches 18.2 ({{tref:t_longdoc_human}}). Its AfriCOMET is 0.691 at b64 and 0.659 at b4096 (reference-based, aligned chunks), a mild decline. It is also faster in wall-clock time on a shared GPU (1,196 s against 1,912 s for all 9,506 items). So *RL-final can translate documents of 2,048 and even 4,096 tokens, but only when decoding is chunked by sentence* (after the science stage the whole-document mode works to about 2,048 tokens but omits text, Section 6.10); windows of 256 tokens are an acceptable alternative (tied with sentences against human references at 1,024 and 2,048 tokens, 1.0-1.3 chrF++ lower against NLLB, twice the loop rate). The final model is 2.1-3.3 chrF++ above the SFT model under the same chunking, and the SFT model does not stop reliably even then (13-48% finished at b512-b4096, loops in 42-59%).

{{figure:fig4_length_bins|Whole-document versus chunked decoding by source length bin (AfriLongDoc core set, macro over 8 languages; sentfix interval = 95% document bootstrap). The SFT model and the RL model fail at different lengths in single-pass decoding; chunking by sentence removes the failure for both. Bottom right: human SmolDoc references (stitched documents beyond ~680 tokens).|100}}

{{table:t_longdoc_chrf|Document-level chrF++ against the NLLB-200 3.3B sentence-by-sentence pseudo-reference by length bin, macro over 8 languages (`reports/longdoc_eval_final_model_2026-10-21.md` section 3). Absolute values are not comparable across bins (different texts).}}

{{table:t_longdoc_fin|Finished rate / documents with a loop (%), same configurations.}}

{{table:t_longdoc_human|Document-level chrF++ [95% CI] against human SmolDoc references (clean documents; "short" = unstitched; 6 languages, 4 for ~2,048 tokens). Stitched documents are unrelated documents concatenated: they test length, not discourse.}}

Four limits keep these numbers honest. (i) *Pseudo-reference bias.* The NLLB reference is itself sentence-by-sentence, which favours chunked hypotheses by construction; the model agrees more with NLLB (65.9 chrF++ at b1024 on five languages) than MADLAD-400 does (62.7), and NLLB-referenced scores are 1-16 chrF++ above the human-referenced scores for all 12 languages with both. We therefore quote the human-reference column for absolute quality (47 to 52 chrF++ from short to ~2,048-token documents) and use the NLLB column only to compare strategies; the ordering (chunked far above whole-document beyond ~512 tokens; sentence at least as good as window) is the same against MADLAD, against humans and under reference-free QE, but "sentence much better than window" is not established. (ii) Absolute chrF++ rises with the bin because the bins are different texts; the AfriCOMET column is the comparable one. (iii) The human documents beyond ~680 tokens are stitched from unrelated ones, so none of this measures discourse quality (pronouns, terminology across paragraphs). (iv) The language set (8 / 12 / 16) is fixed and the intervals resample documents only (35-100 per bin).

The *training* reward tells the same story. The total RL reward of whole-document greedy output is 0.73, 0.60, -0.25, -1.53, -1.95, -2.00 for b64 to b4096, that of sentfix 0.77, 0.83, 0.12, -0.19, -0.46, -1.03, and of the NLLB translations themselves under the same detectors 1.03, 0.89, 0.31, 0.10, -0.20, -0.96 ({{tref:t_longdoc_reward}}): at b1024 and beyond, "about -0.2 to -1.0" is the practical ceiling of the v1 reward because its repetition detector fails good translations (Section 7.4). For T = 1 *rollouts* of the b1024 and b2048 bins the groups are almost flat (the within-group standard deviation of the reward is 0.18 and 0.11): the sampled members all stop, all truncate, and are all about equally bad, so GRPO has little to learn from (`reports/longdoc_reward_v2_2026-10-22.md` section 0).

{{table:t_longdoc_reward|Mean total RL reward (reward v1) per bin, macro over 8 languages, 12 documents per bin and language; yardsticks are the NLLB and MADLAD pseudo-references scored as if they were hypotheses.}}

### 6.5 Decoding

For short text, beam search is the only family that improves on greedy decoding: macro chrF++ over 20 languages (200 `no_pair` sentences each) is 32.52 for greedy, 33.18 for beam 4 (+0.66 [+0.55, +0.78]) and 33.26 for beam 8, with AfriCOMET 0.6230, 0.6338 and 0.6344. Beam search loops where greedy does not (0.1% for greedy, 0.6% for beam 4, concentrated in Nuer and Kabiye), so a greedy fallback when the beam output is unfinished, repetitive or empty is recommended (33.20). Consensus decoding (MBR over 8 samples at T 0.5-0.7) gains about 0.5 chrF++ but no spBLEU; single-sample sampling is monotonically *worse* (T = 0.3 / 0.5 / 0.7: -0.13 / -0.35 / -0.79), and the Gemma default sampler shipped in `generation_config.json` (T 1, top-$k$ 64, top-$p$ 0.95) costs 1.40 chrF++; repetition penalty 1.1 costs 1.48 and `no_repeat_ngram_size` 3 costs 1.06 and removes 80% of the copied numbers ({{tref:t_decoding}}, {{fref:fig6_decoding}}). Greedy output is nevertheless repetitive in the weakest languages (a repeated word 3-gram in 52% of Kabiye and Nuer sentences against 2.7% in the references), and removing the repetition by sampling costs more chrF++ than it gains (`reports/generation_config_study_2026-10-22.md`). For the SFT model the picture is different (it loops in 10.9% of sentences; T 0.5 helps its weak group by +1.40, MBR over 16 samples gives +2.45), which is one more way in which RL changed the model beyond a shift in chrF++.

{{figure:fig6_decoding|(a) Decoding rules for the RL model, difference in macro chrF++ to greedy (20 languages, 200 sentences each; 95% paired bootstrap). (b) Single-sample samplers: temperature alone and with a top-$p$ 0.9 cut-off (80 combinations of temperature and cut-off were run; none beats greedy).|100}}

{{table:t_decoding|All sentence-level decoding configurations (`reports/final_model_decoding_study_2026-10-21.md` section 2). Asterisk: the paired interval excludes 0. Cost relative to greedy on the same engine.}}

### 6.6 The science stage and its guardrails

A short supervised stage on AfriScience-MT with replay improves science translation sharply but, in its plain form, costs the languages that RL had improved with its own consensus signal ({{tref:t_sft_stage_v1}}): with the first mixture (science plus replay), the sentence chrF++ on the AfriScience dev set rises from 45.65 (RL-final) to 47.7-48.1 and the paper-level chrF from 36.0 to 45-46, and the paper-level finished rate from 74.7% to 89-92% (interpolation weights 0.5 to 1.0; at 0.3 the gains are smaller), but the 19 weak-blend languages lose 0.4 to 2.3 chrF++ at every interpolation weight ($\alpha=0.7$: -1.04 [-1.39, -0.68]), and an L2-SP pull towards the start weights does not fix it. The cause is the replay of human references that pull the model back from what its own consensus signal had taught it. A second mixture (halved weak-language human replay, unfiltered self-distilled anchors, RL-archive translations and 13.5% long-document distillation; "A2W") brings the weak-blend loss at $\alpha=0.7$ to -0.36 [-0.58, -0.16] while keeping the science gain (sentence 47.97, paper-level chrF 47.81, finished 92.0%) and passes the guardrail rule ({{tref:t_sft_stage_v2}}, {{fref:fig7_sft_stage_guardrail}}). On the *test* split, read once, it moves the sentence chrF++ from 44.90 to 47.13, the paper-level chrF from 33.05 to 45.76 and the paper-level finished rate from 72% to 94%. Adding the NLLB-3.3B teacher data (4.47 M tokens, 27 languages, non-commercial) did not beat A2W (its best variant is 0.36 chrF++ lower on science sentences) and was not adopted; adding the IbomMT *training* data brought no gain for Ibibio or Efik (RL-final 22.27 / 31.27 chrF++, the mixture without IbomMT data 22.07 / 30.79, the mixture with it 21.92 / 30.66 at $\alpha=0.5$ and 21.58 / 30.02 at $\alpha=0.7$). Note that the paper-level (single-pass whole-document) gains of this stage come from supervised long-document distillation, not from RL. This is the stage that makes native long output finish (Section 6.10).

{{table:t_sft_stage_v1|First science mixture: the guardrail test fails at every interpolation weight (`reports/final_run_integration_2026-10-21.md` section 3). Weak-blend: 19 languages from the weak-language dev sets.}}

{{table:t_sft_stage_v2|Second round of mixtures; four interpolation weights per cell. A2W at $\alpha=0.7$ is the only variant that passes the guardrail rule and has the best sentence chrF++ among those that pass.}}

{{figure:fig7_sft_stage_guardrail|Interpolation between RL-final ($\alpha=0$) and the science-stage model ($\alpha=1$) for five mixtures: gains on the AfriScience dev set (left, centre) against the loss on the weak-blend languages (right; dotted line: the guardrail).|100}}

### 6.7 The final run (final2)

*What was run and how it ended.* The final run (science and general prompts in the ratio 70:30, 77 languages, Section 4.7) started from the interpolated science-stage model A2W ($\alpha=0.7$) on 2026-10-22 at 13:00 UTC with 700 steps planned and **ended at step 401**. The project lead had fixed four stop criteria in advance, and at the evaluation of step 350 all four held: no gain of the evaluation reward of +0.03 or more, sentence-level chrF++ within $\pm 0.3$, the paper-level chrF++ (mean of the evaluations at steps 300 and 350) less than 1.0 above the reference value, and the weak-language dev chrF++ not up by 0.3; no forgetting criterion triggered. The wording of the criteria is not in a repository file (they are quoted from the project lead's instruction) and the reference point is not stated there. Measured from the evaluation of step 250, the start of the 100-step window, all four hold: the evaluation reward was -0.586 and -0.566 at steps 300 and 350 against -0.525 at step 250, sentence chrF++ 48.82 and 48.86 against 48.84 (48.79 at the start), paper-level chrF++ 45.36 on average against 45.24, and weak-language dev chrF++ 25.32 and 25.26 against 25.41. Measured from step 0, the paper-level criterion would not hold (45.36 against 44.02, +1.34; TODO(owner: confirm the reference point of the paper-level criterion)). A linear wind-down of the learning rate over 50 steps (steps 351-401; 3.75e-7 at step 351 to 7.5e-9 at step 401) was then started (written 2026-10-23 at 13:42 UTC), followed by the final evaluation at step 401 and the push of the weights.

*Interruptions and settings.* The step series of the final lineage is continuous from step 1 to 401 ({{fref:fig9_final2}} marks the events). (i) A planned restart after the step-150 checkpoint (the process was ended by SIGTERM at 21:22 UTC on 2026-10-22 and restarted twenty seconds later): the KL guard, which had cut the learning-rate scale to 0.5, 0.25 and 0.125 between steps 121 and 145, was reset (scale 1.0 at step 151, 0.5 again from step 155), and the padding ("stuffed output") detector stopped firing: it had marked 3.2-4.7% of the completions of every 25-step block of steps 1-150 as hard failures and marked none in steps 151-401, so the hard-failure share of the training batches fell from 0.056-0.061 to 0.013-0.017 (25-step blocks) and the mean training reward rose by about 0.2 at that step ({{fref:fig9_final2}}, top left). The configuration of the restart points to version 3 of the anchors (Section 7.3); we infer from this change in the metrics, not from a log line, that version 3 was in force from step 151 on and the first 150 steps ran with the earlier detector (TODO(owner: confirm)). The training reward is therefore not comparable across step 150 and is not a quality measure; the same holds for the evaluation reward before and after step 150. From step 182 the per-language draw weights of `LANG_WEIGHTS.json` (the dry-run proposals of the adaptive controller at step 150, applied by the project lead) were in force; the controller itself never changed a weight (dry run, 0 updates). (ii) Two out-of-memory kills (2026-10-23 at 04:22:56 and 04:49:17 UTC) because other jobs on the shared GPU had filled its memory; the run was resumed from the step-250 checkpoint each time with a smaller scoring micro-batch (24,576, then 12,288, then 6,144 tokens per forward), which does not change the optimisation, and steps 251-267 of the first attempt were discarded (the third process, which produced steps 251-401 including the wind-down, is the one reported). The operator-set learning-rate scale was 0.75 from step 251 (written 03:13 UTC; 0.5 before), the KL guard was reset at the restart (scale 1.0 from step 251, 0.5 again from step 341). The run is therefore not a single uninterrupted trajectory, and we have not checked how the sampling streams of the resumed steps relate to those of the discarded attempt.

*Time.* The 401 steps of the final lineage took 26.2 hours of step time (median 218 s per step; 197 s in steps 1-150, 205 s in 151-250, 283 s in 251-401, where the GPU was shared and the micro-batch smaller), 26.6 hours of wall-clock between the first and the last step of the lineage excluding the 112 minutes lost between the step-250 evaluation and the first resumed step, and 28.4 hours between the first and the last record of the metrics file (Appendix F). The last process, steps 251-401, ran 45,456 s (12.6 h).

*Result: the sentence-level gain over the start is small, the run mostly made the model stable and finishing.* {{tref:t_final2}} and {{fref:fig9_final2}} give the evaluations (every 50 steps, and at the last step). On the AfriScience dev sentences chrF++ is 48.92 at step 401 against 48.79 at the start (+0.13; BLEU 25.74 against 25.46; the nine evaluations after step 0 lie between 48.63 and 48.93 with a standard deviation of 0.10; the per-language changes are zul +0.43, amh +0.31, yor +0.06, lug -0.01, hau -0.02, nso -0.03). On the general dev set (19 languages, 760 sentences) it is 42.04 against 41.97 (+0.07; 13 of 19 languages up, largest Fon +1.12 and Igbo +0.77, smallest Swahili -1.23 and Zulu -0.88), on the weak-language dev set (13 languages, 60 sentences each) 25.28 against 24.90 (+0.38; 12 of 13 languages up, Mossi +1.08, Umbundu +0.64, Kabiye -0.21), but that series peaked at 25.55 at step 100 and drifted down by 0.27 afterwards, and with 60 sentences per language (noise about 3 chrF++ per language, Section 8.2) we do not consider a change of this size established. We computed no confidence intervals for these changes (the per-sentence outputs are not part of the metrics file); the spread of the evaluations above is the only noise estimate. At paper level (150 development papers, 25 in each of six languages of 542-1,346 source tokens, translated in a single pass) the finished rate went from 0.927 (139 of 150) to 1.000 within 50 steps, stayed at 0.993-1.000 until step 300 and was 0.967 (145 of 150) at steps 350 and 401; paper-level chrF++ went from 44.02 to 46.93 at step 50 and then 45.47, 45.27, 46.49, 45.24, 46.11, 44.61, 45.00 and 44.37 at step 401 (+0.35 at the end, standard deviation of the series 0.85). The evaluations at steps 400 and 401 are one optimiser step apart at a learning rate of 1.5e-8 and 7.5e-9 and differ by 0.63 chrF++ and by three documents in the finished count: that gives the order of magnitude of the evaluation noise at paper level, and the early gain (+2.9 at step 50) has not been sustained. Per language at paper level (end against start, one 25-document sample each) Luganda +1.91, Zulu +1.36 and Amharic +0.94 are up and Hausa -1.58 and Yoruba -0.56 down. The overall reading is that, as in phase 2c, RL moved nothing at sentence level beyond the noise of these sets (the +0.13 on the sentence dev set is slightly larger than the standard deviation of that series, 0.10, and we do not call it established), and that the paper-level finished rate, which RL had already moved to about 1.0 in the first 50 steps, ended slightly below its best. The main effects of the run are the ones that the earlier sections describe: the finish-rate and loop repair of the science-stage model and a stable optimisation with the amended reward. The benchmark of Section 6.10 shows that finishing is not the same as translating everything (the step-250 checkpoint covers 87% of the source sentences at about 1,024 tokens and 70% at about 2,048).

*Evaluation reward.* The mean reward on 192 held-out prompts was -0.783 at the first evaluation (step 25), -0.443 at step 175 (the best, *not* kept: only the latest weights were pushed, Section 13), -0.516 to -0.586 at steps 200-350 and -0.560 at step 401 (mean QE of the scored completions 0.544, finished rate 0.9375). The series is flat after step 200 within a standard deviation of 0.03, and the step-175 maximum follows the change of the padding detector at step 150, so that earlier values are not comparable. By length bin the final evaluation reward is +0.10 (b64), -0.21 (b256), -0.48 (b512), -0.62 (b1024) and -1.92 (b2048); the b2048 prompts stayed between -1.82 and -1.95 at all 17 evaluations, i.e. close to the hard-failure floor of -2: RL with this reward did not teach the 2,048-token windows, in line with Section 6.4. By domain the final reward is -0.86 on science and -0.15 on general prompts.

*Specific languages.* The Amharic sentence chrF++ of the AfriScience dev set is 25.59 at step 401 against 25.28 at the start and 25.65 at step 250, and Amharic at paper level 23.50 against 22.57 (24.71 at step 250): the small Amharic gain of the stage was already in place at step 250 and was not extended (Section 9.1). The Yoruba sentence dev score is flat (39.55 against 39.49), the Yoruba paper-level score lower (31.71 against 32.28, with all 25 papers finished against 20 at the start).

*Stability.* Over the 401 steps the KL to the start model had a median of 0.096 (10th and 90th percentile 0.049 and 0.152, maximum 0.267; 45 of 401 steps above the guard level of 0.15, 44 of them before step 351 and one in the wind-down) with the KL coefficient 0.1; the pre-clip gradient norm had a median of 0.71 and a maximum of 3.45 (64 steps above the clip at 1.0, 3 of them in the wind-down). The greedy member, with its advantage computed from the sampled members only, no longer dominates the gradient. The wind-down itself changed nothing visible: the mean KL of its steps (median 0.101) and the evaluations at steps 350, 400 and 401 are within the noise of the evaluations before it.

*Checkpoint.* The final weights (step 401) are in the private Hub repository `<HF_ORG_LEGACY>/gemma-science-translate` on the branch `latest`, on the branch `final2/step401` and under the tag `final2-step401` (commit 8bb1a029); the branch `main` of that repository still holds the SFT-stage models (for example A2W at $\alpha=0.7$), and no mid-run checkpoint was kept (Section 13). The checkpoint is the last step of the run, not one selected by the evaluations.

*Evaluation of the final checkpoint on the benchmarks (added after the run; CPU analysis of the outputs the evaluation scripts wrote, 2026-10-23).* The numbers above are all development sets. We then ran the final checkpoint through the same scripts, decoding (greedy, 768 new tokens, vLLM) and metric stack as the other checkpoints on FLORES+ devtest (45 languages), NTREX-128 (24), MAFAND-MT (15), SmolSent (45) and on the AfriScience-MT dev and test splits (6 languages, sentences and whole papers); the hypotheses of SFT-final and RL-final on the first four benchmarks are those of the pairwise comparison of Section 6.9. Confidence intervals are 95% percentile intervals of a paired bootstrap over sentences (1,000 resamples, the same resample for all systems; over papers for the paper level); they contain no language-sampling noise and no seed-to-seed variance (there is one run). AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET were first computed for the final checkpoint on FLORES+ only (Section 6.8); a later pass (agent R20, 2026-10-24, same scorers) added per-sentence AfriCOMET-QE, AfriCOMET with reference, SSA-COMET-QE and SSA-COMET with reference for NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT, SmolSent, SALT and GlotStoryBook, and the pairwise comparison with RL-final under the learned reference-based metrics (agent G1, 2026-10-24) is given below ({{tref:t_final2_pw}}).

*Sentence level, benchmarks of the general kind ({{tref:t_final2_bench}}, {{tref:t_final2_wtl}}).* On FLORES+ `no_pair` rows the final checkpoint reaches 34.31 chrF++ against 34.08 for RL-final (+0.23 [+0.19, +0.27] over the 45 languages; spBLEU +0.26 [+0.22, +0.31]), and by the same margin on the other three benchmarks (NTREX-128 +0.17 [+0.13, +0.21], MAFAND-MT +0.16 [+0.06, +0.24], SmolSent +0.20 [+0.15, +0.24]; on the `clean` rows the intervals include zero on FLORES+, +0.12 [-0.02, +0.26], and exclude it elsewhere). NTREX-128 and SmolSent are two of the six test sets on which the anchors of the final run were fitted (Section 6.9a), so those two differences are not independent tests of the final run; FLORES+ and MAFAND-MT are not among them (for FLORES+, the five languages with an NLLB reward signal were chosen with its devtest gaps). The gain is consistent in sign but more than 20 times smaller than the +5.4 of RL over SFT: it is the size of the difference between two checkpoints of the same plateau (Section 6.3). Sentence by sentence the final checkpoint wins 31-33% of the sentences, ties in 38-41% and loses 28-30% (chrF++, threshold 1 point), i.e. it is preferred in 52-53% of the non-tied sentences (FLORES+ 53.4% [52.8, 54.0], NTREX-128 52.1% [51.5, 52.7], MAFAND-MT 53.0% [52.0, 54.0], SmolSent 52.4% [51.8, 53.1]), against 63.7% for RL over SFT in Section 6.9. The learned metrics do **not** agree on the direction: on FLORES+ AfriCOMET is lower for the final checkpoint by 0.0016 [-0.0022, -0.0011] (42 languages; higher in 9 of the 42) and SSA-COMET by 0.0017 [-0.0026, -0.0007] (22 languages; higher in 6 of the 22), the paired intervals excluding zero but the differences amounting to a quarter of a percent of the scale ({{tref:t_final2_flores_paired}}, Section 6.8). The chrF++ gain is concentrated in the six languages of the science stage (amh, hau, lug, nso, yor, zul): +0.82 chrF++ on FLORES+ against +0.14 for the other 39 languages (point estimates of the per-language differences; the same split gives +0.73 against +0.12 on SmolSent, +0.42 against +0.10 on NTREX-128 and +0.31 against +0.08 on MAFAND-MT), the largest per-language gains on FLORES+ being Amharic +1.04, Northern Sotho +1.02 and Yoruba +0.98; three languages are clearly lower (Kabuverdianu -0.49, Luo -0.40, Dyula -0.22, {{tref:t_final2_perlang}}). Against the science-stage start A2W the final checkpoint is +0.15 [+0.12, +0.17] chrF++ (45 languages) and +0.0034 [+0.0030, +0.0038] AfriCOMET, against the step-250 checkpoint -0.05 [-0.08, -0.03] chrF++ and -0.0006 AfriCOMET: the 150 further steps of the run did not move the benchmark scores in any direction larger than 0.1 chrF++. Reading: **the final checkpoint is not worse than RL-final on these benchmarks and by chrF++ is marginally better (about +0.2), by the learned metrics marginally worse (about -0.002 AfriCOMET), and neither difference is large enough, nor measured with enough independent units (one run, no language-sampling noise), for us to call it a change in quality.** The honest summary is "level". Section 7.9 checks, on the same stored outputs, whether the extra reward terms of the final run show an effect on copying, entity loss, padding, repetition and whole documents: they show lower padding and repetition in the weak languages and no demonstrated protection elsewhere, and the run has no control.

{{table:t_final2_bench|The final checkpoint (final2, step 401) against RL-final and SFT-final on four benchmarks ($^\dagger$: the final run's anchors were fitted on this benchmark's test split, Section 6.9a, so the final2 $-$ RL-final difference there is not an independent test), chrF++ and spBLEU, macro over languages (corpus level per language), `no_pair` and `clean` rows, 95% paired-bootstrap intervals over sentences (language-sampling noise not included; "clearly" = the paired interval of that language excludes zero). Hypotheses: SFT-final and RL-final from the pairwise comparison of Section 6.9 (`/workspace/jobs/e1`), final2 from `/workspace/jobs/e2/out_final2` and `out_final2_other`; all rows, references and contamination flags identical in the three systems (152,309 rows). The bootstrap resamples here differ from those of {{tref:t_final2_flores_paired}} (E2 aggregation), so the intervals differ in the last digit. Source: `paper/data/final2_eval/`, `paper/scripts/box_p5_bench_compare.py`.}}

{{table:t_final2_wtl|Final2 against RL-final, sentence by sentence (chrF++ of the sentence, tie threshold 1 point, `no_pair` rows pooled over languages). Intervals: sentence bootstrap, 1,000 resamples. The preferences under the learned metrics are in {{tref:t_final2_pw}}.}}

*Learned reference-based metrics, sentence by sentence ({{tref:t_final2_pw}}).* To repeat the pairwise comparison of Section 6.9 for the final checkpoint we joined its per-sentence scores to those of RL-final by (benchmark, language, row id) and kept the `no_pair` rows on which both systems have the metric (AfriCOMET with reference: all `no_pair` rows of the AfriCOMET languages, from the pairwise comparison and its completion for NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT and SmolSent; SSA-COMET with reference: the sample of the pairwise comparison, at most 400 sentences per language plus the `clean` rows, 22 languages). AfriCOMET-QE, the reward model of RL-final, is not used. The two scoring pipelines agree: on the 14,339 rows where the two systems wrote identical text, the scores differ by 0.0002 on average (AfriCOMET with reference, largest difference 0.005; SSA-COMET with reference 0.00006, largest 0.004). Ties are differences below 1 chrF++ or 0.01, as in Section 6.9. On the three benchmarks pooled the learned metrics point the other way from chrF++: AfriCOMET with reference is lower for the final checkpoint by 0.0027 [-0.0032, -0.0023] (40 languages, 92,673 sentences; 17 languages clearly lower, 1 clearly higher) and SSA-COMET with reference by 0.0033 [-0.0041, -0.0025] (22 languages, 42,700 sentences; 11 clearly lower, 1 clearly higher), and per sentence the final checkpoint is preferred in 47.5% [47.1, 47.8] and 47.6% [47.1, 48.2] of the non-tied sentences, against 52.4% [52.0, 52.8] by chrF++ on the same benchmarks (100,982 sentences). The sign is the same on each benchmark (AfriCOMET with reference lower by 0.0047 on NTREX-128, 0.0030 on MAFAND-MT, 0.0024 on SmolSent, 0.0014 on the FLORES+ sample; SSA-COMET with reference lower by 0.0051, 0.0019, 0.0019 and 0.0025; the SSA-COMET preference on MAFAND-MT, 49.2% [48.1, 50.3], is the one interval that includes 50%). For comparison, the learned metrics prefer RL-final over SFT-final in about 70% of the non-tied sentences (Section 6.9): the difference between the final checkpoint and RL-final is about a quarter of a percent of the metric scale and runs in the opposite direction to chrF++. The chrF++ gain sits in the languages where the learned metric falls most: for the six science-stage languages the chrF++ difference is +0.37 and the AfriCOMET difference -0.0054 (point estimates, mean over languages), for the other languages +0.06 and -0.0023; Amharic is +0.55 [+0.39, +0.73] chrF++ in this pool but -0.0154 [-0.0188, -0.0119] AfriCOMET (SSA-COMET -0.0085 [-0.0121, -0.0045]) and Yoruba +0.55 [+0.34, +0.78] chrF++ but -0.0099 [-0.0123, -0.0076] AfriCOMET, while Luganda is clearly higher in both learned metrics (AfriCOMET +0.0036 [+0.0011, +0.0062], SSA-COMET +0.0064 [+0.0032, +0.0098]; chrF++ +0.10 [-0.17, +0.37]). Two cautions. The metrics disagree, so neither supports a statement that the final checkpoint translates better or worse: the honest summary stays "level", with surface overlap with the human references slightly higher and the learned metrics slightly lower. And the learned metrics are not held-out for the final run: its reward contained a QE ensemble of AfriCOMET-QE and SSA-COMET-QE (Section 4.7), whose reference-based counterparts are the metrics used here, so they are not independent evidence of quality for this checkpoint (as for RL-final, Section 6.9); moreover its anchors and long-document limits were fitted on the test splits of NTREX-128, SmolSent, SALT, GlotStoryBook, IbomMT, FLORES-MOZ, SmolDoc and AfriLongDoc core, so its benchmark results on those sets are not independent (Section 6.9a); chrF++ against the human references is independent of every learned model of the reward stack (though the consensus part of the reward is built from chrF++). All of this is agreement with human references under automatic metrics, not a human evaluation.

{{table:t_final2_pw|The final checkpoint (final2, step 401) against RL-final, sentence by sentence, with chrF++ and the learned reference-based metrics (`no_pair` rows; macro over languages with at least 20 sentences; benchmarks as in Section 6.9). Languages: those where both systems have the metric; sentences: pooled over those languages. Ties: difference below 1 chrF++ or below 0.01. "Preferred": wins over wins plus losses, sentences pooled, 95% paired bootstrap over sentences (1,000 resamples, language strata resampled independently). "Languages higher / clearly higher / clearly lower": point estimate above zero / interval of the language mean difference above / below zero. The sentence-level chrF++ here is the sentence mean (the corpus-level chrF++ of {{tref:t_final2_bench}} differs). SSA-COMET with reference covers a hash sample of the sentences of RL-final (at most 400 per language plus the `clean` rows). Source: `paper/data/final2_eval/g1_pairwise_final2_summary.csv`, `paper/scripts/box_g1_pairwise_final2.py`; scores of the final checkpoint from the private Hub dataset `<HF_ORG_LEGACY>/SabiYarn-MT-benchmark-translations` (R20), of RL-final from `/workspace/jobs/e1/neural` and `/workspace/jobs/t1/comet_extra`.}}

*AfriScience-MT ({{tref:t_final2_afriscience}}).* The final run was trained on the AfriScience-MT training split and its checkpoint-selection rule uses the dev split, so the **test** columns are the clean ones (the split was not used for any decision; it had been read once for A2W and RL-final in Section 6.6, and it is read here for the final checkpoint). Against RL-final the final checkpoint is +2.40 [+2.25, +2.56] sentence chrF++ on test (all six languages up; dev +2.46 [+2.29, +2.62]) and +13.0 [+11.4, +14.4] chrF at paper level in one pass (test 46.05 against 33.05, 28 papers per language; the finished rate 98.2% against 72.0%). Against the science-stage start A2W, which it continued with 401 RL steps, the sentence-level gain is +0.17 [+0.07, +0.27] on test (+0.15 [+0.03, +0.27] on dev; three of six languages clearly up on test, none clearly down), and at paper level the change is **inside the noise** (test chrF +0.28 [-1.18, +1.73], chrF++ +0.12 [-1.32, +1.50]; dev +1.44 [+0.04, +2.88] chrF, which only just excludes zero and is a development set); the finished rate is 98.2% against 94.0% (+4.2 points [0.0, 8.3]). The gains over RL-final therefore come from the science-stage supervised data, and the final run adds at most the small sentence-level gain and a few more finished papers. A caveat on the comparison: the final2 outputs come from `eval_afriscience.py` and those of A2W and RL-final from the stage-evaluation script of Section 6.6; both are vLLM greedy decodes, and the two scripts give RL-final the same dev score and 44.86 against 44.90 on test (0.04 chrF++), which is the size of the pipeline noise between them. The papers translated by the final checkpoint are shorter than their references (median character ratio 0.84, and 45% of the papers below 0.8, against 0.84 and 42% for A2W; RL-final 0.92 and 30% with 37% above 1.25, i.e. loops), so the paper-level result should be read as "finishes and writes the language, and omits as much as A2W", not as a repair of the omissions of Section 6.10 (the 2,048-token benchmark of Section 6.10 was **not** run for the final checkpoint). The paper-level numbers are for papers of about 900 tokens, not the 1,000-2,000-token documents of the benchmark.

RL-final and A2W: the stage evaluation of Section 6.6 (their 44.90 / 47.13 / 45.76 / 94% are reproduced exactly); final2: `/workspace/jobs/a4/eval_final2_step401`. Differences: paired bootstrap over sentences (sentence level) or papers (paper level), 1,000 resamples; finished-rate differences in percentage points. Dev: 843 sentences and 25 papers per language; test: 970 sentences and 28 papers per language. Source: `paper/data/final2_eval/afs_macro.csv`, `afs_paired.csv`, `paper/scripts/box_p5_afs_compare.py`.}}

{{table:t_final2_afriscience_pub|AfriScience-MT test, chrF (without word n-grams; the metric of the published tables) per language: our checkpoints next to the numbers printed by the dataset paper (copied by the evaluation script from `reports/afriscience_science_run_2026-10-21/published_chrf_test_eng2x.csv`; the published rows are fine-tuned or few-shot systems of 0.6-9B parameters or API models, and their Yoruba values for the NLLB and M2M models (13-18) are far below the Gemma2 value (42) in the same table, which suggests a diacritic-handling effect that we did not investigate; macros including Yoruba should not be read as quality differences). Not the same decoding or prompts, no paired intervals.}}

With the numbers published with the dataset [@abdulmumin2026afriscience] ({{tref:t_final2_afriscience_pub}}) the final checkpoint's 50.2 sentence chrF on test is level with the published fine-tuned Gemma2-9B LoRA (50.3), above Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite 10-shot (48.8), below the fine-tuned NLLB models (51.7-52.3, with the Yoruba caveat of the caption: without Yoruba their macros are 58.5 and 59.2 against 52.2 for ours, our computation from the table) and below GPT-5.4 10-shot (53.3); at paper level 46.1 is below GPT-5.4 1-shot (55.2) and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite 1-shot (49.4) and far above TranslateGemma-12B and Gemma2-9B-IT 1-shot (21.8, 11.4). These are comparisons with the authors' numbers on a split that our model trained on, and were produced by different pipelines; they are context, not evidence of ranking.

*Which checkpoint should the paper present as its headline model?* We keep RL-final (phase 2c, step 1,433) as the headline, and **recommend** that decision to the project lead (TODO(owner: decide the headline model; evidence in this subsection and in `paper/TODO.md`)). The evidence: (i) On the general benchmarks the final checkpoint is level with RL-final (about +0.2 chrF++, about -0.002 AfriCOMET on FLORES+ and -0.003 AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET with reference on the three other benchmarks, preferred in 52% of the non-tied sentences by chrF++ and in 47-48% by the learned reference-based metrics; +0.15 chrF++ / +0.003 AfriCOMET over A2W): there is no benchmark on which it is clearly better in a way that matters. (ii) On the AfriScience test split it is clearly better than RL-final (+2.4 sentence chrF++, +13 paper-level chrF, finished 98% against 72%), but not clearly better than A2W (+0.17 sentence chrF++, paper level inside the noise): that gain is the science-stage SFT, not RL. (iii) In the long-document benchmark of Section 6.10 the final checkpoint behaves like the step-250 checkpoint and like A2W: it finishes 91-98% of the documents of about 1,000-2,000 tokens in one pass and writes the language natively (chrF++ 39.6 and 27.7 at about 1,024 and 2,048 tokens, against 39.6 and 28.2 for the step-250 checkpoint), while omitting about 12% and 27% of the sentences, and it loops more often than the step-250 checkpoint at about 2,048 tokens (loop rate 0.25 against 0.10); its paper-level behaviour at about 900 tokens is statistically indistinguishable from that of A2W. The 401 steps of the final run did not improve long-input translation. (In the independent judge of Section 6.12 its whole-document units are 10.6 points [7.6, 13.9] above RL-final's, but at 25.8 of 100 far below its own sentence-by-sentence output, 67.0.) (iv) Every analysis of the paper that concerns what RL did (Sections 6.1-6.5, 6.9, 7 and the ablation of 8.4) is about RL-final or its SFT start; replacing the headline by a model that also contains a science-stage SFT mixture and 401 further RL steps would confound those claims, and the two models differ in training data (the final run uses the NLLB-derived pseudo-reference signal for five languages and the AfriScience training split, Sections 4.7 and 12). (v) If the paper's purpose is to offer the best translator, the final checkpoint is at least as good as RL-final everywhere we measured, markedly better on science text, and finishes papers; it would then be presented as a second, named model ("RL-final: general; final2: science-adapted"), not as a replacement. We do not claim that the final checkpoint is better than RL-final on general text.

Dev sets: AfriScience-MT dev sentences (6 languages x 200, "AfriScience dev sentence"), the same 150 dev papers translated in one pass ("paper-level"), general dev (19 languages, 760 sentences), weak-language dev (13 languages, 60 sentences each). Evaluation reward: 192 held-out prompts (not evaluated at step 0). The wind-down covers steps 351-401.}}

Grey dashed line: restart at step 150 (KL guard reset, padding detector no longer firing); red dashed line: the step-250 checkpoint from which the run was resumed after two OOM kills on 2026-10-23; green band: learning-rate wind-down, steps 351-401. Re-generated by `paper/scripts/make_figures.py fig9` from `paper/data/final2_metrics.jsonl` (refresh with `paper/scripts/refresh_final2.sh`).|100}}

### 6.8 Comparison with other systems on identical sentences

Section 6.2 compared our model with the tables printed in other papers. That comparison cannot include a learned African metric, because **AfriCOMET is not published for almost any of the baseline systems**. We audited the 43 baseline models of our published-baseline file (full matrix in Appendix G, {{tref:t_model_comparison_comet_audit}}): an AfriCOMET score appears in a paper for exactly two of them, NLLB-200-distilled-600M and its pruned variant AfriNLLB, and there with an older checkpoint (`africomet-mtl` v1.0, 9 FLORES-200 languages, Wolof outside the encoder's languages) than the one we use (`africomet-stl-1.1`) [@moslem2026afrinllb]. What the recent African-MT papers report is SSA-COMET [@li2025ssacomet], a different metric: for 34 of the 43 models (25 via TranslatePsy-AfriSLM [@gritta2026translatepsy], 19 languages with both directions averaged as far as we can tell; 9 more only via AfriqueLLM's English-to-X table [@yu2026afriquellm], 24 languages, checkpoint not named). For NLLB-200-distilled-1.3B, the 54.5B mixture-of-experts model, Aya-101, GPT-4o, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Gemma 2 27B and Llama 3.1 70B we found no COMET-family number at all. A joint chrF++ and AfriCOMET table over the baselines is therefore impossible from the literature, and we built one from our own scoring.

*Protocol.* All systems we could run were scored with one metric stack on the *same* FLORES+ devtest English-to-X sentences, restricted to the `no_pair` rows (the sentences whose reference pair our SFT never saw; 170 to 1,012 per language; the `clean` rows, 48 per language whose English side is not in the SFT text, are reported in the appendix tables): chrF++ (sacreBLEU, word order 2, NFC), spBLEU, reference-based AfriCOMET-STL-1.1 and, for 22 languages, reference-based SSA-COMET-MTL. The systems are our five checkpoints (RL-final; SFT-final; A2W, the start of the final run; the step-250 checkpoint of the final run; and the final checkpoint of that run, step 401, added after the run ended), NLLB-200-distilled-600M and NLLB-200-3.3B (the CTranslate2 beam-4 translations of Section 7.5, re-scored), and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B (all 19 of its languages) and 2B (only 9 of them: the run was stopped when the GPU budget was cut). The 2B model's weights alone, like those of TranslatePsy-4B, AfriqueGemma-4B and Gemma-3-4B-it, did not fit the 6 GB left to us on the shared GPU; those models are *not* in the computed rows, and their published numbers stay in a separate column. Intervals are 95% percentile intervals over sentences (1,000 resamples, one resample shared by all systems, hence paired); they do **not** contain language-sampling noise, and a macro is given only for systems that cover every language of the set. As a check of the stack, our chrF++ for NLLB-200-3.3B on the whole devtest differs from the published value by -0.17 on average over 43 languages (mean absolute difference 0.40; the largest is Yoruba, +3.02, caused by Unicode normalisation: 28% of the Yoruba references are in decomposed form).

*Headline comparison: 42 languages without Amharic and Tigrinya, with the two reported alongside.* Amharic and Tigrinya, the only Ge'ez-script languages in our set, are, together with Kabuverdianu (a creole, gap 21.0, which we keep in), the largest single gaps to NLLB-200 3.3B (Amharic 19.0 and Tigrinya 11.0 chrF++; RL-final 19.0 and 13.8, NLLB-200 3.3B 38.0 and 24.7, NLLB-200-distilled-600M 37.3 and 22.4). We therefore take the 42 other languages as the headline comparison with NLLB, because the two Ge'ez-script languages are an outlier whose cause we have not tested, not because they are unimportant; they are in the same table ({{tref:t_noGeez_flores}}), next to the macro over all 44 languages, and the all-language number is the one to quote when no language may be left out. On the 44 languages for which RL-final, the final run, NLLB-200 3.3B and NLLB-200-distilled-600M all have a `no_pair` row (the 43 languages below plus Kanuri, for which our NLLB re-run exists but no published NLLB number does), the macro chrF++ is 34.25, 34.48, 38.09 and 35.94; without the two Ge'ez-script languages (42 languages) it is 35.10, 35.32, 38.42 and 36.23. The gap of RL-final to NLLB-200 3.3B is **3.32 chrF++ on the 42 languages (3.85 on all 44)** and the gap to the 600M model **1.13 (1.69)**; the medians of the per-language gaps are 2.78 (3.17) and 0.51 (0.62), because a few languages carry the mean (next paragraph). On AfriCOMET (the 40 non-Ge'ez languages that have a score) RL-final reaches 0.624 against 0.667 for the 3.3B model and 0.651 for the 600M model, gaps of 0.043 and 0.028 (0.050 and 0.035 with the two Ge'ez-script languages, 42 languages; Amharic 0.490 against 0.719 and 0.712, Tigrinya 0.588 against 0.727 and 0.709). The mean gaps are the differences of the macros in the CSV (the macro of the per-language differences); they are point values without an interval, and {{tref:t_noGeez_flores}} shows the median gap beside each mean. Excluding the two languages narrows the gap but does not remove it: RL-final is below NLLB-200 3.3B in 34 of the 42 non-Ge'ez languages on chrF++ and in 36 of the 40 on AfriCOMET. This says where the gap lies, not why it is there. Four candidate causes of the deficit in Ge'ez script (and perhaps in similar languages) are open, and we tested none of them: capacity sharing of 270M parameters across 77 languages, tokenisation (only 862 of the 262,144 vocabulary entries contain Ethiopic characters, Section 9.1; 3.03 and 3.46 Gemma tokens per word for Amharic and Tigrinya against 2.09 for Swahili), the amount and quality of Amharic and Tigrinya training data, and Gemma's own pretraining coverage of the two languages (Section 10). NLLB-200 is also larger, was trained on far more data and on many more language pairs, so a comparison with NLLB cannot separate these causes; a matched specialist-model or larger-model experiment is future work (Section 11).

*How skewed is the gap?* The mean gap is carried by a few languages, and we say so wherever it is quoted. Per language (RL-final minus NLLB-200 3.3B, chrF++, point values over the 44 languages), the mean gap is 3.85 and the **median 3.17** (3.32 and 2.78 over the 42 without Amharic and Tigrinya; against the 600M model the mean is 1.69 and the median 0.62). The six largest gaps are Kabuverdianu 21.0, Amharic 19.0, Tigrinya 11.0, Kanuri 9.6, Nuer 7.9 and Kirundi 7.5 ({{tref:t_skew_langs}}); the three largest, Kabuverdianu, Amharic and Tigrinya, carry **30%** of the summed gap (the six carry 45%), and without those three the mean is 2.89 and the median 2.48 (41 languages, {{tref:t_skew_summary}}). Stated separately, the Ge'ez-script languages Amharic and Tigrinya carry 17.7% of the summed gap (mean 15.0 over the two) and Kabuverdianu alone 12.4% (21.0). Kabuverdianu stays in every set, the headline included, because it cannot be discarded: it is also the largest gain of RL-final over SFT-final (+14.2, from 12.6 to 26.8 chrF++) and, in the same pipeline, the largest remaining gap to the 3.3B model (NLLB scores 47.8, we 26.8); both facts are shown in {{tref:t_skew_langs}}. At the other end, RL-final is at or above NLLB-200 3.3B on chrF++ in 8 of the 44 languages (point estimates: Dyula +2.9, Umbundu +2.4, Kamba +2.0, Wolof +1.9, Kimbundu +1.9, Fon +1.4, Yoruba +1.0, Igbo +0.7). The stored files hold a 95% interval for each system and language but no per-sentence NLLB scores, so we give no paired per-language interval; the two unpaired intervals do not overlap for six of the eight (not Yoruba and Igbo). This is the chrF++ count; on AfriCOMET (a different metric, 42 languages scored) RL-final is above the 3.3B model in 4 of 42 (Fon, Igbo, Wolof, Yoruba), the count quoted in the paragraph below, and the two statements are about different metrics and language sets, not in conflict. The gaps to the 3.3B model are thus a small number of large deficits (Ge'ez script and a few others, including a creole) on top of a modest deficit in most languages, which is why we give the median beside the mean and read the 42-language mean as a headline, not as a typical language.

{{table:t_skew_summary|Skew of the gap. FLORES+ devtest (`no_pair` rows), chrF++, the 44 languages with a row for RL-final, SFT-final and NLLB-200 3.3B. Gap = NLLB-3.3B minus RL-final per language (negative: RL-final ahead); share = the set's part of the summed per-language gap (169.3 chrF++ points over the 44, i.e. a mean of 3.85); gain = RL-final minus SFT-final (mean +5.35, median +4.38 over the 44). Point values; no per-sentence NLLB scores are stored, so no paired interval. Source: `reports/data/model_comparison_master.csv`, computed by `paper/scripts/make_noGeez.py` (`skew`), values in `paper/data/noGeez_summary.json`.}}

{{table:t_skew_langs|The languages behind the means (`no_pair` rows, chrF++, point values). Top block: the eight largest gains of RL-final over SFT-final; middle: the other languages among the six largest gaps to NLLB-3.3B (Kabuverdianu, Kanuri and Nuer are in the top block and are also among the six largest gaps); bottom: the other languages where RL-final is at or above NLLB-3.3B. Gap = NLLB-3.3B minus RL-final (negative: RL-final ahead). SFT finished: share of the SFT-final translations that end within 768 tokens (RL-final: 100% in all of them; part of the gain of Kabiye, Nuer and Kabuverdianu is repaired unfinished output; Kanuri has no SFT data and its gain is a stopping effect). Last column: whether the stored unpaired 95% intervals of RL-final and NLLB-3.3B overlap ("clear": they do not). Source: `model_comparison_master.csv`, `model_comparison_computed/per_language_with_final2.csv`, `final_model_per_language.csv` (finished rates).}}

*The 43-language set of the earlier tables.* {{tref:t_cmp_main}} and {{fref:fig12_model_size}} give the comparison on the sets used before the headline above, which include Amharic and Tigrinya. On the 43 FLORES+ languages that NLLB-200 covers, RL-final reaches 34.7 chrF++ [34.6, 34.8] against 36.4 for NLLB-200-distilled-600M and 38.4 for NLLB-200-3.3B; on AfriCOMET (the 42 languages on which it can be computed) 0.620 against 0.654 and 0.669. **Our 270M model does not beat NLLB.** Its deficit to the 600M model, which has 2.3 times as many parameters, is 1.7 chrF++ [1.6, 1.8] and 0.035 AfriCOMET, and to the 3.3B model 3.7 [3.6, 3.8] and 0.050 (paired differences, Appendix G); on the `clean` rows the gaps are 0.7 and 3.0 chrF++ (RL-final 32.3, 600M 33.0, 3.3B 35.3 over the same 43 languages, intervals of about plus or minus 0.4), so the ordering does not depend on contamination (3.16 and 0.70 chrF++ over the 44 languages of the headline set, Section 6.9a). RL-final scores higher than NLLB-200-distilled-600M in 17 of the 44 languages on chrF++ (Afrikaans, Dyula, Fon, Igbo, Kimbundu, Kanuri, Luganda, Mossi, Oromo, Sango, Southern Sotho, Swahili, Tumbuka, Twi, Umbundu, Wolof, Yoruba) and in 6 of the 42 on AfriCOMET (Fon, Igbo, Mossi, Twi, Wolof, Yoruba); it is higher than NLLB-200-3.3B in 8 of 44 (4 of 42 on AfriCOMET: Fon, Igbo, Wolof, Yoruba). Counting only differences whose 95% intervals do not overlap, it wins 7 and loses 19 languages against the 600M model and wins 6 and loses 30 against the 3.3B model ({{tref:t_cmp_wins}}, per-language values in Appendix G). The largest deficits are the languages that Section 7.5 already identified: Amharic, Tigrinya, Kabuverdianu, Kanuri against 3.3B, Nuer and Kabiye.

Gap = NLLB system minus RL-final: mean (macro of per-language differences) and median over the languages of the column, point values, no intervals; the last column is Kabuverdianu alone (the largest single gap to NLLB-200 3.3B, in every set). AfriCOMET scores 42 of the 44 languages (not Kabuverdianu and Kanuri), 40 without Amharic and Tigrinya; each single-language column is one language. Amharic and Tigrinya are excluded from the headline because they are an outlier (with Kabuverdianu, the largest single gaps to NLLB-200 3.3B) with an untested cause, not because they are unimportant. Source: `paper/scripts/make_model_comparison.py`.|100}}

*Other small and large models.* TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B, which is trained on translations by NLLB-200-3.3B for 19 languages, is clearly better than our model on those languages: chrF++ 45.3 against 41.2 (paired difference +4.1 [+3.9, +4.2]; RL-final is higher in none of the 19 languages on chrF++ and clearly lower, with non-overlapping intervals, in 14; on AfriCOMET it is higher only in Yoruba), AfriCOMET 0.717 against 0.657 (+0.060), and also above NLLB-200-3.3B on AfriCOMET (0.701). The 2B model scores 46.4 against 41.3 for RL-final on the 9 languages it was run for (AfriCOMET 0.716 against 0.638). On the 21 languages of the AfriqueLLM study (4-14B models adapted to African languages), RL-final reaches 39.1 chrF++ on the `no_pair` rows, against published 31.1-38.6 for the adapted Gemma, Llama and Qwen3 models (AfriqueGemma-4B 31.1, AfriqueQwen-4B 33.1, AfriqueQwen-8B 35.1, AfriqueQwen3.5-4B 36.6, AfriqueLlama-8B and AfriqueQwen-14B 36.7, AfriqueGemma-12B 38.6), 9.9-27.4 for the base models, and 41.7 for the 50-language Qwen3.5-4B variant, which is above us. This is **indicative only**: the published values are for the whole devtest, with 5-shot prompts through a harness whose split we could not confirm, and our model saw the English side of most of those sentences (a comparison on `clean` rows is not possible and the earlier one was not like for like, Section 6.2; we claim neither superiority nor inferiority). On SSA-COMET our own re-scoring gives RL-final 52.1 (21 languages), NLLB-200-600M 56.7 and NLLB-200-3.3B 59.3, against the papers' 51.3-62.9 for the adapted Afrique models; since the Afrique paper does not name its SSA-COMET checkpoint, the two columns are not interchangeable.

*What RL and the later stages add on identical sentences.* RL adds +5.2 chrF++ [+5.1, +5.3] and +0.061 AfriCOMET [+0.060, +0.062] over SFT-final (43 and 42 languages respectively; the 5.4 of Section 6.1 is over all 45). The science-stage start (A2W) and the step-250 checkpoint of the final run lie within +0.1 to +0.4 chrF++ above RL-final (paired intervals exclude zero, e.g. +0.28 [+0.24, +0.32] for step 250 on the 43 languages) and within -0.001 to -0.005 AfriCOMET: they have not left the plateau of Section 6.3. The final checkpoint (step 401) is +0.23 chrF++ [+0.19, +0.27] above RL-final on the 43 languages and on the 45 (+0.23 [+0.19, +0.26]), +0.27 spBLEU [+0.23, +0.32], and **below** it on both learned metrics: -0.0016 AfriCOMET [-0.0022, -0.0011] over the 42 languages and -0.0017 SSA-COMET [-0.0026, -0.0007] over the 22 that can be scored; against A2W it is +0.12 chrF++ [+0.09, +0.15] and +0.0034 AfriCOMET [+0.0030, +0.0038], against the step-250 checkpoint -0.05 [-0.08, -0.02] and -0.0006 ({{tref:t_final2_flores_macro}}, {{tref:t_final2_flores_paired}}; on the `clean` rows all intervals against RL-final include zero). The final run therefore also stayed on the plateau: its differences from RL-final have opposite signs under chrF++ and under the learned metrics and are, in absolute terms, 0.7% of the chrF++ and 0.3% of the AfriCOMET value (Section 6.7 for the other benchmarks and AfriScience-MT).

{{table:t_final2_flores_macro|The five checkpoints of ours on identical FLORES+ devtest sentences (`no_pair` rows): macro with 95% sentence-bootstrap intervals. The last row is the final run (final2, step 401). Source: `reports/data/model_comparison_computed/macro_with_final2.csv`.}}

{{table:t_final2_flores_paired|Paired differences of the final checkpoint (final2, step 401) to each of the other checkpoints, same sentences and metrics; 95% sentence-bootstrap intervals (no language-sampling noise). Source: `reports/data/model_comparison_computed/paired_vs_with_final2.csv`.}}

*Language coverage of AfriCOMET.* AfriCOMET could score 42 of our 45 FLORES+ languages (not Kabuverdianu, Kanuri and Makhuwa), and only 54 of our 77 languages are among the 75 codes its model card lists (the card lists the language set of the underlying encoder; the metric itself was trained on WMT data without African labels, and the human-annotated AfriMTE pairs cover 13 pairs and 11 African languages, 9 of them ours). For languages outside the card every AfriCOMET cell in our tables is "n/a", never imputed. This is the reason the RL reward needed a consensus signal for 23 languages (Section 4.4): no learned African metric covers them.

*Language coverage as a claim.* We support 77 African target languages in a 270M-parameter model; 44 of them are in FLORES-200 and 33 are not. A survey of 35 systems (Appendix G, {{tref:t_coverage_claim}}, {{fref:fig13_language_coverage}}) does not support the claim that this breadth is unique: NLLB-200 supports 55 African languages [@nllb2022nllb]; MADLAD-400 lists about 85 African entries among its 419 languages [@kudugunta2023madlad]; TranslateGemma about 60 in its training-language tables [@finkelstein2026translategemma]; and Omnilingual MT (Meta, 2026, arXiv 2603.16309, not verified by us) claims over 1,600 source languages. The MADLAD-400 and TranslateGemma counts are *our manual tallies of the papers' tables and are unverified* (plus or minus 5 for MADLAD-400, which includes variants; Zulu is missing from the extracted TranslateGemma tables although the model is evaluated on it). We therefore make only three narrower statements, each with its condition. (W1) To our knowledge, the open translation model with the widest African target-language coverage *below 1 B parameters*: 77 languages against 55 for NLLB-200-600M; the survey cannot exclude unlisted community fine-tunes. This is a coverage statement, not a quality statement: on quality our model is below NLLB-200-distilled-600M. (W2) To our knowledge the first open 270M-parameter model fine-tuned with reinforcement learning against an African-language quality estimator, on prompts of up to 2,048 source tokens (Section 2; the literature search is recorded in `paper/lit/notes_*.md`; the clause on 2,048-token prompts concerns training, not document-translation quality, Section 6.10); the clauses on the open release and the reward are facts about our model, not outputs of the survey TODO(owner: confirm that the weights will be released openly, see Section 13, before the word open is used in W1 and W2). (W3) The model covers 33 African languages that are absent from FLORES-200, 17 of which (Urhobo, Edo, Dagbani, Okpe, Tachelhit, Ghomala, Kaonde, Lunda, Lozi, Makhuwa, Nyankore, Herero, Yao, Sidamo, Esan, Duala, Cameroon Pidgin) we could not find in the language lists of NLLB-200, MADLAD-400 or the TranslateGemma tables; the match is on exact language, and Google Translate, Gemini, Omnilingual MT and Apertus were not checked language by language. We do not claim to be the first or the only system with this breadth.

{{figure:fig13_language_coverage|African languages supported (as claimed or trained) against parameter count for the systems of the coverage survey. Filled: the system has per-language English-to-X results; open: claimed, no per-language results; crosses: no African target. The MADLAD-400 and TranslateGemma counts are unverified tallies. Source: `reports/data/language_coverage_claim_check.csv`, `paper/scripts/make_model_comparison.py`.|55}}

*Limits of this comparison.* (i) NLLB is run sentence by sentence with beam 4 (CTranslate2, int8 for the 3.3B model), ours greedy; beam 4 gave our own model +0.66 chrF++ on 20 languages in Section 6.5, so part of the gap may be decoding, but we did not re-run either side with the other's decoding. (ii) Our SFT data contain the English side of most FLORES+ sentences and, for seven languages, the reference translation (Section 6.9a); `no_pair` and `clean` control for the pair leak and the English-side exposure, and the NLLB and TranslatePsy models were not exposed to our SFT data; we did not check their own training data for overlap with FLORES+ (NLLB-200 used FLORES as its development set [@nllb2022nllb]; for TranslatePsy we do not know), so the bias of the comparison in either direction is unknown. (iii) AfriCOMET is a learned metric of limited validity for several of our languages (Section 7.1) and is blind to 23 of the 77; chrF++ and AfriCOMET agree here on the ordering of the systems, which is no proof that either is right. (iv) One checkpoint per system, one decoding setting, no language-sampling noise in the intervals. (v) We did not run the Gemma-3-4B-it, AfriqueGemma-4B and TranslatePsy-4B models.

### 6.9 Benchmark-based pairwise comparison of SFT-final and RL-final (stand-in for a human evaluation)

We have no native-speaker evaluation of our outputs (Section 11). To test the claim that RL *improved* the model with something other than a macro average of one metric, we compare RL-final with the SFT-final model it started from **sentence by sentence** on the human-reference benchmarks, under five metrics, and ask how often each metric prefers the RL translation. This measures agreement with human references under automatic metrics; it is not a human evaluation, and we word every result as "preferred by metric X in Y% of sentences". The analysis (agent E1; `reports/pairwise_benchmark_comparison_2026-10-23.md`; scripts `gemma_mt/scripts/pairwise_*.py`) uses the hypotheses of both models for all 161,272 English-to-X sentences of the eight benchmarks of Section 6.1 (66 languages with at least 20 `no_pair` sentences; greedy decoding, 768 tokens; the run reproduces the corpus-level chrF++ of Section 6.1 within 0.01-0.2 per benchmark).

*Method.* For every sentence and metric the RL output *wins* if its score exceeds the SFT output's by more than a threshold (1 chrF++ point; 0.01 for the COMET-family metrics, whose scale is about 0-1), *loses* if it is lower by more than the threshold, and otherwise ties; identical outputs are ties. "RL preferred" is wins divided by wins plus losses. The metrics are sentence-level chrF++ and the learned metrics AfriCOMET-STL-1.1 and SSA-COMET-MTL with the human reference, plus the reference-free QE variants (AfriCOMET-QE, SSA-COMET-MTL without reference). The learned metrics were run on the 51 languages that AfriCOMET covers and have at least 20 `no_pair` sentences, on every `clean` row plus at most 400 rows per (benchmark, language): 79,062 sentence pairs per metric. Intervals are paired sentence-bootstrap intervals (1,000 resamples); language-level statements use the bootstrap interval of each language's mean difference; the sign tests of the report are optimistic because several benchmarks translate the same English sentences into every language, and we do not use them.

*Which metrics are independent of the reward.* RL-final (phase 2c, the model of Sections 6.1-6.6) was trained with AfriCOMET-QE as the only learned metric: the `rl2c` configuration names `masakhane/africomet-qe-stl-1.1` as its sole scorer, and the ensemble that adds SSA-COMET-MTL (decision D-55, 2026-10-21) exists only in the final run and is off by default, so SSA-COMET is **not** in the reward of RL-final or of any phase before it. Consequently chrF++ against human references and SSA-COMET with reference are independent evidence for the RL-final comparison; AfriCOMET with reference shares its model family (encoder and training data) with the reward model and is not independent in the strict sense, and the QE metrics are the reward model itself or its sibling. The AfriCOMET-QE reward model also appears in the table, as a check on reward hacking.

**Headline** ({{tref:t_pw_headline}}, {{fref:figpw1_wtl}}). On the `no_pair` rows (reference pair not in the SFT text), macro over languages with all benchmarks pooled per language, the mean sentence chrF++ rises from 27.7 to 31.1 (+3.32 [+3.26, +3.38]); RL wins in 54.8% of the sentences, ties in 20.0% and loses in 25.1%, so it is preferred by chrF++ in 63.7% [63.5, 64.0] of the non-tied sentences. With the human references as the anchor, AfriCOMET prefers RL in 70.0% [69.7, 70.4] of the non-tied sentences, SSA-COMET in 70.4% [70.1, 70.7], and the majority of the three reference-based metrics in 70.2% [69.8, 70.5]; the two reference-free metrics give 72.9% (the reward model) and 70.8%. On the `clean` rows, whose English side the SFT never saw, the shares are 61.6% (chrF++), 69.0%, 69.6% and 68.9% (majority), i.e. 0.6-2.1 points lower than on `no_pair`, and the macro chrF++ gain is +3.86 [+3.72, +4.00]. The language-level result does not rely on sentence independence: RL has a bootstrap interval of the mean chrF++ difference above zero in 65 of the 66 languages (the exception is Southern Sotho, +0.29 [-0.01, +0.62]) and below zero in none, and on all four learned metrics in all 51 languages they cover ({{fref:figpw2_languages}}, Appendix H). At the level of (benchmark, language) cells with at least 20 sentences, chrF++ is significantly better for RL in 128 of 145 cells, neutral in 16 and significantly worse in one (SALT Luganda, 81 sentences, -2.98 [-5.84, -0.47], where the COMET metrics are flat).

{{table:t_pw_headline|SFT-final against RL-final, sentence by sentence (FLORES+, NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT, SmolSent, IbomMT, FLORES-MOZ, SALT, GlotStoryBook pooled per language, `no_pair` rows, macro over languages). The neural metrics cover the 51 AfriCOMET-covered languages; the second chrF++ row restricts to the sentences they scored. Ties: difference below 1 chrF++ or 0.01. "Preferred": share of non-tied sentences (micro) with paired-bootstrap interval; "languages clearly better / worse": interval of the language mean above / below zero. Source: `reports/data/pairwise_benchmark_comparison/main/macro.csv`.}}

{{figure:figpw1_wtl|(A) Macro win, tie and loss shares of RL-final over SFT-final per metric (`no_pair`). (B) Share of non-tied sentences in which RL is preferred, per benchmark (sentences pooled). (C) The same per reward group (chrF++, AfriCOMET with reference, AfriCOMET-QE; consensus-only languages have no learned metric). Source: `paper/scripts` of agent E1, `gemma_mt/scripts/pairwise_fig_wtl.py`.|100}}

*Every group, benchmark and tier.* The preference is above 50% in every benchmark, reward group, language family and resource tier on every metric ({{tref:t_pw_bench}}, {{tref:t_pw_groups}}; the lowest share is 52% by chrF++ for the Indo-European and Austronesian languages, Afrikaans and Malagasy). It is largest for the weakest languages (weak tier: +4.7 chrF++, preferred 71% by chrF++ and 77% by AfriCOMET with reference; the 13 consensus-only languages, for which no learned metric exists, 75% by chrF++), and smallest on the benchmarks nearest to the training domain (MAFAND-MT +1.9 chrF++, 57%; SALT +1.5, 59%) and for the languages that SFT already handled best (Malagasy +0.29, Southern Sotho +0.29, Luganda +0.41).

{{table:t_pw_bench|By benchmark (`no_pair` rows): macro difference (RL minus SFT) and the share of non-tied sentences in which RL is preferred, per metric. The neural metrics cover only the AfriCOMET-covered languages (IbomMT: one language).}}

{{table:t_pw_groups|By reward group, language family, script and resource tier (`no_pair`, all benchmarks pooled): macro difference and the share of non-tied sentences in which RL is preferred. Consensus-only and SFT-only languages have no learned metric (`sft_only`: the two zero-shot targets without SFT data).}}

*Is the reward model being gamed?* A policy that had learned to please AfriCOMET-QE would show a QE preference far above the reference-based ones. It does not: the QE metric prefers RL in 72.9% of the sentences against 70.0% for AfriCOMET with reference and 70.4% for the independent SSA-COMET with reference (a gap of 2.9 and 2.5 points). Of the 38,604 sentences on which RL wins on *both* QE metrics, the majority of the three reference-based metrics agrees in 89.7%, is neutral in 5.0% and prefers SFT in 5.3% ({{fref:figpw3_agreement}}B). Southern Sotho is the only language (all benchmarks pooled) that wins on both QE and both reference-based COMET metrics but is flat on chrF++; at the (benchmark, language) level 20 of 127 cells show a QE gain without a reference-based gain, and in 19 of them chrF++ is flat while AfriCOMET with reference is up; these are the high-resource Bantu, Sotho-Tswana, Malagasy, Afrikaans and Hausa languages, where chrF++ resolves less than a point. SALT Luganda is the one cell in which the QE gain is not supported by a reference-based metric (chrF++ -2.98; the COMET metrics are flat), one weak signal in 127 cells that a human check would settle. This is reassuring, not a proof of the absence of reward hacking.

*How well do the metrics agree?* Only moderately at sentence level ({{tref:t_pw_agree}}, {{fref:figpw3_agreement}}A): chrF++ and the learned metrics pick the same winner in 64-73% of the sentences where both call one (Cohen's kappa 0.17-0.28 over three classes), most with the reference-based COMET metrics (73% and 71%) and least with the QE metrics (64% and 66%); the two AfriCOMET variants agree in 87%, the two SSA-COMET variants in 95%, the two model families in 79-82%. Since the metrics are only weakly related sentence by sentence, the pooled preference of each is the meaningful quantity, and the fact that all five metrics, with different error sources (surface overlap, two learned models, with and without reference), give 62-73% is the central evidence.

{{table:t_pw_agree|Agreement between the sentence-level winners of the metrics (`no_pair`, sentences scored by all learned metrics; pairs of metrics; sentences on which both call a winner).}}

*Part of the gain is the removal of loops, which is a real quality difference.* SFT does not finish 11.4% of its sentences within the budget (macro 88.6% finished) and loops in 13.0% (RL: 99.7% finished, 1.0%); its mean output is 1.51 times as long as the reference (RL 1.15). Removing every sentence on which either model loops or does not finish (8.5% of the rows), the chrF++ difference halves (+3.32 to +1.67; AfriCOMET with reference +0.059 to +0.039; SSA-COMET +0.059 to +0.048), but the *preference* hardly moves: RL is still preferred in 60.0% of the non-tied sentences by chrF++, 67.7% by AfriCOMET with reference and 68.6% by SSA-COMET with reference ({{tref:t_pw_noloop}}). A translation that does not stop is worse than one that does, so the loops are part of what RL improved (Sections 6.1 and 8.2), but the improvement does not depend on them.

{{table:t_pw_noloop|Robustness of the comparison to looping outputs (`no_pair`, macro over languages for the difference, micro for the shares): all rows, rows on which neither model loops or fails to finish, and additionally both length ratios in [0.5, 2].}}

*Long inputs.* Using the sentence-by-sentence translations of the AfriLongDoc documents by both models (the setting in which long documents work, Section 6.4; 8 languages for the NLLB-referenced documents, 4,282 clean documents; the SFT model was run on that subset only), RL is preferred by chrF++ in 63-84% of the non-tied documents from 64 to 1,024 source tokens (79-80% at 2,048 and 4,096) and in 79-100% of the SmolDoc documents with human references (80 documents at about 1,024 tokens; 5 at about 2,048 tokens, a single language) ({{tref:t_pw_docs}}). The SFT model still fails to stop inside documents (finished 93% at b64 down to 13% at b4096, against 99% down to 82% for RL), which makes whole-document scores an unfair comparison for it, so the per-sentence rows inside the documents are the cleaner evidence: there RL is preferred in 60-65% of the non-tied sentences by chrF++, 67-71% by AfriCOMET with reference and 66-70% by SSA-COMET with reference, flat across lengths, with human and NLLB-pseudo references alike ({{tref:t_pw_segs}}, {{fref:figpw3_agreement}}C). The NLLB-referenced rows reward NLLB-likeness (Section 6.4); the human-reference rows agree with them.

{{table:t_pw_docs|Long input, whole documents (sentence-by-sentence decoding for both models; clean documents; NLLB-referenced bins b64 to b4096 and SmolDoc with human references, stitched documents of about 512, 1,024 and 2,048 tokens; the last row is a single language and five documents): chrF++ difference, share of documents preferred, finished and looping rates.}}

{{table:t_pw_segs|Long input, aligned sentences inside the documents (up to 500 sentences per bin and language for the learned metrics; 7 of the 8 languages for AfriCOMET): difference and share of non-tied sentences in which RL is preferred.}}

{{figure:figpw3_agreement|(A) Winner agreement between metrics (percent of the sentences on which both call a winner). (B) The 38,604 sentences on which RL wins on both QE metrics: what the majority of the three reference-based metrics says. (C) Per-sentence chrF++ win, tie and loss shares inside long documents. Source: `gemma_mt/scripts/pairwise_fig_agreement.py`.|100}}

*Sensitivity.* The preference is stable under the tie threshold: 62-72% for chrF++ with thresholds of 0 to 5 points and 68-80% for the learned metrics with thresholds of 0 to 0.05 (with no ties, RL wins 60.3%, 66.0% and 66.3% of all sentences by chrF++, AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET with reference), and for the decoding budget: on FLORES+ at 320 instead of 768 new tokens the chrF++ difference is +2.91 [+2.83, +2.98] against +3.19 [+3.11, +3.27] and RL is preferred in 67.4% against 67.6% of the non-tied sentences (the size of the difference depends on the budget, the preference does not).

**What this does and does not show.** (i) These are metric preferences: chrF++ and the learned metrics agree on a sentence's winner in only 64-73% of the cases and the learned metrics were validated by their authors on a subset of our languages, with one reference; a rater study could still differ. (ii) AfriCOMET with reference is not independent of the reward; chrF++ and SSA-COMET with reference are, and they point the same way. (iii) FLORES+ and NTREX-128 are public and the SFT text held the English side of 95% of the FLORES+ sentences and, for seven languages, the reference translation itself (Section 6.9a); the `no_pair` subset controls for the pair leak only, the `clean` subset (48 sentences per language) for both, and the preference is similar on both (63.7% and 61.6%), so the conclusion does not change, but the levels do. (iv) The benchmarks are news, encyclopaedic and children's-story text, while the training data are religious and pivot-translated; the smallest gains are where the domains are closest, and nothing here tests user text, science or dialogue. (v) Greedy decoding only; one reference per sentence; equal weight per language; the learned metrics were run on a sample (all `clean` rows and at most 400 rows per benchmark and language). (vi) Only the step from SFT-final to RL-final (phases 1 to 2c) is compared this way with the learned metrics; the science-stage checkpoints were not, and the final run's checkpoint was compared with RL-final by chrF++ on four benchmarks and with the learned reference-based metrics on NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT, SmolSent and FLORES+ (Section 6.7, Appendix H), not on SALT, GlotStoryBook, IbomMT or FLORES-MOZ.

**Leakage audit of the RL prompts.** The pairwise comparison and the `clean` subset control for leakage into the *SFT* text (the models saw the English side of 95% of the FLORES+ devtest sentences, 91% of SmolSent, 84% of SALT, 40% of NTREX-128, 22% of MAFAND-MT and 7% of GlotStoryBook, by the contamination study of Section 3.6). The RL prompt pools had not been audited, so we did that for this paper (CPU only, `gemma_mt/scripts/p3_rl_leakage_audit.py`, result `reports/data/rl_leakage_audit_2026-10-23.json`): the English side of every benchmark row (23,042 distinct sentences, 161,272 rows) against the English windows of the 77 sentence-level and document-level RL pools (5,940,000 prompt windows, drawn from FineWeb-Edu and Wikipedia text) and of the 77 science pools of the final run (137,172 windows), with the definitions and normalisation of the SFT contamination study (NFKC, case-folding, 8-token n-grams). Exact overlap is rare: 0.69% of the FLORES+ sentences (7 of 1,012; the same sentences back IbomMT and FLORES-MOZ), 0.45% of NTREX-128 (9 of 1,993), 0.46% of MAFAND-MT (81 of 17,737 distinct sentences), 0.23% of SmolSent (2 of 863), 0.2% of SALT (1 of 500) and 1.7% of GlotStoryBook (16 of 937) occur in some RL prompt of some language; at least one 8-gram is shared by 4.8% of the FLORES+ sentences and 1-2% of the others. In the pool of the *same language* as the test row, which is the prompt set that language was trained on, the exact overlap is at most 0.16% of the rows of any benchmark (MAFAND-MT: 31 of 20,008 rows; 0.04-0.10% on the `no_pair` rows: FLORES+ 0.04%, NTREX-128 0.07%, MAFAND-MT 0.09%, SmolSent 0.08%, GlotStoryBook 0.09%, SALT none). On the `clean` rows (English side not in the SFT text) it is 0 of 2,160 FLORES+ rows and 0 of 3,420 SmolSent rows, 48 of 28,824 NTREX-128 rows (0.17%), 12 of 15,633 MAFAND-MT rows (0.08%) and 49 of 3,179 GlotStoryBook rows (1.5%). The science pools of the final run add at most 0.2% on any benchmark. Hence the RL stage trained on the English side of the test sentences about two orders of magnitude less often than the SFT did. The reward scores a completion without a reference translation, but **its thresholds were fitted on human references**: shaping anchors, length and omission windows, diacritic windows, word-ratio and language-identification thresholds on FLORES+ dev and MAFAND dev; for Efik on the IbomMT test set (997 of its 1,012 sentences), which Section 6.1 reports. The final run's anchors were also fitted on the test splits of NTREX-128, SmolSent, SALT, GlotStoryBook, IbomMT and FLORES-MOZ, its long-document limits on SmolDoc and the AfriLongDoc core references, and the five languages with an NLLB signal were chosen by FLORES+ devtest chrF++ gaps. No translation text reached the policy through these, but results on those sets for the final run, and the Efik IbomMT result for RL-final, are not independent tests (Section 6.9a). An exact English overlap of an RL prompt does not hand the policy a translation, which makes prompt overlap a much weaker concern than the parallel leakage of SFT (the pair itself was in the SFT text for 4.1% of the FLORES+ rows, hence the `no_pair` subset). And exact and 8-gram matching misses paraphrases and translations of the same source, and the same English text may enter through the learned reward models' own training data (AfriMTE and SSA-MTE are news-domain; we did not audit them). We therefore treat RL-stage leakage as small but not excluded, and the SFT-stage leakage, shared by both models of the comparison, as the one that matters; it is the reason for the `no_pair` and `clean` subsets and for the finding that the preference is slightly lower, not higher, on `clean` rows by chrF++ (61.6% against 63.7%).

**The same comparison for the final checkpoint (added 2026-10-24).** The final checkpoint (final2, step 401) was compared with RL-final in the same way on NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT, SmolSent and FLORES+ with chrF++, AfriCOMET with reference and SSA-COMET with reference ({{tref:t_final2_pw}} in Section 6.7; per language in Appendix H). The result is the opposite of the SFT-to-RL result in size and, for the learned metrics, in direction: final2 is preferred in 52.4% [52.0, 52.8] of the non-tied sentences by chrF++ and in 47.5% [47.1, 47.8] and 47.6% [47.1, 48.2] by AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET with reference (RL over SFT: 63.7% and about 70%), and the mean learned-metric difference is -0.003, on scales on which RL gained +0.059 over SFT (AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET with reference, macro over 51 languages). We read this as "level"; the two kinds of metric disagree on the sign of a very small difference.

### 6.9a Exposure of the evaluation sets to our training, selection and reward fitting

*What this section is.* Sections 6.1-6.9 and 6.12 report results on public test sets, and what those numbers mean depends on how much of that text our pipeline had seen. A contamination audit (2026-10-26) traced it. It keeps three roles of an evaluation text apart: **training signal** (the text enters a loss through the SFT text, the RL prompts, the thresholds that the reward is fitted on, or a start model), **selection or monitoring signal** (it decides a checkpoint, a mixture, an interpolation weight, a set of languages or a stopping point) and **reported evaluation**. Every statement carries an evidence label: VERIFIED (read from a scan output, configuration, script or the Hub), REPORTED (stated in an earlier report, not re-checkable now) or UNKNOWN (no file settles it; the file that would is named in the audit). The audit used the flag files of the scan of the SFT text of 2026-10-18/19 (`clean`, `no_pair` and `pair` flags for 11,442,172 windows), a re-scan with the same scanner of a random sample of 87,498 SFT windows, the RL leakage audit of Section 6.9, and the configurations, calibration files and scripts of the runs; it stored counts and ids, no benchmark text (`reports/benchmark_contamination_audit_2026-10-26.md`, data in `reports/data/contamination_audit_2026-10-26/`, scripts `gemma_mt/scripts/contam_audit_*.py`). It changed several statements of earlier drafts of this paper (ledger Part V).

{{table:t_exposure_stages|Where the evaluation sets entered our pipeline, stage by stage (the contamination audit of 2026-10-26; tables generated by `paper/scripts/make_exposure.py`).
