Bold: the places where a test split was calibration material or was left out of a hold-out. VERIFIED / UNKNOWN as in the audit.}}

*Eight benchmarks, five English texts.* FLORES+ devtest, IbomMT and FLORES-MOZ are translations of the same 1,012 English sentences and have identical exposure (95.3% seen, 4.7% never seen), so the eight test sets rest on five independent English texts: FLORES+, NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT, SmolSent (with SmolDoc) and SALT (with GlotStoryBook). Agreement among the three FLORES-derived sets is not independent confirmation, and the tables below list them separately only because they have different languages and references.

{{table:t_exposure_bench|Exposure of each test set (all rows of all languages). "English in SFT" and "pair in SFT": rows whose English sentence, or whose English sentence and reference translation, occur in the SFT text (flag files of the scan of 2026-10-18/19); "`clean`": English never seen. RL prompts: exact overlap of the English sentence with a prompt of any language's pool (distinct sentences) and with the pool of the row's own language (rows; MAFAND-MT 31 of 20,008). Source: `reports/data/contamination_audit_2026-10-26/flag_summary_by_benchmark.csv`, `reports/data/rl_leakage_audit_2026-10-23.json`.}}

*The SFT text: yes, and in more than one way.* The flags say that the English side of 964 of the 1,012 FLORES+ devtest sentences (95.3%; 984 of 997, 98.7%, of dev) occurs in the SFT text, and that the FLORES+ translation itself occurs in the text of its own language for 933 of the 1,012 sentences in at least one of seven languages (Hausa 842 sentences, 83%; Igbo 559, 55%; Yoruba 355, 35%; Zulu 36, Tswana 30, Oromo 24, Luganda 2). The scan sees only exact copies after normalisation, so these are lower bounds. The exposure is not article-specific: the 48 never-seen sentences lie in 44 of the 281 articles, and the exposure is 96.8% for wikinews, 93.4% for wikibooks and 95.6% for wikivoyage sentences, so the text holds FLORES+ sentence by sentence, from all three domains. MAFAND-MT test pairs are in the text for Igbo (90%) and Yoruba (48%), SALT test pairs for Luganda (84%), SmolSent pairs for Wolof (73%), Bambara (65%) and Ewe (61%), and the SSA-MTE train split (English source with the human reference) is in it as well.

*Why 95% of the English side but only 4% of the pairs (as far as it is known).* The packed SFT windows and the table that records the source repository of every pair were not backed up, so the audit re-scanned a random sample of 87,498 original windows (780 to 2,611 per language, kept in a replay pool). Twenty-four of them carry exactly one FLORES+ sentence: 16 Luganda windows, whose Luganda side is an independent translation (chrF++ 21-60 against the FLORES+ Luganda reference, so no Luganda `pair`), 4 Yoruba, 2 Igbo, 1 Hausa and 1 Zulu windows, four of which carry the FLORES+ human translation of that language. The English side thus entered through the union of several language streams, and a row of language X counts as `pair` only when X's own FLORES+ translation is in an X window, whereas its English counts as seen when any language's window carries it; this is the mechanism behind 95.3% English and 4.1% pairs. Scaled by each language's share of windows in the sample, the 24 windows stand for about 8,300 windows of the full text, with very wide intervals (Luganda about 900 [510, 1,450]; the others 150 to 4,300 with intervals that span an order of magnitude); this is a sample-based estimate. *One carrier is named* (VERIFIED by streaming): `African-Languages-Lab/multi-open`, as repackaged in `<HF_ORG_LEGACY>/sft-new`, holds FLORES+ rows in Zulu (49 devtest and 47 dev English sentences, 96,150 rows scanned) and Igbo (23 devtest and 36 dev sentences with their Igbo translation, 179,604 rows scanned), which accounts for the Zulu pair flags and about 4% of the Igbo ones. Several repositories were checked and excluded (0 FLORES+ hits: `UBC-NLP/AfroLingu-MT`, the opus100 part of `sft-new`, the Inkuba Hausa files, six Luganda corpora). **The carriers of the Luganda, Hausa, Yoruba and most Igbo rows, and of the MAFAND-MT, NTREX-128 and SALT pairs, are UNKNOWN**: they must be among private repositories of the owner's older Hub accounts, which return 404, and the pair pattern (Hausa, Igbo, Yoruba) matches the language mix of aggregated Nigerian-language translation collections, but that is circumstantial. The file that would settle it (`pairs_final` with its `source_dataset` column, or the windows) is not on the laptop or in the Hub backups.

*RL prompts: no devtest text as a prompt or target.* 7 of the 1,012 FLORES+ devtest sentences (0.69%) occur as an exact sentence in some RL prompt of some language, 21 of 45,540 rows (0.05%) in the pool of the row's own language, and 0 of the 2,160 `clean` rows; the prompts carry no target text (Section 6.9). Paraphrases and the reward models' own training data (AfriMTE, SSA-MTE) were not audited, and, as reported in the decision log, the pools were not decontaminated against the benchmarks when they were built (REPORTED).

*The reward: thresholds fitted on human references, and five places where a test split entered.* The reward scores a completion without a reference translation, but it has thresholds and statistics fitted on human references (Section 4.3). Most of them come from FLORES+ dev (997 sentences per language) and MAFAND dev (Pidgin), which are disjoint from FLORES+ devtest (0 of 30,418 dev rows have their English in devtest). **The following are not dev-only, and we state them without softening:**

1. **RL-final, Efik: the IbomMT test set.** The shaping anchors, the length and omission windows, the diacritic window and the lexical block of Efik were fitted on 997 of the 1,012 IbomMT test sentences, because Efik has no other human reference set (`calibration/shaping_flores_dev_v7.json`, entry `efi`; AUC 0.731). No translation text reaches the policy, but the Efik reward geometry was fitted on the references of the Efik result in Section 6.1 (SFT 18.5 to RL 31.2 chrF++) and of the IbomMT macro (+11.04). The same Efik entry is in the final run. We have no re-run with Efik calibrated elsewhere (for example on pooled priors), so the size of the effect is unknown; Ibibio, for which nothing was fitted on IbomMT, gains +9.4 against +12.7 for Efik, but the two languages differ in data and reward group and this is not a test.
2. **The final run: anchors fitted on six test splits.** Its constraint anchors (copy, entity, function-word, padding, repetition, boilerplate) were fitted on human units that include, besides FLORES+ dev, SmolDoc and AfriScience dev documents, the test splits of NTREX-128, SmolSent, IbomMT, SALT, FLORES-MOZ and GlotStoryBook ("thresholds only, never training data"). The final run's results on NTREX-128 and SmolSent in Section 6.7 (+0.17 and +0.20 chrF++ over RL-final) are therefore not independent tests; RL-final's reward has no such anchors.
3. **The final run: long-document limits fitted on the evaluation documents.** The limits of the long-document reward (repetition quantile, tail gap) were fitted on 19,770 documents: the SmolDoc human references stitched over 45 languages and the NLLB-3.3B references of the AfriLongDoc core set, which are the evaluation documents of Section 6.10 (statistics only, no text in the policy).
4. **The final run: five NLLB-signal languages chosen with FLORES+ devtest.** Kabuverdianu, Kabiye, Nuer, Bambara and Kabyle received the NLLB pseudo-reference signal because the rule ("teacher minus student at least +2 chrF++ on devtest, interval above 0") was applied to FLORES+ devtest scores. The final run's devtest results in those languages are selected on the test set.
5. **The science stage: no devtest in the hold-out.** The replay of original SFT windows (65% of the first mixture and 43% of A2W by tokens) was filtered against AfriScience dev and test, AfriLongDoc, the clean general dev set and FLORES+ dev English, but not against FLORES+ devtest, NTREX-128, MAFAND-MT test, SmolSent, SALT or GlotStoryBook. A UniMax draw of 9-12.5 M tokens is 0.4-0.6% of the windows, so about 0.4-0.6% of the roughly 8,300 FLORES+-carrying windows, a handful (an estimate from the sample).

*Selection and monitoring: development sets only.* The 760-sentence dev set that monitored training is 40 rows per language of a 10,043-row file (1,190 FLORES+ dev and 8,853 MAFAND dev rows, no devtest; which of the two sources the 40 come from is UNKNOWN). The headline RL-final is the last step (1,433), not the dev-best (step 986: 41.977 against 41.883 for the last step, a flat curve between 41.567 and 41.977), so selection by dev played no role for it; the interpolation weight, the mixture and the stopping rule of the final run used AfriScience dev, the general dev set and the weak-language dev set (FLORES+ dev, 60 sentences per language). FLORES+ devtest informed four decisions that do not change RL-final: the NLLB-language rule above, the decoding study (Section 6.5), informal increases of the draw weights of weak languages for the final run (a bet, not a measured effect), and the choice of RL-final over the final run as the headline model. How "the 17 weakest languages x2.5" of phases 1-2c were chosen is UNKNOWN (the launch note is missing); if they were read off a devtest report, it is one more selection-type use.

*How large is the effect?* The exposure raises the level of our scores (all rows minus `clean` rows, macro chrF++ over languages; {{tref:t_exposure_size}}): FLORES+ +3.8 for SFT-final and +2.6 for RL-final (about 3.2 and 2.0 after the 0.6 chrF++ that the harder never-seen sentences cost the untrained base model), SmolSent +6.4 and +4.6, SALT +3.6 and +4.2, NTREX-128 +1.8 and +1.6, MAFAND-MT +1.4 and +0.5, IbomMT +1.8 and +2.1, GlotStoryBook 0.0 and -0.1. The `no_pair` headline removes almost none of it (28.7 and 34.1 against the `clean` 25.1 and 31.7: 3.6 and 2.4 above), because only 7 of the 45 languages have pairs in the text. For the languages whose pair was seen the `no_pair` rows are below the `all` rows, as expected (Hausa SFT 47.6 `all`, 43.0 `no_pair`, 43.9 `clean`; RL 49.7, 45.4, 44.9; Igbo 39.7, 37.0, 35.8 and 41.1, 38.5, 37.8; Yoruba 20.9, 19.5, 18.6 and 29.6, 27.2, 27.4). Both models share the SFT exposure, so differences between them are less affected than levels, but the RL-specific parts (Efik; for the final run the six anchor sets) are not shared. We did not recompute any result without the test-fitted reward components, so their effect on the scores is not measured.

{{table:t_exposure_size|Size of the exposure effect and what survives it. Macro chrF++ over languages on all rows and on `clean` rows (English never in the SFT text), SFT-final and RL-final, the inflation (all minus `clean`), and the paired-bootstrap interval of the RL gain on the `clean` rows. Source: `benchmark_subset_table.csv` of the audit, from `reports/data/final_model_per_language.csv` and `macro_boot.json`.}}

*What survives.* (a) **RL over SFT, on `clean` rows of all eight benchmarks:** the interval excludes zero on each (Table above): FLORES+ +6.62 [5.83, 7.24], NTREX-128 +4.09 [3.87, 4.32], MAFAND-MT +3.66 [3.03, 4.16], SmolSent +7.06 [6.17, 7.70], GlotStoryBook +8.26 [7.58, 8.87], SALT +2.31 [0.85, 3.57], IbomMT +10.70 [8.42, 12.77] and FLORES-MOZ +5.15 [1.09, 9.26]; the gain is larger on `clean` rows than on all rows for FLORES+, NTREX-128, SmolSent and GlotStoryBook, equal for MAFAND-MT and smaller (intervals overlapping) for SALT, IbomMT and FLORES-MOZ. Without the seven languages whose FLORES+ pair was seen (hau, ibo, yor, zul, tsn, orm, lug) the 38 remaining languages go from 27.45 to 33.21 (+5.76 on all and `no_pair` rows, +7.06 on `clean` rows: 23.75 to 30.81). The pairwise analysis of Section 6.9 on the pooled `clean` rows of all benchmarks (49,552 sentence pairs per metric) gives +3.86 [3.72, 4.00] and 61.6-69.0% preference. The caveat of Section 6.1 (part of the gain is repaired unfinished output) stands. (b) **RL-final stays behind NLLB-200 3.3B on identical sentences, by a smaller margin on `clean` rows** ({{tref:t_exposure_nllb}}): 3.84 on all rows, 3.85 on `no_pair`, 3.16 on `clean` rows (44 languages; the 600M model 1.65, 1.69 and 0.70); RL-final is above the 3.3B model in 8 of 44 languages on all and `no_pair` rows and 11 of 44 on `clean` rows. NLLB-200 itself is not known to be free of FLORES+ (not checked). (c) **The largest gains survive on the 48 clean sentences** ({{tref:t_exposure_highlights}}): Kabuverdianu +15.16 [12.22, 17.23], Luo +14.14 [10.34, 17.44], Kabiye +13.03 [12.10, 14.07], Nuer +12.68 [11.08, 14.09], Kimbundu +12.13, Kamba +12.10 [9.96, 14.09] and Kikuyu +11.89, as large as on all rows; none of these languages has a seen pair, and RL-final's reward had no NLLB signal for them, so they are not caused by the exposure or by the NLLB-language rule (which exists only in the final run). The caveat that part of the gain is repair of unfinished output applies to the all-row numbers (SFT finished 34% of Kabiye, 51% of Nuer, 70% of Kamba, 76% of Kabuverdianu) and the `clean` gains are as large. (d) **The final run is level with RL-final** on every subset (+0.1 to +0.25 chrF++: FLORES+ `clean` +0.12 [-0.02, +0.26], `no_pair` +0.23 [+0.19, +0.27]; NTREX-128 `clean` +0.17; MAFAND-MT `clean` +0.17; SmolSent `clean` +0.25), but not independently on NTREX-128 and SmolSent (item 2). (e) **The LLM judge keeps its direction on `clean` sentences** (Section 6.12): +3.4 [0.3, 6.2] on the 129 sampled never-seen sentences against +4.0 [3.2, 4.9] on all 1,570.

{{table:t_exposure_nllb|RL-final against NLLB-200 on the identical FLORES+ devtest sentences scored by us (44 languages with an NLLB row; macro chrF++), by subset. Source: `nllb_clean_table.csv` of the audit (from `reports/data/model_comparison_master.csv`).}}

{{table:t_exposure_highlights|The largest gains of RL-final over SFT-final, on all 1,012 sentences and on the 48 `clean` sentences (paired bootstrap, 95% interval; the pipeline of the generation-run table of Section 6.1, which differs from the identical-sentence pipeline of Section 6.8 by at most 0.53 chrF++ per language). None of these languages has a FLORES+ pair in the SFT text. Source: `highlight_languages.csv` of the audit (from `bench_boot.csv`).}}

*What does not survive.* (i) The cross-paper `clean` comparisons of Section 6.2 and of earlier drafts ("6.6 chrF++ behind on `clean` rows", "the comparison with the Afrique LLMs turns negative"): they set our 48 harder never-seen sentences against published scores on all 1,012 sentences; they are removed, and no claim about the Afrique LLMs is made in either direction. (ii) The statement that "published systems did not see these sentences": we did not check it. (iii) The independence of IbomMT Efik (RL-final and the final run) and of the final run on NTREX-128, SmolSent, SALT, GlotStoryBook, IbomMT, FLORES-MOZ, SmolDoc and AfriLongDoc core. (iv) The AfriMTE-WMT2024 and SSA-MTE material used to validate the LLM judge: the English of AfriMTE-WMT2024 is 100% FLORES+ devtest sentences (1,916 sentences in nine pairs) and the SSA-MTE train split is in our SFT text (19 sample windows carry its English; 2 windows carry English of its test split, by a route that is UNKNOWN). The validation scored *other systems'* outputs, so our models' exposure does not enter the validation correlations, but the validation sentences are not independent of our training and evaluation text (Section 6.12, caveat 8).

*What was not done.* No result was recomputed without the test-fitted reward components, the carriers of most FLORES+ rows are not named, and no evaluation on text that the pipeline has never seen is reported; a planned fresh evaluation is described in Section 11 (item 17) and is future work. The audit also proposes a protocol for the next model (decontamination against every protected set on both sides in any language, calibration and selection sets fixed in advance and disjoint from the evaluation sets, provenance per window); it was not applied to the models of this paper.


### 6.10 Long-context benchmark: does the model translate 2,048-token documents better than other systems?

Our working hypothesis during the project was that *our 270M model translates documents of up to about 2,048 tokens far better than other models, which do not support such inputs*. Sections 6.4 and 6.6 already weakened it for the RL model; this subsection tests it directly against other systems on one benchmark (agent LC1, `reports/long_context_benchmark_2026-10-23.md`). **The hypothesis is only partly supported, and the part that holds is narrower than the sentence above.** We state the test as a result, not as a claim of the model.

*Benchmark (LongCtx-MT, {{tref:t_longctx_benchmark}}).* Items are document times language. Real documents of the AfriLongDoc core (about 120 to 4,100 tokens, 12 languages, not in our training data) with the NLLB-200-3.3B sentence-by-sentence translation as a pseudo-reference; the human-reference SmolDoc documents (short ones and unrelated documents *stitched* to about 512, 1,024 and 2,048 tokens; 12 languages, 6 of which are common to all systems: hau yor swh amh zul ibo); and the AfriScience-MT dev and test papers (about 900 tokens, 6 languages, human references). Systems: our five checkpoints (SFT-final, RL-final, A2W, the step-250 checkpoint of the final run, and the final checkpoint of that run, step 401, added on 2026-10-24 with the same scripts, documents, languages and settings), NLLB-200 distilled-600M and 3.3B (inputs truncated at their 1,024 positions; their model card says they are not intended for document translation), TranslatePsy-AfriSLM 0.8B and 2B (262,144 context), and Qwen3.5-0.8B and Gemma-3-1B-it zero-shot. Our models generate the whole document natively, in one pass, and that is the mode they were trained for; sentence by sentence is a wrapper (split the source, translate each sentence, join) that we added to compare with systems that cannot take long input and as a repair route for unfinished sentences. Every system is run in two modes: the *whole document in one pass*, and *sentence by sentence* (for our models with the best-of-four repair of unfinished sentences of Section 6.4; for the others plain). Metrics are document-pooled chrF++ and spBLEU, finished rate, loop rate, source-sentence coverage (the alignment of the long-document reward), length ratio and GlotLID share. Reductions forced by the shared GPU: the NLLB whole-document runs cover six languages, TranslatePsy-0.8B seven, TranslatePsy-2B five languages with fewer bins; Gemma-3-4B-it, TranslatePsy-4B, AfriNLLB and Qwen3.5-2B were not run.

{{table:t_longctx_benchmark|The LongCtx-MT benchmark (agent LC1; median Gemma tokens of the English source). Stitched documents are unrelated texts joined: they test length robustness, not discourse. Source: `reports/data/longctx/bench_summary.json`.}}

*Whole-document input.* {{tref:t_longctx_human_chrf}} and {{fref:fig14_longctx_chrf}} show the result. Up to about 512 tokens our models match their own sentence wrapper, while NLLB and TranslatePsy already lose about 30 chrF++ at about 512 (stitched: 23.0-23.5). From about 1,024 tokens the picture splits. The *science-stage* models, A2W and the live run, still give a usable whole-document translation: chrF++ against human references 39.4 and 39.6 at about 1,024 tokens and 28.8 and 28.2 at about 2,048, and on the AfriScience papers (about 900 tokens) 43.8 / 41.5 (dev / test) for the live run, against 9.8-23.3 / 8.4-17.9 for the NLLB and TranslatePsy models (NLLB-600M 11.5 / 10.8, NLLB-3.3B 9.8 / 8.4, TranslatePsy-0.8B 18.3 / 17.9, TranslatePsy-2B 23.3 on dev only). The final checkpoint of the run (step 401), evaluated afterwards, is level with them: 39.6 [38.3, 40.7] and 27.7 [25.8, 29.7] at about 1,024 and 2,048 tokens and 43.2 [40.7, 45.3] / 41.3 [39.2, 43.3] on the AfriScience papers (dev / test); its intervals overlap those of the step-250 checkpoint and of A2W in every column, and the paired document bootstrap of the difference to the step-250 checkpoint includes zero in all eight columns of the benchmark (stitched about 1,024 tokens -0.03 [-1.01, +0.95], about 2,048 tokens -0.46 [-2.31, +1.40], AfriScience dev -0.61 [-1.80, +0.48] and test -0.20 [-1.15, +0.71]; against A2W +0.15 [-0.95, +1.19], -1.13 [-3.14, +1.00], -0.48 and -0.23), so 151 further RL steps neither raised nor measurably lowered the chrF++ of native long input (`reports/data/longctx/final2_paired_vs_other.csv`); it is +9.8 [+8.0, +11.5] chrF++ above RL-final at about 2,048 tokens and +14.0 [+12.0, +15.9] at about 1,024. The checkpoints of the paper's headline do not: RL-final reaches 25.6 / 17.9 (finished 51% and 39%, loops in 67% and 90% of the documents) and SFT-final 14.7 / 12.3 (finished 7% and 3%). The other systems in whole-document mode score 12.4 / 8.5 (NLLB-600M), 9.7 / 5.9 (NLLB-3.3B), 11.7 / 8.1 (TranslatePsy-0.8B) and 14.9 / 7.5 (TranslatePsy-2B, five languages). NLLB truncates its input at 1,024 positions (100% of the 2,000-token documents, 90% of the 1,000-token ones, 36-57% of the papers) and returns a short, repetitive output (median 260-360 tokens whatever the input length; source-sentence coverage 0.33-0.41); TranslatePsy accepts the input on paper but loops or never stops (finished 0.77 / 0.61 for the 0.8B model at about 1,024 / 2,048 tokens, 0.21 on the papers). Zero-shot Qwen3.5-0.8B and Gemma-3-1B-it do not write most of these languages (chrF++ 10-14 and 21-23; Qwen writes Hausa in Arabic script and scores 1.7 on Amharic, Gemma 0.7), so context length is not their limit.

{{table:t_longctx_human_chrf|chrF++ against human references (macro over the six common SmolDoc languages and the five common AfriScience languages; 95% bootstrap intervals over documents). Whole document: one pass; sentence by sentence: for our models with the best-of-four repair of unfinished sentences. The live row is the step-250 checkpoint of the final run and the final2 row its last step (401); A2W, the live run and final2 were trained on the AfriScience-MT training split, and the run's checkpoint-selection rule uses the AfriScience dev set. Source: `reports/data/longctx/macro_common.csv`, `paper/scripts/make_longctx.py`; the final2 rows were added on 2026-10-24 by `lc_agg.py --only final2,live` (the step-250 rows reproduce exactly).}}

{{figure:fig14_longctx_chrf|chrF++ against source length for whole-document input (top) and sentence by sentence (bottom): (A) AfriLongDoc core documents against the NLLB-3.3B pseudo-reference (biased towards NLLB-like output, ordering only), (B) SmolDoc against human references, (C) AfriScience papers against human references. Source: `paper/scripts/make_longctx.py`.|100}}

*What the native result is, and is not.* The step-250 checkpoint of the science-and-general run (the live run) finishes 94-100% of the documents up to about 2,048 tokens (stitched 1.00 / 0.95 at about 1,024 / 2,048 tokens, core documents 0.99 / 0.94, AfriScience papers 0.95-0.96) and writes the target language (GlotLID share 0.94-0.99); its starting model A2W finishes 90-98% at about 1,024 tokens but only 72-80% at about 2,048 tokens (0.80 stitched, 0.72 core). The final checkpoint (step 401) lies in between: it finishes 98% at about 1,024 and 91-92% at about 2,048 tokens (0.92 stitched, 0.91 core; AfriScience papers 0.94-0.95), writes the target language (GlotLID share 0.95-0.99) and omits as much as the step-250 checkpoint (coverage 0.88 at about 1,024 and 0.73 at about 2,048 tokens, length ratio 0.81 and 0.59, i.e. about 12% and 27% of the source sentences left untranslated), but its outputs loop more often (loop rate 0.08 and 0.25 stitched, against 0.04 and 0.10 for the step-250 checkpoint, paired difference +0.04 [+0.01, +0.08] and +0.15 [+0.10, +0.20]; core documents at about 2,048 tokens 0.25 against 0.12, +0.13 [+0.04, +0.21]; AfriScience dev 0.10 against 0.04, +0.06 [+0.01, +0.10], test 0.05 against 0.07, not different; A2W 0.15 and 0.30): at about 2,048 tokens a quarter of its whole-document outputs contain a repetition loop that is absent from the reference, although the chrF++ is unchanged. Against A2W it finishes more documents at that length (stitched +0.12 [0.00, +0.24], core +0.19 [+0.12, +0.30], AfriScience test +0.09 [+0.03, +0.14]). We do not know whether the higher loop rate is a systematic effect of the 151 further steps or a property of one more checkpoint; there is one run. Both **omit text**: source-sentence coverage is 0.87 at about 1,024 and 0.70 at about 2,048 tokens and the length ratio to the reference 0.78 and 0.56 for the live run (A2W: coverage 0.87 / 0.72, length ratio 0.80 / 0.75), i.e. about 13% and 30% of the source sentences are left untranslated by the live run (12-28% for A2W); for a Hausa document of about 2,000 tokens the step-250 checkpoint writes 4,490 of the 9,188 reference characters ({{tref:t_longctx_failure_human}}, {{fref:fig15_longctx_failure}}; this document was not inspected for the final checkpoint). RL-final covers 0.95 / 0.94 of the sentences but loops, SFT-final neither stops nor covers. On a 2,000-token document, then, no system translates the whole text in one pass with the quality it reaches sentence by sentence.

{{table:t_longctx_failure_human|Failure anatomy for whole-document input and the sentence wrapper, stitched ~1,024 and ~2,048-token SmolDoc documents, macro over languages: finished rate / loop rate / source-sentence coverage / length ratio to the reference / GlotLID share.}}

{{figure:fig15_longctx_failure|Finished rate, loop rate and source-sentence coverage against source length on the AfriLongDoc core documents, for whole-document input (top) and sentence by sentence (bottom). Source: `paper/scripts/make_longctx.py`.|100}}

*A sentence-by-sentence wrapper beats the native mode for every system, and the other systems then lead.* With sentence-by-sentence decoding every system finishes, covers every sentence and loops rarely (NLLB's own sentence mode loops in 9-33% of the documents, from repeated sentences and list items). For the live model the wrapper adds +13 chrF++ at about 1,024 tokens and +27 at about 2,048 (paired; +12 and +14 on the AfriScience papers); for the final checkpoint +13.1 [+11.9, +14.4] and +27.8 [+26.4, +29.1] (+12.4 and +14.1 on the papers), and its wrapper output scores the same as the live model's (52.6 / 55.5 chrF++ at about 1,024 / 2,048 tokens; AfriScience 55.6 / 55.4). The other systems gain far more and end *above* ours: at about 1,024 / 2,048 tokens NLLB-600M, NLLB-3.3B, TranslatePsy-0.8B and 2B reach 53.9-56.0 / 56.6-58.7 chrF++ against 52.6 / 55.5 for our live model (1-4 chrF++ ahead at every length; on the AfriScience papers 57.6-58.8 against 55.4-55.6). With the wrapper the order is TranslatePsy ~ NLLB-3.3B > NLLB-600M > our science-stage and RL models > SFT-final. "Far better than other models" is therefore true **for native long input only**; as a statement about document-translation quality it is false: a user of NLLB or TranslatePsy gets a better translation of a 2,000-token document by splitting it into sentences than the user of our model gets from the whole-document mode. On sentence-level scores of the AfriScience papers the same ordering holds (dev / test chrF++: live 45.7 / 45.4, NLLB-3.3B 48.3 / 48.0, TranslatePsy-0.8B 49.4 / 49.0).

| claim | measured | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| other systems do not support such inputs | NLLB truncates; TranslatePsy accepts the input but loops; Qwen and Gemma do not write the languages | true in practice, not by context length |
| our model translates documents up to about 2,048 tokens | only the science-stage models finish more than 70% of the documents at that length (step-250 checkpoint 94-95%, final checkpoint 91-92%, A2W 72-80%); coverage 0.70-0.88 | partly: stable, but omits 12-30% of the sentences, and an independent judge scores the text it writes at 25.8 of 100 against 67.0 for the wrapper (Section 6.12) |
| far better than other systems | whole-document 39.6 / 27.7-28.2 against at most 14.9 / 8.5; but 52.6 / 55.5 against 53.9-56.0 / 56.6-58.7 for their wrappers | true for native long input only |

*Pseudo-references need care.* The NLLB-3.3B sentence-by-sentence translations that serve as the reference for the core documents (Section 6.4) are themselves a system. Against human references NLLB-3.3B is at least 2 chrF++ above our best model only in Amharic, Kinyarwanda, Malagasy and Zulu (+9.8, +8.7, +6.9, +2.7), marginally ahead (0-2) in Hausa, Igbo, Somali, Swahili, Twi and Xhosa, and *below* ours in Luganda (-2.6), Northern Sotho (-5.2), Wolof (-3.2) and Yoruba (-0.8) ({{tref:t_longctx_pseudoref_validation}}, {{fref:fig16_longctx_pseudoref}}). Agreement with NLLB exceeds quality against humans by +2 to +9 chrF++ for our models (stitched about 1,024 tokens: SFT-final +6.6, RL-final +8.6, A2W +7.8, live +8.0; AfriScience dev +2.2 to +4.5) and by +13 to +19 for the NLLB and TranslatePsy lineage (NLLB-600M +15.6, TranslatePsy-0.8B +17.5, 2B +19.1; {{tref:t_longctx_agreement_nllb}}), which are trained on or distilled from NLLB-like data. NLLB-referenced scores therefore favour NLLB-lineage systems and are used for ordering only; the human-reference rows carry the conclusions.

{{table:t_longctx_pseudoref_validation|Is the NLLB-3.3B pseudo-reference trustworthy? chrF++ of NLLB-3.3B (sentence by sentence, beam 4) against the human references per language, next to the systems it is used to judge, and the verdict per language.}}

{{table:t_longctx_agreement_nllb|Agreement with NLLB-3.3B against quality against humans (same documents, sentence-by-sentence mode): the gap is chrF++ against the NLLB-3.3B outputs minus chrF++ against the human references.}}

{{figure:fig16_longctx_pseudoref|(A) chrF++ of NLLB-3.3B against the human references per language (stitched ~1,024-token SmolDoc documents). (B) Agreement with NLLB-3.3B outputs against quality against human references for RL-final, NLLB-600M and TranslatePsy-0.8B, per language; points above the diagonal are systems that resemble NLLB more than a human translator. Source: `paper/scripts/make_longctx.py`.|100}}

*Caveats.* (i) A2W and the final run (whose step-250 checkpoint is evaluated here) were trained on the AfriScience-MT *training* split, which advantages them on the AfriScience sets, and the run's checkpoint-selection rule uses the AfriScience dev set (the step-250 checkpoint evaluated here is simply the one that was current when the evaluation was started), so its dev column is not independent (its test column was not used for selection but is treated as dev-like); the SmolDoc and core documents are outside all training data (exact and 8-gram check). (i-b) The final checkpoint (step 401) was added to this benchmark on 2026-10-24, after the other systems, by running the same scripts on the same documents with the same decoding (vLLM, greedy, 3.5 times the source tokens; GPU memory fraction 0.25 instead of 0.07, which changes the batching but not the greedy decoding in principle) and the same scoring and bootstrap; the existing rows were re-aggregated by the same code and reproduce exactly. Its sentence-level chrF++ on the AfriScience papers (6 languages, sentence-by-sentence mode) is 48.06 / 47.26 (dev / test) against 48.05 / 47.26 for the step-250 checkpoint here and 48.12 / 47.30 from the separate evaluation script of Section 6.7, which also checks the harness. The step-250 and step-401 checkpoints differ by 151 RL steps on a plateau, there is one run per checkpoint and the document bootstrap contains no seed-to-seed variance, so the benchmark has little power to separate them. (ii) Stitched documents are unrelated texts joined, so they measure length robustness, not discourse; no human judge of discourse quality was used, and the independent LLM judge of Section 6.12 scores aligned units of one to a few sentences, not discourse. (iii) Document-pooled chrF++ forgives clause shifts of the sentence alignment and lies about 10 points above the sentence-level scores of the same output; it is not comparable with published sentence-level numbers. (iv) The numbers differ slightly from those of Section 6.4 because the document selection (20 documents per bin, 6 common languages) and the decoding budget (3.5 times the source tokens, greedy) differ, with the same conclusions; likewise the step-250 checkpoint finishes 96% of the AfriScience dev papers here against the 1.000 of Section 6.7 (the budget differs). (v) One checkpoint per system and one decoding setting; models above 8 GB were not run; whether the pretraining of NLLB, TranslatePsy, Qwen or Gemma contains SmolDoc or AfriScience is unknown (TranslatePsy lists Smol among its evaluation sets). (vi) Coverage and loop metrics use our own alignment and loop detector, which was designed for the reward and may disadvantage other systems' outputs slightly; the NLLB sentence-mode loop rate is inflated by repeated list items.

**Wording we use.** Whole-document decoding beyond about 512 tokens fails for NLLB (input truncated at 1,024 tokens), for TranslatePsy-AfriSLM (repetition loops) and for our SFT and RL checkpoints. After the science stage the step-250 and step-401 checkpoints of our 270M model finish 91-100% of the documents of 1,000-2,000 tokens and write the target language (chrF++ 39.6 and 28.2 / 27.7 against human references at about 1,024 and 2,048 tokens, A2W 39.4 and 28.8; on the AfriScience-MT test papers 41.5 / 41.3), where the NLLB and TranslatePsy systems we tested score 5.9-14.9 (AfriScience test 8.4-17.9), but they omit 12-30% of the sentences. A sentence-by-sentence wrapper removes the failure for every system and gives higher scores (52.6-58.7 chrF++, ours 52.6 and 55.5, the same for the step-250 and final checkpoints) than native long input, with NLLB and TranslatePsy 1-4 chrF++ ahead of ours. Better on native long input is therefore not better document translation, and we do not claim that our model translates long documents better than other models. An independent LLM judge applied to aligned units of the same core documents (Section 6.12) agrees and is sharper about quality: the whole-document units of the final checkpoint score 25.8 of 100 (16.4% of the sampled sentences without output), against 67.0 for its sentence-by-sentence wrapper and 85.6 and 86.5 for NLLB-200 3.3B and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B (five languages; whole minus wrapper -42.0 [-45.1, -38.8]), falling from 33.8 at about 1,024 tokens to 12.1 at about 2,048; the judge is validated only at Spearman about 0.5 and the whole-document units are blocks of 1.4 sentences, but these gaps are far larger than that noise. The chrF++ numbers and the judge tell consistent stories of different size: chrF++ against the pseudo-reference is much less harsh (the wrapper 1-4 chrF++ behind NLLB and TranslatePsy) than the judge (19.9 points behind NLLB, six languages), and the model finishes long documents and writes the right language, but what it writes is judged far below the wrapper and far below NLLB-200 3.3B; this is not specific to the Ge'ez-script languages (Section 6.12).

*An optional second pseudo-reference.* Because the NLLB pseudo-reference is biased and absent for 22 of the 23 consensus-only languages, LC1 prepared, but did not run, a Gemini Batch-API pseudo-reference; the plan and its costs are in Appendix I. No API call was made for that pseudo-reference (the LLM-judge calls of Section 6.12 are a separate use of the API).


### 6.11 Running the model on a laptop CPU

The model is small enough to run without a GPU, and the question a user asks is what it costs in quality and time. We measured this for the final checkpoint (`final2-step401`) converted to llama.cpp GGUF and to CTranslate2 (agent C1b, `reports/cpu_speed_2026-10-23.md`, data in `reports/data/cpu_speed_2026-10-23/`). **The measurements were made on a shared server, not on a laptop**: an Intel Xeon Gold 6126 (2.6 GHz, Skylake, AVX-512) pinned to exactly four cores under `nice`, three repetitions, while the ablation and evaluations of the project ran on the same machine (one to three busy neighbour cores). The headline numbers are *solo* runs; runs with several benchmarks in parallel were 7-17% slower ({{tref:t_cpu_speed}}; `solo_vs_parallel_streams.csv`) and rows measured that way are labelled "shared box". Quality is chrF++ against human references on 1,170 FLORES+ devtest sentences of six languages (Swahili, Hausa, Yoruba, Kinyarwanda, Amharic, Tigrinya; rows whose reference pair was not in the SFT text; 200 sentences each, 170 for Hausa), generated greedily; Tigrinya stands in for Nigerian Pidgin, which FLORES+ does not contain. The prompts were checked to match the Hugging Face chat template token for token (`prompt_mismatch_vs_hf_template` = 0 in `lean_rss.csv`).

*Quantisation costs almost nothing down to 8 bits.* {{tref:t_cpu_quant}} lists macro chrF++ and its difference to the bf16 GPU output (paired bootstrap over sentences). GGUF Q8\_0 is indistinguishable from the GPU (-0.02 chrF++, [-0.22, +0.16]) although only 18% of its outputs are identical strings, because greedy decoding diverges at near-ties without changing the score; CTranslate2 fp32 is level too (+0.03). The 6-bit GGUF costs 0.20 [-0.42, -0.01], the hybrid CTranslate2 model that keeps attention in fp32 and the rest in int8 0.25 [-0.48, -0.06], Q5\_K\_M 0.68 [-0.92, -0.48], Q4\_K\_M 1.40 [-1.83, -1.07] and CTranslate2 int8 1.71 [-1.99, -1.38], where Yoruba alone loses 4.8 chrF++ (29.4 to 24.6) and Amharic 1.5. The intervals contain sentence-sampling noise only. The recommended variant is therefore **GGUF Q8\_0**: a 300 MB file (size from the report), 0.42 GB of RAM at batch 1 and 1.2 GB at batch 32.

{{table:t_cpu_quant|Quantisation against quality on 1,170 FLORES+ devtest sentences (swh, hau, yor, kin, amh, tir), final checkpoint, greedy decoding, 95% paired bootstrap intervals over sentences. Source: `reports/data/cpu_speed_2026-10-23/quality.csv`, `paper/scripts/make_cpu.py`.}}

*Speed.* {{tref:t_cpu_speed}} and {{fref:fig18_cpu_tradeoff}}: with four threads Q8\_0 translates 0.97, 1.85 and 2.00 sentences per second at batch 1, 8 and 32 (139 hours per million FLORES-length sentences at batch 32; first token after 80 ms at batch 1; 135 decoded tokens per second at batch 32). CTranslate2 int8 is faster at large batch (2.87 sentences per second, 97 hours per million) and slower at batch 1 (0.77), but it is the variant that loses 1.7 chrF++, so we do not recommend it. Q4\_K\_M was not faster than Q8\_0 (1.72 against 1.83 sentences per second at batch 32 in the same shared-box condition), the fp32 and hybrid CTranslate2 models are slower (1.6-1.7 sentences per second at batch 32 on a shared box) and need 2.5-3.6 GB of RAM without Python overhead (`lean_rss.csv`). A build restricted to AVX2 instructions runs at the speed of the native build (1.85 against 1.83 sentences per second, batch 32, both with other jobs running), so the numbers do not depend on AVX-512.

{{table:t_cpu_speed|Speed on four cores of the shared Xeon (3 repetitions, mean and range). "Solo": no other benchmark ran; "shared box": other jobs were running, speeds are 7-17% too low. The hours per million sentences assume FLORES-length sentences of the six-language mixture. RAM includes the Python/PyTorch process for CTranslate2. Source: `headline_speed_4threads.csv`, `paper/scripts/make_cpu.py`.}}

{{figure:fig18_cpu_tradeoff|Quality against speed at batch 32 on four cores (macro chrF++ on the same 1,170 sentences). NLLB-200 is shown as a reference only (Section 6.11). Source: `headline_speed_4threads.csv`, `paper/scripts/make_cpu.py`.|70}}

*Threads and processes.* Q8\_0 scales with the number of threads (batch 8: 0.63, 1.14 and 1.85 sentences per second at 1, 2 and 4 threads; {{tref:t_cpu_threads}}); CTranslate2 int8 scales poorly (1.46, 1.71, 1.95), so four single-thread processes with eight sentences each reach 3.5 sentences per second in aggregate at the price of loading the model four times (7.5 GB of RAM summed over the processes, which include PyTorch). For Q8\_0 four processes give 2.1 against 1.85 for one four-thread process at 2.4 GB in total.

{{table:t_cpu_threads|Thread scaling (solo runs) and four independent single-thread processes (aggregate throughput of 120 sentences divided by the time of the slowest process; three repetitions). Source: `speed.csv` (rows `z_*`), `data_parallel_4x1thread.csv`.}}

*Paragraphs.* On 20 stitched SmolDoc paragraphs of 475-516 prompt tokens ({{tref:t_cpu_paragraph}}; one small sample, one repetition per variant except where stated) Q8\_0 reaches chrF++ 47.4 against 47.6 for fp32 and 46.1 for int8, at 0.119 paragraphs per second at batch 8 (8.4 s per paragraph, about 430 per hour). 4 or 5 of the 20 outputs (20-25%) reach the 1,024-token cap even in fp32, which is the looping of the model itself and not an effect of quantisation. **Decoding a whole paragraph is not faster per token than decoding sentences:** the decode rate is 109 tokens per second for paragraphs at batch 8 against 125 for sentences at batch 8 and 135 at batch 32, and the paragraph outputs average about 840 tokens. The benefit of the model's long-input ability is therefore coherence across sentences (Section 6.10), not speed.

{{table:t_cpu_paragraph|Paragraph translation on the CPU: 20 stitched SmolDoc documents of 475-516 prompt tokens, batch 8 (NLLB-200: beam 4, reference only). "Hit the cap": unfinished at the 1,024-token limit. Source: `quality_paragraph.csv`.}}

*Reference: NLLB-200 on the same cores (not usable commercially).* To place the numbers we ran NLLB-200-distilled-600M and NLLB-200 3.3B through CTranslate2 int8 on the same four cores and sentences ({{tref:t_cpu_nllb}}). NLLB-200 is licensed CC-BY-NC and **this is a reference, not a claim of superiority**: the decoders and engines differ (ours greedy in GGUF Q8\_0, NLLB int8 with beam 4 or greedy) and no interval was computed for the cross-system differences. NLLB-200 3.3B with beam 4 scores 41.0 macro chrF++ against 35.0 for ours on the same 1,170 sentences, at 0.48 sentences per second (574 hours per million) and 10.9 GB of RAM, which does not fit an 8 GB laptop. NLLB-200-distilled-600M scores 38.7 with beam 4 (1.49 sentences per second, 3.2 GB) and 37.4 greedy (4.17 sentences per second, 67 hours per million). Per language our Q8\_0 output is ahead of the 600M model with beam 4 in Swahili (+1.1), Hausa (+0.6) and Yoruba (+3.5) and behind in Kinyarwanda (-0.6); the mean of these four languages is 44.2 against 43.1 (and 45.9 for the 3.3B model). The macro gap is almost entirely in the two Ethiopic-script languages, where ours is far behind (Amharic 19.6 against 37.4 and Tigrinya 13.2 against 22.6 for the 600M model with beam 4). Ours is faster than NLLB-600M with beam 4 (2.00 against 1.49 sentences per second) and slower than NLLB-600M greedy (4.17, a factor of 2.1; CTranslate2 int8 of ours: 2.87, a factor of 1.45), which is faster and scores 2.4 chrF++ higher than our Q8\_0 on these sentences. On 490-token paragraphs NLLB writes only about 240 tokens and scores 23.0 and 24.4 against 47 for ours: it drops content (Section 6.10).

{{table:t_cpu_nllb|Reference comparison with NLLB-200 on the same four cores and 1,170 sentences (batch 32, CTranslate2 int8 for NLLB). Not a like-for-like decoding comparison; no intervals. Source: `quality.csv`, `headline_speed_4threads.csv`.}}

*Why is a 270M model not faster than NLLB-600M?* The files give a measured part and leave a hypothesis. **Measured:** per sentence our model generates about 59-60 tokens against about 44 for NLLB (`gen_tok_mean` in `quality.csv`: 60.4 for Q8\_0, 59.1 for int8, 43.7 for NLLB-600M greedy), that is 1.35 times more output tokens, because the Gemma tokenizer fragments these languages more (Section 3.3). The generated tokens per second (end to end, batch 32) are about equal between our CTranslate2 int8 model (172) and NLLB-600M greedy (175), and lower for Q8\_0 (118). So the 1.45 times lower sentence rate of our int8 model is explained by the token count (1.35 times) and not by a slower step, but a model with less than half the parameters is also not faster per generated token. **Hypothesis, not measured:** 168M of our 270M parameters are the vocabulary table, which is also the output head, so every decoding step computes scores for 262,145 entries (about 168M multiply-adds against roughly 100M for the 18 layers), and CPU decoding at small batch is bound by memory traffic over all weights, which is consistent with the rise from 0.97 to 2.00 sentences per second between batch 1 and 32; the per-token cost of the 270M model would then be dominated by the head and not much lower than that of a larger model with a smaller head. We did not profile the time share of the output projection, and we did not count the output projection of NLLB-600M, which may be large too; the restricted-vocabulary idea of Section 3.3 (TODO(vocab)) would test it. The two token-rate figures also come from different sentence subsets and token definitions, so the equality is approximate.

*Estimate for a laptop (ESTIMATE, not a measurement).* We have no laptop measurement. The report assumes 0.5-1.0 times the measured Q8\_0 speed on an 8 GB, four-core laptop, because the Xeon core is probably faster than an older laptop core, laptop memory bandwidth (DDR4) lowers decode speed, and Apple performance cores may be faster; the factor is a judgement and not a result. With it, Q8\_0 would translate 1-2 sentences per second, that is 8-17 minutes per 1,000 sentences and 6-12 days per million: fine for thousands to tens of thousands of sentences, not for millions. For comparison the GPU (vLLM) was about 100 times faster in the same project.

*Caveats.* Shared machine and some one-repetition or shared-box rows (hybrid, fp32, Q4\_K\_M, the PyTorch rows); small speed subsets (120 sentences per timing, 20 paragraphs; Hausa has 170 clean sentences instead of 200); six languages, one of them a stand-in; one checkpoint (the final checkpoint, not the headline RL-final model; the speed does not depend on the weights, the quality numbers do); FLORES-length sentences only; no energy or battery measurement; no human evaluation. No TranslatePsy data or outputs were used.


### 6.12 An independent LLM judge: validation, FLORES+ and long documents

*Why, and what this section is.* Every learned metric used above is either part of the reward stack or shares data and encoders with it (Section 6.9), and chrF++ rewards surface overlap. A large language model prompted as a judge is a scorer that neither we nor the metric authors trained. The validation pilot of Section 7.1a showed that such a judge (gemini-3.8-flash) tracks human adequacy ratings of *other* systems about as well as SSA-COMET with reference (Spearman about 0.5); with the owner's approval we then validated the reference-free prompt and applied the judge to our models in three runs (2026-10-25): **(1)** validation of the reference-free prompt, **(2)** FLORES+ translations of SFT-final and RL-final, **(3)** long documents, judged on aligned units. The judge is a model and not a human, it is validated only at Spearman about 0.5, and **none of this is human evaluation**; we read only language-level or pooled differences, and, in Run 3, differences that are far larger than that noise.

*Protocol.* The prompts are Figure 8 (reference-based) and Figure 9 (reference-free, "QE") of the SSA-COMET paper [@li2025ssacomet], verbatim (Appendix J; the only change is that "[67]Most" is written "[67] Most"), with five demonstrations, one per adequacy interval (SSA-MTE train split for Amharic, Hausa, Kinyarwanda and Yoruba; other rows of the same AfriMTE-WMT2024 pair, scores min-max scaled, for Swahili, Igbo, Somali and Xhosa; the demonstrations are those of the pilot), score only, gemini-3.8-flash as served on 2026-10-25, temperature 0, thinking at the model default, Batch API, **one run per request** (nothing resubmitted). Figure 9 is Figure 8 with the `Reference:` field removed from the demonstrations and the test line (a unit test asserts this on all 300 pilot segments). The output cap was 1,000 tokens including thinking (1,500 for Run 1); the largest thinking budget used was 912 tokens, so the cap was never reached, and **none of the 6,190 responses failed to parse** (no non-STOP finish). The three runs cost USD 5.42 (the pilot of Section 7.1a USD 1.82 more; {{tref:t_judge_j2_cost}}), under a guard that refused any submit whose worst case would take the running total above USD 8. All judge scores below are on a 0-100 scale.

*Why Gemini-3.8-Flash, and not the judge of the SSA-COMET paper.* Li et al. [@li2025ssacomet] judge with Gemini-2.5-Pro. For our API key that model returned "no longer available to new users" (Batch API and standard API), so we did not run it, and the judge used here is not a reproduction of theirs; we do not claim that Flash is better than 2.5 Pro. In the pilot of Section 7.1a we compared Gemini-3.1-Pro-preview (Google's named successor of 2.5 Pro) with Gemini-3.8-Flash on the same 300 SSA-MTE and AfriMTE segments (one run per judge): Flash reaches a pooled Spearman correlation with the human z-score of 0.511 [0.41, 0.60] against 0.454 [0.35, 0.55] for 3.1 Pro; the paired difference Pro minus Flash is -0.057 [-0.09, -0.02], and its per-language intervals include zero in every language except Hausa and Somali; Flash costs about one fifth as much (USD 0.29 against 1.53 for the 300 segments). We therefore used Flash: on this sample it was not worse than the stronger model we could run, at a fifth of the price.

*Run 1: is the reference-free prompt usable?* On the 300 pilot segments the QE prompt reaches a pooled Spearman correlation with the human z-score of **0.494 [0.394, 0.586]**, against 0.511 [0.41, 0.60] for the reference-based prompt of the pilot, 0.536 [0.44, 0.62] for SSA-COMET-MTL with reference and 0.528 [0.43, 0.61] for SSA-COMET-MTL-QE (without reference); per-pair means removed 0.552 [0.46, 0.65], 0.584, 0.556 and 0.552. The paired differences are not distinguishable from zero (QE minus reference prompt, pooled: -0.017 [-0.048, +0.013]; per-pair means removed -0.033 [-0.056, +0.002]; QE judge minus SSA-COMET-MTL-QE, pooled: -0.034 [-0.117, +0.053]; no per-pair Spearman difference to the reference prompt excludes zero), and the scores of the two prompts correlate at 0.954 (Spearman; mean score 80.6 with, and 78.7 without, the reference). By the rule "usable if the confidence interval of the correlation excludes values below 0.2" the reference-free judge is usable for Amharic, Hausa, Kinyarwanda, Yoruba, Swahili and Igbo (**Kinyarwanda and Igbo only just**, lower bounds 0.203 and 0.214) and **not usable for Somali (lower bound -0.03) and Xhosa (-0.21)**, which were not used with the QE prompt ({{tref:t_judge_j2_validation}}). The six long-document languages pooled give 0.517 [0.414, 0.612]. The published Gemini-2.5-Pro figure for the QE prompt (average Spearman 0.610) is over the paper's own pairs, which include easier ones, and is not comparable with ours.

{{table:t_judge_j2_validation|Run 1: validation of the Gemini-3.8-Flash judge with the reference-free prompt (Figure 9) against human adequacy ratings (z-scores), 300 segments of other systems' output (SSA-MTE: 50 per pair; AfriMTE-WMT2024: 25 per pair). Spearman correlations; 95% bootstrap intervals over segments (4,000 resamples, the same resamples for all scorers); the reference prompt column is the pilot's. Source: `reports/data/judge_j2_2026-10-25/run1_correlations.csv`, `run1_language_usability.csv`, `run1_paired_differences.csv`.}}

*Run 2: SFT-final against RL-final on FLORES+.* Data: the stored greedy outputs of the paper's evaluation (768 new tokens) against the human FLORES+ reference (reference-based prompt, because a reference exists). **Sampling choice:** per language, 200 sentences drawn with a fixed seed from the `no_pair` rows (the sentence pair was not in the SFT text; this is the subset of the paper's headline and the one on which the stored AfriCOMET and SSA-COMET scores exist), identical for both systems; Hausa has only 170 such sentences, so n = 170 there. Sampling all rows would have put the memorised reference pairs of Hausa (83% of its rows) into the sample. The eight languages are those with pilot demonstrations (amh, hau, kin, yor, swh, ibo, som, xho; 1,570 sentences, 3,140 requests). {{tref:t_judge_j2_run2}} gives the result: **the judge scores RL-final 62.2 against 58.2 for SFT-final, +4.04 [+3.21, +4.86]** (macro over the eight languages; paired bootstrap over sentences), higher in seven languages (Amharic +5.0, Hausa +4.4, Kinyarwanda +7.5, Yoruba +7.2, Swahili +2.0, Igbo +3.7, Somali +2.5; every interval excludes zero) and level in Xhosa (+0.06 [-1.92, +2.11]), the language where the pilot validation was weakest. Pooled, RL-final wins 856 of the 1,421 sentences on which the two scores differ (60.2%). On the same sentences chrF++ gives +2.13 [+1.75, +2.51], AfriCOMET with reference +5.32 [+4.83, +5.83] and SSA-COMET with reference +4.40 [+3.94, +4.87] (x100). Across the eight languages the judge's differences are ordered like those of AfriCOMET (Spearman 0.93), SSA-COMET (0.98) and chrF++ (0.79); **single sentences are not read**: at sentence level the paired differences agree only at 0.39 (AfriCOMET), 0.55 (SSA-COMET) and 0.30 (chrF++), with the same sign in 65%, 71% and 60% of the sentences where both are non-zero. On the 129 sampled sentences whose English side was never seen in SFT (`clean`; 5-48 per language, too few to read per language) the pooled judge difference is +3.36 [+0.28, +6.16] (chrF++ +1.88), against +4.04 [+3.21, +4.86] on all sampled sentences; per language it is positive in six of the eight (Hausa +3.33 with n = 48; Kinyarwanda +11.45, n = 11; Amharic +6.0, n = 5) and negative in Xhosa (-4.43, n = 14) and Somali (-2.50, n = 10), with no per-language interval. This is the same direction as Sections 6.1 and 6.9, measured by a scorer that is not part of the reward stack; because the judge saw the human reference and both systems saw the English side of about 95% of the sentences, it does not remove the contamination caveat of Section 6.1.

{{table:t_judge_j2_run2|Run 2: FLORES+ devtest (`no_pair` rows; up to 200 sentences per language, Hausa 170), SFT-final (step 27,638) against RL-final (GRPO step 1,433), reference-based judge (Figure 8, 0-100), and the same sentences' differences in chrF++ (sentence level) and in AfriCOMET-STL-1.1 and SSA-COMET-MTL with reference (x100). Win / tie / loss: sentences where RL-final scores higher / equal / lower. Intervals: paired bootstrap over sentences (4,000 resamples). Source: `reports/data/judge_j2_2026-10-25/run2_judge_by_language.csv`, `run2_same_sentences_metric_differences.csv`, `run2_agreement_with_other_metrics.csv`, `run2_clean_subset.csv`.}}

*Run 3: long documents, judged on aligned units.* The question of Section 6.10, asked of an independent judge. **Design (as corrected by the owner):** the models translate the whole document in one generation and the judge never sees the document; each system's output is aligned to the source sentences and the *aligned units* are judged with the QE prompt (no human reference exists for the core documents). Data: the LongCtx-MT core documents (Section 6.10) of the 1,024- and 2,048-token bins, 20 + 20 documents in each of six languages (hau, yor, swh, amh, ibo, kin). Systems: the final checkpoint (step 401) in whole-document mode and with its sentence-by-sentence wrapper (with repair), RL-final in whole-document mode, NLLB-200 3.3B sentence by sentence and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B sentence by sentence (no output for Kinyarwanda, so the Kinyarwanda sample of that system is absent; its five-language macro is compared with the five-language macros of the others). **Alignment:** `plan_document` of the long-document reward v2 (language-aware Gale-Church with omission and extra beads), with the length model of the coverage metric of Section 6.10; a unit is one aligned bead (consecutive source sentences with their output text), judged once however many sampled sentences it covers; a sentence with no output, or whose output copies the source, is an *omission* and has no unit. As a replication check, the omitted and copied counts and the number of output sentences equal the stored LC1 scores for all 480 whole-document documents. **Sample:** per language 50 source sentences from the 1,024-token and 50 from the 2,048-token documents, at most three per document, the same positions for every system (seed written to disk before any request; 600 sampled sentences, 2,750 units). **Unit size:** the whole-document units of the final checkpoint cover 1.4 source sentences on average (RL-final 1.25; at most 4); wrapper and baseline units are single sentences.

{{table:t_judge_j2_run3_means|Run 3 (a): mean judge score (0-100, QE prompt) of the judged aligned units (a block counted once), per system and language; macro over the five languages common to all systems (no Kinyarwanda) and over all six, with document-cluster 95% bootstrap intervals. Source: `reports/data/judge_j2_2026-10-25/run3_table_a_unit_means.csv`.}}

{{table:t_judge_j2_run3_omission|Run 3 (b): share of the sampled source sentences with no judged unit (omitted or copied; LC1 rule), per language, and the omission-penalised mean: the mean over all 100 sampled sentences per language, an omitted sentence counting 0 (a sentence in a judged unit takes that unit's score); macro of the five common languages. Source: `run3_table_b_omission.csv`.}}

{{figure:fig19_judge_run3|Run 3: judge scores of aligned units of long documents. (a) Judged-unit mean per language with document-cluster 95% intervals; black ticks: omission-penalised mean; the last group is the macro of the five languages common to all systems. (b) Sentence-weighted mean of the covered sampled sentences by document length (six languages). Whole-document units are blocks of about 1.4 sentences, wrapper and baseline units single sentences. Source: `run3_table_a_unit_means.csv`, `run3_table_b_omission.csv`, `run3_sentence_level_scores.csv`; `paper/scripts/make_judge.py`.|100}}

*Result.* On the five common languages ({{tref:t_judge_j2_run3_means}}, {{tref:t_judge_j2_run3_omission}}, {{fref:fig19_judge_run3}}) **the final checkpoint's native whole-document output has a judged-unit mean of 25.8 [22.4, 29.2], 16.4% of the sampled sentences get no output (omission-penalised mean 21.3 [18.3, 24.4])**, against 67.0 [65.3, 68.6] for the same model's sentence-by-sentence wrapper, 85.6 [84.3, 86.9] for NLLB-200 3.3B and 86.5 [85.3, 87.6] for TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B; RL-final in whole-document mode scores 12.9 [10.0, 16.0] (7.2% omitted). On identical sampled sentences ({{tref:t_judge_j2_run3_paired}}) the whole-document output is **42.0 points below the wrapper** (-42.0 [-45.1, -38.8], macro of six languages, both covered; every language has a negative interval, from -30.6 in Amharic to -56.1 in Yoruba), 10.6 points above RL-final's whole-document output (+10.6 [+7.6, +13.9]; higher in Hausa, Yoruba, Igbo and Kinyarwanda, level in Amharic +0.6 [-2.9, +3.8], Swahili +10.8 [-0.2, +22.5]), and 61.6 below NLLB-200 3.3B (-61.6 [-64.7, -58.6]). The wrapper is itself 19.9 points below NLLB-200 3.3B (-19.9 [-21.5, -18.4]), dominated by Amharic (-50.3), with Hausa (-5.7) and Swahili (-6.6) closest, in the same direction as the chrF++ comparison of Section 6.10 (NLLB and TranslatePsy ahead of the wrapper), but by a larger margin than the 1-4 chrF++ there; the two scales are not comparable. The whole-document score falls with the length of the document ({{tref:t_judge_j2_run3_diag}}): 33.8 at about 1,024 tokens and 12.1 at about 2,048 (RL-final 19.1 and 3.9), with 11% and 23% of the sampled sentences omitted for the final checkpoint, whereas the wrapper and the baselines show no length effect (wrapper 66.3 and 64.5, NLLB 85.7 and 84.8).

{{table:t_judge_j2_run3_paired|Run 3 (c), (d): paired differences of the judge score on identical sampled sentences (A minus B; document-cluster 95% bootstrap intervals; macro over the languages of both systems; right-hand columns: per-language differences on the sentences covered by both). "Omission-penalised": all sampled sentences, an omitted sentence counting 0. Source: `run3_table_c_d_paired.csv`.}}

{{table:t_judge_j2_run3_diag|Run 3 (e) and diagnostics: sampled sentences in documents flagged as looping or unfinished by the LC1 detectors (the repeated output was judged as it is); judged-unit mean in flagged and unflagged documents; mean by document length; share of units for which a block shifted by up to three sentences matches the NLLB pseudo-reference clearly better than the unit's own block ("possibly misaligned"); Spearman correlation of the judge score with the own-block chrF-like score. Source: `run3_table_e_loops.csv`, `run3_sentence_level_scores.csv`, `run3_alignment_check.csv`.}}

*How much of it is looping, and how much is the alignment?* The loop and unfinished flags of LC1 explain only part of the gap: 17.5% of the final checkpoint's sampled sentences lie in flagged documents (67.8% for RL-final), where the judged-unit mean is 8.9 (RL-final 4.2) against 27.3 (28.6) in unflagged documents; even outside the flagged documents the whole-document units score 27-29, still far below the wrapper (65.8 for its unflagged documents). Whole-document units that cover two or more sentences score lower (final checkpoint 4.6, against 29.9 for single-sentence units; RL-final 4.8 against 12.5), but block size is confounded with the failure mode (an output that drifts produces long blocks), so this does not by itself say that block-level judging lowers the score. The alignment diagnostic (an own character n-gram score, diagnostic only) finds that only 4.5% of the final checkpoint's units (2.2% of RL-final's, 0.2% of TranslatePsy's, none of the wrapper's and NLLB's) match a shifted block clearly better than their own block (their judge mean is 1.7, so removing them would move the mean by about 1 point), and the judge score correlates with the own-block score at 0.74 (final checkpoint, whole document; RL-final 0.57); within the window of three sentences the low scores are not an alignment artefact, but the check cannot see larger drifts or an unlucky bead boundary.

*What this adds to Section 6.10, and what it does not.* The evidence of Section 6.10 (chrF++ against human references and a pseudo-reference, finished rate, loop rate, coverage) said that native whole-document translation of the science-stage model finishes documents, writes the target language and omits 12-30% of the sentences, while a sentence wrapper gives every system more. The independent judge says the same, and more sharply about *quality*: the text that the final checkpoint does write in whole-document mode is judged far below its own sentence-by-sentence translation and far below NLLB-200 and TranslatePsy, and it gets worse from 1,024 to 2,048 tokens. We therefore keep the existing statements (finishes, writes the language, beats the whole-document baselines on chrF++) and add that its judged quality is low; whole-document capability is "finishes and writes the language", not "translates well". **The two kinds of number tell the same story at different sizes.** chrF++ against pseudo-references (Section 6.10) shows a model that finishes long documents and writes the right language, with the wrapper 1-4 chrF++ behind NLLB; the judge, once the output is aligned to the source sentences, says that what the model writes in whole-document mode is far worse than its own wrapper (25.8 against 67.0) and than NLLB-200 3.3B (85.6), and puts the wrapper itself 19.9 points behind NLLB (six languages). chrF++ against pseudo-references is much less harsh than the judge, and the two scales are not comparable (caveat 4); we report both and rely on neither alone.

*Is it the Ge'ez-script languages?* Amharic accounts for much of the wrapper's gap to NLLB (-50.3 points, against -5.7 to -25.8 in the other five languages), so {{tref:t_noGeez_judge}} gives the figures without it. The macro over the four non-Ge'ez languages common to all systems (Hausa, Yoruba, Swahili, Igbo; the same document-cluster bootstrap, seed and resampling weights as above, computed by `paper/scripts/make_noGeez.py`, which reproduces the stored five-language rows exactly) is **74.3 [72.4, 76.3] for the wrapper, 85.1 [83.6, 86.6] for NLLB-200 3.3B, 86.9 [85.6, 88.1] for TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B, 30.4 [26.1, 34.6] for the final checkpoint's whole-document output and 14.5 [10.9, 18.1] for RL-final's**. The wrapper-minus-NLLB difference on identical sentences is -10.9 [-12.7, -9.1] on these four languages (-18.8 [-20.5, -17.1] on the five with Amharic) and the wrapper-minus-TranslatePsy difference -12.6 [-14.3, -11.0] (-19.6 on five). Ge'ez script therefore explains part of the sentence-level gap, but not all of it: the wrapper is also far behind NLLB in Kinyarwanda (-25.8) and Yoruba (-19.5). **The whole-document failure is not a Ge'ez effect.** The final checkpoint's whole-document mean is 7.7 in Amharic, but also 9.0 in Yoruba, 14.6 in Kinyarwanda and 25.9 in Igbo, against 43.0 in Hausa and 43.6 in Swahili; whole document minus wrapper is -44.3 [-48.7, -40.0] on the four non-Ge'ez languages (-42.0 on all six) and is larger in Yoruba (-56.1) than in Amharic (-30.6); whole document minus NLLB-200 3.3B is -54.8 [-59.0, -50.6] on the four. We did not test why the model is further behind in the Ge'ez-script languages (script, tokenisation cost, little data, model capacity are all candidates) and make no claim about the cause; a specialist model and a larger model are future work (Section 11).

{{table:t_noGeez_judge|Run 3 without the Ge'ez-script language: judged-unit mean (0-100, QE prompt) per language and the macro over the four non-Ge'ez languages common to all systems (hau yor swh ibo) with document-cluster 95% intervals (4,000 resamples, seed and weights of the Run 3 analysis; Kinyarwanda is shown but is not in the macro because TranslatePsy has no Kinyarwanda output). Source: `run3_units_scored.csv`, `run3_sentence_level_scores.csv`, per-language values as in `run3_table_a_unit_means.csv`; `paper/scripts/make_noGeez.py`.}}

*Caveats.* (1) **Validated at about 0.5.** The judge correlates with human ratings of other systems' output at Spearman about 0.5; a language-level or pooled difference can be read, a single sentence cannot (Run 2: 0.30-0.55 agreement with the learned metrics). The Run 3 gaps (20-80 points) are far above that noise; the Run 2 gaps of 2-7 points are not, and the Hausa, Swahili, Igbo and Somali values rest on 25-50 validation segments per pair. (2) **Unit size and prompt.** The QE prompt was validated on single sentences (Run 1); Run 3 judges blocks of 1-4 sentences (mean 1.4) for the whole-document systems and single sentences for the wrapper and the baselines, so the whole-document minus wrapper contrast mixes block-level and sentence-level judging; we did not judge the wrapper outputs at block level. For Kinyarwanda and Igbo the lower validation bound is 0.20-0.21; Somali and Xhosa are not used. (3) **Alignment.** The aligner is the production Gale-Church aligner with omission beads; its pair F1 on corrupted outputs is 0.78 (macro; 0.34 for a text cut at half its length) and lower for loops and truncation (figures from the reward-v2 evaluation as quoted in the judge report, not recomputed here; Section 4.4 gives 0.89 for the anchored aligner on twelve other corruptions); the aligned unit of a drifting output is uncertain by construction. Omissions follow the LC1 definition (omitted or copied). (4) **A reference-free LLM judge is harsher than chrF++ against pseudo-references** (NLLB's own outputs are the pseudo-references of Section 6.10 and favour NLLB-like systems), and we did not test whether the judge itself prefers NLLB-like text. (5) **Sample sizes and sampling.** Run 3 has 100 sampled sentences per language and system with document-cluster intervals; Run 2 uses `no_pair` rows only and 170 sentences for Hausa. The NLLB-3.3B outputs of the core documents are the same sentence translations that serve as the LC1 pseudo-reference and as the length model of the aligner for every system. (6) One run of a hosted model that may change; no human reference for Run 3; whole-document modes of NLLB and TranslatePsy and the Gemini pseudo-reference of Appendix I were not judged. (7) **Overlap of the validation material with our training and evaluation text (Section 6.9a).** The validation used the SSA-MTE train split, which is in our SFT text, and AfriMTE-WMT2024, whose English sources are 100% FLORES+ devtest sentences (1,916 sentences, nine pairs). The judge scored other systems' outputs there, so our models' exposure does not enter the validation correlations, but the validation sentences are not independent of our training and evaluation text, and all 19 few-shot demonstrations of the Swahili, Igbo, Somali and Xhosa prompts are FLORES+ devtest sentences (with other systems' translations and human scores) from the benchmark that Run 2 samples (an index-based check found 5 of the 800 sampled sentences of these four languages among the demonstrations of their own prompts, about what chance gives; we did not verify the sentence-id mapping further). The demonstrations are identical for SFT-final and RL-final, so they cannot favour one of the two. (8) **Disclosure.** Run 3 judges the existing LC1 outputs of NLLB-200 3.3B and TranslatePsy-AfriSLM-0.8B; they were sent to the Gemini API **for evaluation only**, as were the benchmark sentences (SSA-MTE, AfriMTE, FLORES+, AfriLongDoc); no output of these systems and no TranslatePsy data was used for training or in any experiment, and judge outputs are used for evaluation only. All prompts and responses are stored with the results (`reports/data/judge_j2_2026-10-25/`).

## 7 Analysis of the reward design

Each component of the reward was motivated by a measurement, on corrupted or human text or on a failure of the previous version. This section reports those measurements, including the ones that went against what we expected, and (Section 7.9) what the extra terms of the final run did and did not do on stored outputs. {{fref:fig5_reward_design}} summarises six of the measurements.

{{figure:fig5_reward_design|Evidence behind the reward design. (a) Spearman correlation of reference-free QE metrics with human adequacy scores (SSA-MTE: 7 English-to-X pairs, 8,345 segments, in-domain for SSA-COMET; AfriMTE-WMT24: 9 pairs, 1,916 segments). (b) Share of corrupted versions that score at least as high as the human reference; lower is better; "mean-or-min" is the rule of Section 4.3. (c) Detection of corrupted human references by the constraint detectors; false-positive rates on held-out human text in the axis label. (d) The v1 repetition guard for long documents flags good output and misses loops; v2 does the opposite. (e) The padding feature of anchors v2 flagged most long human documents (a length bug). (f) AUC of human against mismatched references per metric.|100}}

### 7.1 Which quality-estimation metric should be the reward?

We started with AfriCOMET-QE-STL-1.1, the only QE model validated for most of our languages. Against *human* adequacy scores it was the weakest of the four options we tested ({{fref:fig5_reward_design}}a): Spearman 0.470 on SSA-MTE against 0.550 for SSA-COMET-QE and 0.604 for SSA-COMET-MTL run without reference (we reproduce the published 0.547 / 0.595 with 0.550 / 0.604), and 0.413 against 0.421 and 0.454 on the independent AfriMTE-WMT24 challenge set, where SSA-COMET-QE's advantage vanishes (+0.007 [-0.010, +0.023]) and that of SSA-COMET-MTL stays (+0.040 [+0.011, +0.068]). The human segments come from other systems (Gemini, GPT-4o, NLLB, Google), not from a 270M model, so this is evidence about the metric, not about our policy. A reward model is used inside GRPO to rank samples *of the same source*, and for that every QE metric turned out to be a weak ranker: the within-prompt Spearman correlation with sentence chrF++ over 8 samples of the final model is 0.145 for AfriCOMET-QE, 0.164 for SSA-COMET-MTL and 0.172 for the ensemble, and the sample each metric ranks first is only 0.9-1.0 chrF++ above the group mean, out of 5.1 for an oracle that picks the best of eight. The consensus signal is a much stronger ranker against chrF++ (0.370), partly by construction, which is why we keep it as a component.

### 7.1a An LLM judge as a second, independent check: the validation pilot (2026-10-25)

*Why.* Every learned metric used in this paper is either part of the reward stack or shares training data and encoders with it (Section 6.9), and chrF++ rewards surface overlap. A large language model prompted as a judge is a different kind of scorer, trained by neither us nor the metric authors. Before using it on the translations of our models we measured how well it tracks *human ratings* of other systems' translations. This subsection reports that validation pilot (reference-based prompt); the validation of the reference-free prompt and the application of the judge to our models are in Section 6.12. **The scores of the pilot are not an evaluation of our model, and no judge score is a human evaluation.**

*Judges and prompt.* Two Gemini models as served by the Gemini API on 2026-10-25: gemini-3.1-pro-preview ("3.1 Pro") and gemini-3.8-flash ("3.8 Flash"). We wanted to reproduce the judge of the SSA-COMET paper, Gemini 2.5 Pro [@li2025ssacomet], but the API returned 404 ("no longer available to new users") for gemini-2.5-pro with our key, so a reproduction was not possible; 3.1 Pro is the successor model, and 3.8 Flash was added as the cheap candidate. The prompt is the *reference-based* prompt of Figure 8 of Li et al. [@li2025ssacomet], used verbatim: an adequacy scale from 0 to 100 with four described levels (0, 34, 67, 100), the instruction to output only the score, five demonstrations (source, translation, reference, score; one demonstration per adequacy interval) and then the segment to score. Settings: temperature 0, the model's default thinking, the Batch API, **one run per judge** (no repeat). Output caps of 2,300 (Pro) and 2,200 (Flash) tokens for the 288 main requests and 8,192 for a 12-request probe. No output failed to parse (0 of 300 per judge), so the three parsing variants we tried (the paper's rule that scores an unparsable answer 0.5, dropping failures, and a lenient parser) give the same pooled value (`sensitivity_parse.csv`).

*Sample and target.* 300 segments of other systems' output with human adequacy scores: SSA-MTE (English to Amharic, Yoruba, Kinyarwanda, Hausa; 50 segments each) and AfriMTE-WMT2024 (English to Swahili, Igbo, Somali, Xhosa; 25 each), drawn with seed 2026 and stratified into five strata of the human score within each pair (`sample_ids.csv`). The human target is the z-score of the direct assessment. The judges are compared with SSA-COMET-MTL with reference and the two AfriCOMET with-reference models, scored on the same segments.

*Deviations from the original protocol, all of them ours.* (1) Gemini 3.1 Pro instead of 2.5 Pro, plus a second, cheaper judge. (2) The demonstrations of the four SSA-MTE pairs come from the pair's train split (one per adequacy interval); for the four AfriMTE pairs we did not use a train split but drew them from other rows of the same pair whose sources are not in the sample and mapped the z-scores to 0-100 by min-max scaling per pair. This is our substitute, not the published protocol. (3) A 300-segment stratified sample instead of the complete test sets. (4) One run per judge, so run-to-run variation of the judge is not measured. (5) Demonstration data exist for 11 languages (amh hau kik kin luo twi yor swh ibo som xho); the pilot sampled 8 of them, and the SSA-MTE pairs for kik, luo and twi were **not sampled**.

*Result.* {{tref:t_judge_pilot}} gives Spearman correlations with the human score (Pearson values, in the pilot's `correlations.csv`, give the same order of the three main scorers: Flash 0.610, Pro 0.566, SSA-COMET-MTL 0.628 pooled). Pooled over the 300 segments, 3.8 Flash correlates at 0.511 [0.41, 0.60], 3.1 Pro at 0.454 [0.35, 0.55], SSA-COMET-MTL with reference at 0.536 [0.44, 0.62] and AfriCOMET with reference at 0.447-0.449; after removing the per-pair means (a correlation within languages rather than across them) the values are 0.584, 0.538, 0.556 and 0.497-0.498. The paired difference ({{tref:t_judge_pilot_pairs}}) of Pro minus Flash is -0.057 [-0.09, -0.02], and -0.046 [-0.08, -0.02] after demeaning, so on this sample the larger model is *not* the better correlated one; Pro minus SSA-COMET-MTL is -0.082 [-0.17, +0.00] (-0.018 [-0.11, +0.05] demeaned). The two judges agree closely with each other (Spearman 0.94, Pearson 0.96 between their scores over the 300 segments). Per pair the picture is heterogeneous: Swahili is easy for every scorer (judges 0.82-0.86, learned metrics 0.83-0.87), Amharic, Yoruba, Kinyarwanda, Hausa and Igbo lie at 0.49-0.64 for the judges, and **Somali (judges 0.32 and 0.51, SSA-COMET 0.70) and Xhosa (0.19 and 0.19, SSA-COMET 0.33, AfriCOMET-STL 0.34) are poor for the judges**; on the four AfriMTE pairs pooled, SSA-COMET-MTL (0.635) is clearly better than Pro (0.438; difference -0.197 [-0.36, -0.04]) and higher than Flash (0.462; difference not tested), while on the four SSA-MTE pairs pooled Flash (0.537) and the metric (0.530) are level. Per pair, 3.8 Flash is higher than 3.1 Pro in 7 of 8 pairs (the Igbo and Xhosa differences are below 0.002) and the interval of the difference excludes zero only for Hausa (-0.096 [-0.20, -0.01]) and Somali (-0.197 [-0.44, -0.03]). On the four SSA-MTE pairs for which the SSA-COMET paper reports Gemini-2.5-Pro values (amh 0.605, hau 0.471, kin 0.528, yor 0.524; as recorded in the pilot's `paper_reproduction_check.csv`, not re-verified by us against the publication), 3.1 Pro gives 0.540, 0.516, 0.486 and 0.618 on our 50-segment samples; that is a comparison of similar magnitude, not a reproduction, because the sample, the model and the exact scoring differ.

{{table:t_judge_pilot|Validation pilot of the LLM judge: Spearman correlation with the human adequacy score (z-score) over 300 segments of other systems' translations (SSA-MTE and AfriMTE-WMT2024; one run per judge; reference-based five-shot prompt of Li et al.). Intervals are 95% bootstrap intervals over segments; the learned metrics are shown as point values. "Language-demeaned": per-pair means removed before pooling. Source: `reports/data/judge_pilot_2026-10-25/correlations.csv`.}}

{{table:t_judge_pilot_pairs|Paired differences of Spearman correlations on the same segments (bootstrap interval over segments and bootstrap standard error). Negative: the first scorer is the less correlated with the human score. Source: `reports/data/judge_pilot_2026-10-25/paired_differences.csv`.}}

*What the 300 segments can and cannot resolve.* The bootstrap standard error of a *paired* difference of two scorers is about 0.02 for the pooled sample (0.018 for Pro minus Flash, 0.044 for Pro minus SSA-COMET-MTL) and 0.04-0.15 (Pro minus Flash) and 0.08-0.22 (Pro minus SSA-COMET-MTL) per pair, with 25 or 50 segments each; the confidence intervals of single correlations per pair are 0.3-0.8 wide.
